If you run long

This lesson has a lot of form. If the Error Hunt test shows the class already controls the basic verb lists, cut "Clarify: the patterns" to 6 minutes and go straight to the meaning-change verbs, which are the heart of the lesson. Never cut the Pattern Path or the two-minute talk: that is where the patterns start to become automatic. Workbook Ex 5 can move to homework.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the "feel" behind the lists. Many -ing verbs describe your relationship to an activity as a whole thing (enjoy it, avoid it, finish it, miss it). Many to verbs point forward to something that hasn't happened yet (want, hope, plan, decide, promise). It is a helpful feel, not a perfect rule.

"I'm considering taking a course." Have I decided? (No, I'm thinking about it.) Is it considering to take? (No, consider + -ing.) · "We managed to finish." Was it easy? (No, but we did it.) Did we finish? (Yes.)

Meaning: prepositions and the to trap.

"I'm looking forward to the weekend." Can a noun come after to here? (Yes.) So is this to an infinitive or a preposition? (A preposition.) So: looking forward to… (seeing you, not see.) · "I want to the weekend"? (No! So want to is an infinitive: want to go.)

Meaning: purpose.

"I went out to buy milk." Why did I go out? (To buy milk.) Can I say "for buying milk"? (No, not for the reason of an action.) · "This knife is for cutting bread." Is that OK? (Yes, it's what the thing is for, its function.)

Meaning: the meaning-change verbs.

"I remember locking the door." Did I lock it? (Yes.) Is this a memory or a job to do? (A memory.) · "Remember to lock the door." Have you locked it yet? (No.) Memory or job? (A job, a duty.)
"He stopped smoking." Does he smoke now? (No, he quit.) · "He stopped to smoke." Does he smoke? (Yes!) Why did he stop? (To have a cigarette. He paused his walk or his work.)
"Try adding salt." Is it hard to add salt? (No.) Why add it? (To see if the soup tastes better: an experiment.) · "I tried to open the jar." Was it easy? (No.) Did I open it? (Maybe not: an attempt.)
"I regret to tell you the class is canceled." Is the speaker sorry about the past or about news now? (News now: formal.) · "I regret telling him." Did I tell him? (Yes, and I'm sorry.) · "This job means traveling a lot." = intend or involve? (Involve.) · "I meant to call you." = intend or involve? (Intend: I wanted to, but I didn't.)

Form.

Verb + -ingenjoy, avoid, finish, mind, suggest, imagine, consider, keep, practice, miss, can't stand, don't mindWe're considering moving. · Keep trying.
Verb + towant, decide, hope, promise, agree, offer, refuse, manage, learn, seem, afford, planWe can't afford to wait. · She offered to drive.
Preposition + -inggood at, interested in, tired of, instead of, before, after, without, look forward to, be committed to, be used toHe left without saying goodbye. · I look forward to hearing from you.
-ing as subjectactivity = the subjectGetting up early is hard in winter.
Adjective + tohappy, difficult, easy, ready, surprised + toIt's difficult to explain.
Purposeto + base verb (= in order to)I'm calling to confirm my appointment.
Verb + object + towant, ask, tell, advise, allow, encourage + person + toShe encouraged him to apply.
Bare infinitivemodal + base verb · let / make + person + base verbLet me go. · They made him wait.
Either, little changelike, love, hate, prefer, begin, start, continueI like swimming ≈ I like to swim.

Pronunciation. to before a verb is weak /tə/: "I want /tə/ go" (and in fast American speech, want to → wanna; recognize it, don't write it). But to as a preposition before -ing is also weak: "looking forward /tə/ seeing you." The -ing ending is /ɪŋ/ with no hard /g/ at the end: "cooking" /ˈkʊkɪŋ/, not "cooking-guh." Stress in the meaning-change pairs carries the meaning: "I stopped TO buy coffee" (contrastive, when you correct someone) · "He STOPPED smoking." can't stand: Americans say /kænt/ with a clear vowel and a short, soft t. Listen for the vowel, not the t.

Drill script
  1. Picture + verb card: hold up the swim picture and the enjoy card → S: "I enjoy swimming." cook + want → "I want to cook." dance + can't stand → "I can't stand dancing." hike + decide → "We decided to hike." play the guitar + good at → "She's good at playing the guitar." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Purpose chain: T: "Why do you go to the library?" → S: "To borrow books." T: "Why did you call the clinic?" → S: "To make an appointment." (Short answers with to are natural English: praise them.)
  3. Memory or duty? (point ⬅️ or ➡️ on the board) T: "remember / lock" + ⬅️ → S: "I remember locking it." T: + ➡️ → S: "Remember to lock it." T: "stop / buy coffee" + ⬅️ → "I stopped buying coffee." + ➡️ → "I stopped to buy coffee."
  4. Pronunciation: "I'm looking forward /tə/ seeing you." ×3 (weak to, clear -ing); "I can't afford /tə/ wait." ×2.
  5. Open pairs: S1 asks "What have you stopped doing this year?" S2 answers with -ing; S3 asks "What do you have to remember to do this week?" S4 answers with to. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
Pattern Path — how to run it
  • Groups of 3–4, one board, one coin (heads = move 1, tails = move 2) or a die, and one referee sheet face down. Each player uses a small object as a counter (a coin, an eraser, a paper clip).
  • Land on a square and do the task. Complete: say the whole sentence with the right form. Meaning: explain the difference between the two sentences in your own words ("The first one is a memory, the second is a duty"). Error Hunt: find the mistake and say the correct sentence. Talk: speak for 20 seconds using the pattern on the square.
  • The referee is the player to the right of the speaker. They check the sheet and say "Correct, stay!" or "Not quite, go back to where you were." Then the sheet moves one person to the left. Everyone referees, so a strong speaker can't take over.
  • A Talk square is correct if the speaker keeps going for 20 seconds and uses the pattern at least once. Fluency counts; small slips are OK.
  • First to FINISH wins, but keep playing until time is up. Groups that finish early start again from square 1 and must answer the Talk squares with a different example.
  • While they play, write down errors and good phrases for the feedback stage. Don't correct during the game unless the referee asks you.
ICQs before the game and the talk
Game: What does heads mean? (Move 1.) Tails? (Move 2.) · On an Error Hunt square, is it enough to find the mistake? (No, you fix it too.) · Who checks the answer? (The referee: the person on your right.) · What happens to the sheet after each turn? (It moves to the left.) · Wrong answer? (Go back to where you were.)
Talk: How long does A talk? (Two minutes.) · Can A read a script? (No, only key words.) · What does B do while A talks? (Listens and ticks the patterns.) · And after? (Asks two follow-up questions.) · Must it be your real life? (No, a new identity card or invented is fine.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with three frames only, color-coded on a strip: I enjoy + -ing (blue) · I want to + verb (green) · I remember + -ing / Remember to + verb (both). Use the activity pictures as prompts rather than written sentences. In the Pattern Path, pair the learner with a partner who reads the square aloud; they answer orally. In the talk, one minute is enough, from picture notes. Target: 6 spoken sentences with the right form and one meaning-change pair.

Stretch (toward C1)

Perfect and passive forms: I remember being told… · She denied having taken it · He seems to have left · I hate being interrupted. More meaning-change verbs: need doing (= need to be done: "The car needs washing") and be afraid of falling vs be afraid to ask. In the talk, use at least one perfect or passive gerund. Early finishers write three Pattern Path squares of their own for another group.

L1 watch-outs
  • Base verb after a preposition: "without say goodbye," "before eat," "interested in learn." Spanish (sin decir, antes de comer), Portuguese, French, Italian and Arabic use the infinitive after prepositions. Keep saying: "After a preposition: -ing. Always."
  • for + -ing / for to for purpose: "I came for asking…," "I went for to buy…" (Spanish para comprar, French pour acheter, Portuguese para comprar, Tagalog para). Teach to as the purpose word.
  • "I want that you come" / "I want you come": Spanish quiero que vengas, Russian хочу, чтобы ты пришёл, Arabic ureed an, Chinese 我要你来. Drill "want you to come" as one chunk.
  • "She suggested me to go" / "suggested to go": very common across languages (French suggérer de, Spanish sugerir que). suggest takes -ing or a that-clause: "She suggested going / that I go."
  • "They made him to wait" / "Let me to help": Spanish, Arabic, Russian and Chinese learners often add to. Cross it out on the board every time.
  • Infinitive as subject: "To learn English is difficult" (Spanish Aprender inglés es difícil, French, Italian, Portuguese). It's grammatical but stiff; recast: "Learning English is difficult."
  • No -ing form: Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Indonesian speakers have no verb endings and may say "I enjoy cook." Tap the board where -ing goes.
Homework

Online lesson 2.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_04.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 2.1: finish anything left from Ex 1–5, record (or say to a family member or friend) the "Two minutes about me" talk, "My new words" and the journal (six sentences: two -ing verbs, two to verbs, one meaning-change pair).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B I'm tired of waiting. · Q2 A to buy · Q3 (new) B I remember calling my mom on her birthday. Kai's routine (Ex 1): 1 to change · 2 staying · 3 to be · 4 to clear · 5 getting · 6 joining · 7 meeting · 8 buying · 9 to charge.

2.2 · C5

Modals of Deduction in the Past

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • make deductions about the past with modal + have + past participle: must have (almost sure it happened), can't / couldn't have (almost sure it didn't), might / may / could have (possible), might not / may not have (possibly not)
  • avoid the two classic errors: mustn't have for a negative deduction and could of / must of in writing
  • use should have / shouldn't have to criticize, regret or give too-late advice
  • tell the two jobs of could have apart: a past possibility (He could have gone home) and a missed chance (I could have gone, but I didn't)
  • turn a present deduction into a past one (must be → must have been) and reason aloud with a group to a verdict on a mystery
Materials
  • Workbook 2.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Room 12 detective notes)
  • Slides 9–13 · Past-modal flashcards (must have · can't have · might have · should have · could have) · Room 12 clue cards A–D (1 set per group of 4) · detective notes (1 per learner) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 2.2-A (three short puzzles) and 2.2-B (Maria's voicemail: the solution)

Left: the certainty scale as a vertical line: 95% ✔ must have + pp · 50% might / may / could have + pp · 30% might not / may not have + pp · 95% ✘ can't / couldn't have + pp. Beside it in red: mustn't have · must of · must has · must have rain.

Center: NOW → PAST: He must be tired → He must have been tired · She can't know → She can't have known · It might be a mistake → It might have been a mistake.

Right: JUDGING: You should have told me. (You didn't. ✘) · I shouldn't have eaten that. (I did. ✘) · could have ① He could have gone home. (possible) ② I could have gone, but I stayed home. (a missed chance)

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: patterns quick-fire8S–S → T–SReview C4. Pairs tell each other one thing from their homework talk with each of: I've stopped… · I'm looking forward to… · I must remember to…. Then a 2-minute "Error Hunt" on the board: 4 errors from C4 (e.g. "I went for buying bread," "She made me to laugh"). Learners fix them.
Lead-in: what happened here?5T–SPut a paper cup, a phone charger and a wet umbrella on your desk (or draw them). "When I came into this room today, I found these. What's happening?" Elicit present deductions from B1: "Someone must be here." Then: "The cup is cold. It was here before. So…?" Elicit or model: "Someone must have been here earlier." Write both on the board.
Clarify: past deduction13T–SSlides 9–10. Form (modal + have + past participle; the modal never changes), the certainty scale, the negative pair (must have ↔ can't have, never mustn't have), might not have, and present → past. CCQs (below). Stick the modal cards on the scale as you go.
Drill6T–S → SClue → verdict drill and weak have /əv/ (see Drill script).
Clarify: should have & could have8T–SSlide 11. "Deduction = what happened. Should have = was it a good idea?" Two situations: a friend forgot an umbrella and got soaked; you had a free ticket to a concert but stayed home. Elicit "She should have taken an umbrella" and "I could have gone, but I didn't." Then the two jobs of could have. CCQs. Link back to C2 (third conditional) and C3 (I wish I had…).
Controlled practice12S → S–SPairs read the office dialogue (slide 12 / workbook) aloud and underline every modal + have + participle (3 min). Then Workbook Ex 1–4 alone (clues, how sure, correct the error, react), then check in pairs. Board-check Ex 3: learners come up and cross out of, mustn't and has in red. Early finishers do the stretch line in Ex 4.
Listening: three puzzles6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 5. Read Script 2.2-A (three short puzzles), pausing after each. Learners write one must have and one can't have / might have sentence for each. Pairs compare: "Did we reach the same verdict? Who's more sure?"
Freer: The Mystery of Room 1220S–S (groups of 4) → T–SSlide 13. Read the setting aloud. Each learner gets one clue card (A, B, C or D) and must not show it. (1) Round the table: each learner reads their three clues aloud, the others take notes (5 min). (2) Discuss and write at least six deductions on the detective notes, each with a certainty level (8 min). (3) Each group gives its verdict in 3–4 sentences: "We're almost sure that… They can't have… They might have…" (4 min). (4) Read Script 2.2-B (Maria's voicemail). Which group was closest? (3 min). ICQs first. See "how to run it."
Feedback7T–SDelayed correction: 3 errors ("She mustn't have known." · "They must of left." · "He must has dropped the keys.") and 2–3 good examples of range from the verdicts ("They can't possibly have eaten it," "Lin must have been soaked"). Praise the groups that said how sure they were.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 2.2; workbook journal. Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total908 + 5 + 13 + 6 + 8 + 12 + 6 + 20 + 7 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: deduction about the past. We use evidence to reach a confident guess about something that already happened. The modal shows how sure we are.

"The ground's wet. It must have rained." Did I see the rain? (No.) How do I know? (The evidence: the wet ground.) How sure am I? (Almost sure, about 95%.) · "He can't have finished already." Do I think he finished? (No, I'm almost sure he didn't.)
"She might have missed the bus." Am I sure? (No, it's one possibility.) Can I say may have or could have? (Yes, the same meaning.) · "She mustn't have seen us." Correct? (No! "She can't have seen us.") Why? (mustn't means "it's not allowed.")

Meaning: should have.

"You should have called me." Did you call me? (No.) Was that a good idea? (No, a mistake.) · "I shouldn't have eaten that." Did I eat it? (Yes.) Do I feel good about it? (No, I regret it.) · Is should have about how sure I am? (No, it judges a choice.)

Meaning: could have (two jobs).

"Where's Sam? He could have gone home." Do I know where Sam is? (No: it's a possibility.) · "I could have gone to the concert, but I stayed home." Did I go? (No.) Was it possible? (Yes, a missed chance.) · "You could have warned me!" Did you warn me? (No.) Am I a little annoyed? (Yes.)

Form.

Sure it happenedmust have + past participleEveryone's talking about it. It must have been a great night.
Possiblemight / may / could have + ppThey might have taken a wrong turn.
Possibly notmight not / may not have + ppBen may not have gone. He was sick.
Sure it didn'tcan't / couldn't have + ppIt can't have finished early. The photos are from 2 a.m.
Judging a choiceshould / shouldn't have + ppI should have checked the forecast.
Missed chancecould have + pp (but didn't)We could have won, but we missed the penalty.
Now → pastmodal + be / verb → modal + have + ppmust be → must have been · might know → might have known
Continuousmodal + have + been + -ingShe looked exhausted. She must have been working late.

Pronunciation. In speech, have is weak and joins the modal: must've /ˈmʌstəv/, might've /ˈmaɪtəv/, could've /ˈkʊdəv/, should've /ˈʃʊdəv/. That's why native speakers sometimes write "could of": it's always wrong in writing. Stress the modal and the main verb, not have: "She MUST've LEFT." In American English, can't have /ˈkænt əv/ and can have sound close: the difference is the stressed, longer vowel in can't, so teach learners to say "CAN'T have" with strong stress, or use couldn't have, which is easier to hear.

Drill script
  1. Weak "have": must've · might've · could've · should've · can't've: choral ×2 each, then in a phrase: "She must've left." "He can't've known."
  2. Clue → verdict (T gives evidence, S gives the deduction): T: "The street is wet." → S: "It must have rained." T: "He was at work in Denver until nine. The party was in Chicago at nine." → S: "He can't have been at the party." T: "She didn't answer my call." → S: "She might have been busy."
  3. Now → past: T: "He must be tired." → S: "He must have been tired." T: "She can't know." → S: "She can't have known." T: "They might be lost." → S: "They might have been lost."
  4. Fix it: T: "She mustn't have seen us." → S: "She can't have seen us." T: "He must has forgotten." → S: "He must have forgotten."
  5. Judge it: T: "I didn't study and I failed." → S: "You should have studied." T: "He ate three pieces of cake and felt sick." → S: "He shouldn't have eaten so much."
The Mystery of Room 12 — how to run it
  • Setting (read aloud, slide 13): "It's Saturday, 9:15 a.m. You arrive early at the Maple Street Community Center for your 9:30 English class in Room 12. The door is open, nobody is there, and something strange has happened. What happened here?"
  • Groups of 4. Each learner gets one clue card (A, B, C or D) with three text clues. They read their clues aloud but don't show the card: this is an information gap, so every learner must speak. The others take notes on the detective notes sheet.
  • Then the group discusses. Every deduction needs a modal and a reason: "The coffee is still warm, so they can't have left long ago." Push for the full scale: at least one must have, one can't have, one might have and one should have.
  • Roles (useful for dominant speakers): a secretary writes the verdict; a skeptic must challenge every must have: "Are you sure? It might have been someone else." Give the skeptic role to your strongest speaker: it makes them listen and gives others the floor. The secretary is not the strongest speaker.
  • Verdict: 3–4 sentences, from most sure to least sure. Then read Script 2.2-B. Score: 1 point for each part of the verdict that matches the voicemail (the surprise party · Rosa's note · Plan B at the park · they left in a hurry · the keys were dropped). Celebrate the best reasoning, not only the right answer.
  • Groups of 3: one learner holds cards C and D. Groups of 5: two learners share card A and take turns reading.
  • It's a happy mystery on purpose: a surprise party, a change of plan, a forgotten umbrella. Don't turn it into a crime.
ICQs before the mystery
Can you show your card to the group? (No! Read it aloud.) · How many deductions do you write? (At least six.) · Is "They left" a deduction? (No, it needs a modal: "They must have left.") · What does the skeptic do? (Says "Are you sure?" and suggests another possibility.) · Is it a crime story? (No! Something happy happened.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on two forms: must have (sure yes) and can't have (sure no), with thumbs up / thumbs down. Give a strip with 5 past participles they will need (been, left, eaten, seen, gone). In the mystery, give card D (the shortest clues) and read it quietly with the learner first; their job is to say the key deduction about Rosa's note. Target: 4 spoken deductions with must have / can't have.

Stretch (toward C1)

Degrees of certainty: may well have (likely), can't possibly have, is bound to have. Continuous and passive forms: must have been waiting · might have been taken. needn't have (did it, but it wasn't necessary: "We needn't have brought the cake knife") vs didn't need to. In the verdict, rank six deductions by certainty and use at least two of these.

L1 watch-outs
  • "mustn't have" for "can't have": learners generalize the negative of must. German (muss nicht = doesn't have to) and Dutch speakers find it especially confusing. Use the "opposite pair" card: must have ↔ can't have.
  • Missing or wrong participle: "It must have rain," "She must have went." Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Indonesian have no participle endings; many other learners mix the past simple and the participle. Keep a list of irregular participles on the board (been, gone, seen, eaten, taken, left, forgotten).
  • "must has / must had": agreement habits from the present perfect. After a modal, have never changes.
  • A different structure: Spanish debe (de) haber salido and Portuguese deve ter saído map well; but Arabic, Russian (должно быть, он ушёл = "must be, he left") and Japanese (…はずだ) use an adverb + past tense. Learners may say "Maybe he must left" or "Probably he left." Accept probably as good English, then offer the modal.
  • could of / should of: learners who learn by ear (and many native speakers) write of. Always have in writing.
  • "can" vs "can't" in American English: learners (and listeners) miss the t. Teach stress, and accept couldn't have as the clearer choice.
Homework

Online lesson 2.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_05.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back with weak have; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. The online project's case file is optional: learners may use the Room 12 clues or invent a light, everyday scene instead (a messy kitchen, a canceled event). Workbook 2.2: "My new words" and the journal (a small, puzzling event from the week, real or invented: three deductions + one should have).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A must have · Q2 A I should have reminded you. · Q3 (new) B She can't have seen us. Three puzzles (Ex 5), model answers: 1 Her roommate must have made soup; she can't have left long ago. · 2 Ben must have gone on vacation; he can't have left in a hurry. · 3 The class must have moved to Room 8; the teacher might have written the note.

2.3 · C6

Used to / Be Used to / Get Used to

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • use used to + base verb for past habits and states that are no longer true, with didn't use to and Did you use to…? (no d)
  • use be used to + noun / -ing for something that is familiar and normal for them, in any tense
  • use get used to + noun / -ing for the process of adapting (I'm getting used to…, I got used to…, You'll get used to…)
  • explain why the form changes (in be / get used to, to is a preposition) and apply the "it" test
  • talk about adjusting to something new (a job, a schedule, technology, a neighborhood, a hobby) using all three structures
Materials
  • Workbook 2.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "Adjusting to something new" notes)
  • Slides 14–19 · The three used to cards + the "it" test card · Find someone who grid (1 per learner) · change cards (1 set per group of 3) · role-play cards A/B · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 2.3-A (Dana's new shift) and 2.3-B (dictation)

Left: a timeline. PAST (gone): I used to work in an office. (base verb) · NOW (a state): I'm used to the early start / getting up early. · CHANGING (a process, an arrow getting bigger): I'm getting used to the new app / using the scanner.

Center: used to + BASE VERB · be used to + NOUN / -ING · get used to + NOUN / -ING. The "it" test: I'm used to it ✔ → to = preposition → -ing · I used to it ✘ → base verb.

Right: questions and negatives: Did you use to…? · I didn't use to… (no d) · Present habit? I use to jog → I usually jog. · US English: I've gotten used to it (UK: I've got used to it).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: what must have happened?8S–S → T–SReview C5. Read 4 quick situations ("Your coworker arrives with a big smile and a box of donuts." · "The bus is 20 minutes late and very full." · "Your neighbor's garden is full of balloons." · "The office printer has a sign: DO NOT USE."). Pairs give a must have, a might have and a can't have for each. Two pairs share. Two learners read their journal (only if they want to).
Lead-in: my new bike5T–STell a short invented story: "I used to take the bus to work. Last spring I started biking. At first it was hard: the hills! But I'm used to the hills now. I'm still getting used to the rain, though." Write the three bold phrases. "Which one is finished? Which is normal now? Which is still changing?" Let learners notice.
Clarify: three structures14T–SSlides 14–15. Meaning with the timeline; form (base verb vs noun / -ing); didn't use to; the "it" test; used to is past only (no present form). Then be used to (state) vs get used to (process): "If you can say become, use get." CCQs (below). Mention US I've gotten used to.
Drill6T–S → SStructure-switch drill and used to /ˈjuːstə/ (see Drill script).
Controlled practice10S → S–SPairs read the Luis and Grace dialogue (slide 16 / workbook) and sort every used to into past / now / changing (3 min). Then Workbook Ex 1–3 alone (choose the structure, base or -ing, correct the error), then pairs check. Board-check Ex 3 by asking "Which test did you use?"
Listening: Dana's new shift7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 4. Read Script 2.3-A twice. Learners complete the six sentences. Pairs compare, then answer: "What's Dana's advice? Do you agree?" Then Ex 5 (rewrite) if time allows, or as homework.
Freer 1: Find someone who10S–S (mingle)Slide 17. Learners turn each line into a question ("Are you used to getting up before six?" · "Did you use to play an instrument?") and mingle. Freer 2: Adjusting to something new18S → S–S (groups of 3)Slide 18. (2) Groups of 3 with a talking-turn token: each learner talks for about 2 minutes; the others ask two follow-up questions and then give advice with You'll get used to… / It took me… to get used to… (12 min). (3) Each group shares the best piece of advice they heard (3 min). Pairs who finish early do a role-play card. ICQs first.
Feedback + module check7T–SDelayed correction: "I'm used to work nights." · "I didn't used to like coffee." · "I use to drink tea every morning." · "I'm used to it" when the speaker meant "still adapting." Two good phrases you heard (It took me ages to get used to…). Then learners tick the three Module 2 "I can…" lists.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 2.3; the workbook journal; preview C7: "Next class: It is believed that… The passive, advanced."
Total908 + 5 + 14 + 6 + 10 + 7 + 10 + 18 + 7 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: used to (past). A habit or state in the past that is not true now.

"I used to take the bus." Do I take the bus now? (No.) Did I take it once or many times? (Many times: a habit.) · "There used to be a bakery here." Is it still here? (No.) · Can I say "I use to take the bus" about now? (No! "I usually take the bus.")

Meaning: be used to (a state) vs get used to (a process).

"I'm used to the hills." Are the hills difficult for me now? (No, they're normal.) Were they difficult before? (Yes, maybe.) · "I'm getting used to the rain." Is the rain normal for me yet? (Not yet, but it's getting easier.) Is it finished or still happening? (Still happening.)
"She got used to working nights." Was it easy at first? (No.) Is it OK now? (Yes, the change is finished.) · "You'll get used to it." When? (In the future.) · "I'm used to work long hours." Correct? (No: "used to working".) Why? (to is a preposition. Test: "I'm used to it" ✔.)

Form.

used toused to + base verb · past only · didn't use to · Did you use to…?She used to play tennis. · He didn't use to like coffee.
be used toam / is / are / was / will be + used to + noun / pronoun / -ingI'm used to the noise. · She's used to working nights.
get used toget / am getting / got / will get / have gotten + used to + noun / -ingI'm getting used to the commute. · You'll get used to living alone.
Useful chunksIt took me (a month) to get used to… · I still can't get used to… · It takes some getting used to.It took me months to get used to the new system.

Pronunciation. In all three structures, used to is /ˈjuːstə/: an s sound, not a z, and a weak to. Compare used = "utilized" /juːzd/: "I used /juːzd/ a knife." In speech, used to and use to sound the same, so the d problem is a writing problem: say it, then check the spelling after did / didn't. The contraction does a lot of work: "I'm used to it" vs "I used to…" — the 'm is small but essential. Drill it with a clear /m/.

Drill script
  1. /ˈjuːstə/: "used to" ×3 (hiss the s), then "I used to…" · "I'm used to…" · "I'm getting used to…" (clear 'm).
  2. Switch (T says a situation, S chooses): T: "In the past: I lived near the beach. Not now." → S: "I used to live near the beach." T: "Early meetings are normal for me." → S: "I'm used to early meetings." T: "The new app is still strange, but it's getting easier." → S: "I'm getting used to the new app."
  3. Base or -ing? T: "used to / drive" → S: "used to drive." T: "be used to / drive" → S: "I'm used to driving." T: "get used to / wake up early" → S: "I'm getting used to waking up early."
  4. Did you use to…? Chain: S1 asks S2 "Did you use to play a sport?" S2 answers ("Yes, I used to play volleyball." / "No, I didn't use to…"), then asks S3 a new question.
  5. Advice: T: "I just started the night shift. It's terrible!" → S: "Don't worry, you'll get used to it." T: "It took me two months." → S: "It took me two months to get used to it."
Adjusting to something new — how to run it
  • Put the change cards face up on each table. Each card names a neutral change and three prompts: How did things use to be? What are you used to now? What are you still getting used to? Learners may pick a card, use a change from their own life, or use their new identity card's change. All are equal.
  • Notes, not a script: 2 used to + 2 be used to + 1 getting used to + 1 piece of advice for someone starting the same change.
  • Groups of 3, one talking-turn token (a pen). Only the person holding it talks; after 2 minutes, the listeners each ask one follow-up question ("How long did it take you to get used to…?" · "What do you miss about how it used to be?"), then give one piece of advice ("You'll get used to…" · "Try…ing").
  • Dominant speakers: the token helps; also give the strongest speaker the job of speaking last and summarizing the group's best advice. Quiet learners: they can talk from the card prompts, and 1 minute is fine.
  • Keep it neutral. The changes on the cards are everyday (a new job, a night shift, a new phone or app, self-checkout, a new neighborhood across town, a new hobby). Don't add "moving to a new country" as a card and don't ask about it. If a learner chooses it themselves, that's their choice: listen and respond warmly, without probing.
  • Early finishers: role-play cards (A = two weeks into a change, B = a friend who went through it). They act it out for 3 minutes, then swap cards.
ICQs before the mingle and the discussion
Mingle: How many names on each line? (One.) · Do you say "Find someone who is used to…"? (No, make a question: "Are you used to…?") · Can someone say "Pass"? (Yes.) · What do you write next to the name? (One detail.)
Discussion: Do you have to talk about your real life? (No: a card, a new identity, or invented.) · Who can talk? (Only the person with the pen.) · How long? (About two minutes.) · What do the listeners do after? (Ask a question and give advice.) · Which structure is for "still changing"? (getting used to.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Color-code the three structures on a strip: used to + verb (past, gray) · I'm used to + -ing (now, green) · I'm getting used to + -ing (changing, orange). Give the learner a change card with one sentence head for each color. In the mingle, give a grid with 5 lines instead of 10. Target: one spoken sentence with each structure, with -ing after be / get used to.

Stretch (toward C1)

would for past habits in a story (We would walk to school together) and why it can't do states (I would have long hair); be accustomed to + -ing (more formal); grow used to; It takes some getting used to; I've never gotten used to…. In the discussion, use all of these once and narrate the change as a mini-story. Early finishers write the dialogue from their role-play.

L1 watch-outs
  • "I use to get up early" for a present habit: Spanish suelo, Portuguese costumo, Italian di solito and Tagalog kadalasan have a present "habit" verb or adverb, so learners invent a present use to. Teach usually for now.
  • "I'm used to work": Spanish estoy acostumbrado a trabajar, French être habitué à travailler, Portuguese estou acostumado a trabalhar all use the infinitive. The "it" test is the fix.
  • "I didn't used to": a spelling error that everyone makes (native speakers too). Drop the d after did.
  • Mixing state and process: many languages (Chinese 习惯了, Japanese 慣れた, Korean 익숙해졌다) use one expression for both "be used to" and "got used to." Use the timeline: a dot (state) vs an arrow (process).
  • Dropping "be": "I used to the cold" (Arabic, Russian, Chinese, Haitian Creole speakers may drop be). This accidentally makes a different structure. Point at the missing 'm.
  • /juːzd tuː/: learners who read the spelling say "use-d to" with a z and a strong to. Model /ˈjuːstə/.
Homework

Online lesson 2.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_06.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. For the online project ("a big change"), learners can talk about any change from class (a job, schedule, technology, neighborhood, hobby) or their new identity card: moving countries is not required. Workbook 2.3: finish Ex 5 if needed, "My new words" and the journal (three true or invented sentences, one with each structure).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B am used to · Q2 B I'm used to working long shifts. · Q3 (new) B used to. Dana's new shift (Ex 4): 1 start · 2 sleeping · 3 got used to · 4 is (she's) used to · 5 get used to · 6 get used to.

Closing the Module

End of C6: Module 2 wrap-up

Recap on slide 19, "three tools for precision": I stopped to… / I stopped …ing (verb patterns that change meaning) → They must have… / can't have… / might have… (how sure you are about the past) → I used to… / I'm used to… / I'm getting used to… (past, now, changing). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C7 warmer and again in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 3: the advanced passive (It is believed that…, He is said to have left) uses the perfect infinitive (to have + pp), which looks a lot like the past modals from C5. Learners who just mastered must have left may say "He is said to must have left." Also, the reporting verbs in 3.3 are verb-pattern verbs (suggest -ing, refuse to, advise someone to): recycle the Pattern Path board as a C9 warmer.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: verb + -ing vs verb + to, preposition + -ing (including look forward to), the infinitive of purpose, let / make + base verb, the meaning-change verbs (remember, forget, stop, try), past deduction on the certainty scale (must have / can't have / might have, never mustn't have), should have and the two jobs of could have, and the three used to structures with the right form after each.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 3 — Advanced Structures

Three classes that take structures learners met at B1 (the passive, relative clauses, reported speech) and turn them into tools for precision and economy. First, the passive across every tense and modal, plus the impersonal reporting passive of news and formal writing (It is said that… · He is thought to have…) and a first look at the causative (I had my car repaired). Then relative clauses reduced to participle clauses (the man standing there · a letter written in haste · Having finished, she left) and the dangling-participle trap. Last, precise reporting verbs and the pattern each one demands (suggested going · insisted on paying · denied taking · refused to sign).

3 lessons Lessons 3.1–3.3 Classes C7–C9 Weeks 4–5 90 min per class CEFR B2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (a real communicative task) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-3-advanced-structures.html. Print materials/module-3/classroom-materials.html before C7. It holds the flashcards, the new identity cards, the Newsroom A/B story cards, the Editor's Desk drafts and combine-and-reduce strips, the Spot the Difference scene cards, the Meeting role cards and minutes sheet, the Report It! board game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. At B1 learners met the basic passive (B1 Module 4), relative clauses with commas (B1 3.3) and reported speech with backshift (B1 3.1–3.2). Don't re-teach those. Check them fast in each lead-in and spend the time on what is new at B2: modal-perfect and impersonal passives, reduced clauses and participle clauses, and verb + pattern reporting. The causative gets a short first look in C7. Lesson 5.2 (C15) teaches it fully, so keep it to five minutes here.
  • Fluency before accuracy. In every freer task, listen and take notes in two columns: accuracy (errors to fix) and range (good, ambitious language to praise and spread). Don't stop a newsreader or a meeting to fix a pattern. In the feedback stage, write 2–3 errors and 1–2 "range" examples on the board without names. At B2, feedback on range matters as much as feedback on errors: "Someone said It is widely believed that…. Excellent. Who else can use widely?"
  • Dominant speakers. All three tasks have built-in roles. Use them. Give the strongest speaker the job that needs listening rather than talking: the editor in the newsroom (checks, doesn't read), the word-counter in the compression workshop, the minute-taker in the meeting. Use a talking-turn token (a pen: you speak only when you hold it) in the meeting. Say it kindly: "Great point, Omar. Let's hear from someone who hasn't spoken yet."
  • Stretching strong learners toward C1. Each lesson has a stretch box. The C1 direction for this module is choice and register, not new forms: why the passive here, when not to reduce a clause, which reporting verb carries the most exact meaning (claimed vs admitted vs insisted). Ask strong learners to explain the choice, not only produce the form.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British spellings and words (analysed, apologise, organise, memorise, signalling, cancelled, queue, resit, film). Teach analyzed, apologize, organize, memorize, signaling, canceled, line (up), retake, movie. Also teach the American pattern suggest / insist / recommend that + base verb (She suggested that we meet on Friday · He insisted that we stay for dinner). Accept British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Shift the focus → cut the words → name the act. In C7 learners move the doer out of the spotlight and speak in the calm voice of the news: The tortoise is thought to be 120 years old. In C8 they compress: two or three short sentences become one tight one (Built in 1890, the station is the oldest building in town.). In C9 they compress other people's words: a whole line of speech becomes one exact verb (She refused to sign. He admitted forgetting.). Every class ends with a real product that other learners hear or read: a radio bulletin, an edited notice, a set of meeting minutes.

Trauma-informed: light news, low stakes

The language of this module lives close to hard topics: news of disasters and crime, He is believed to have been killed, She had her identity stolen, accusations, denials, interviews. In class, keep everything light and invented. Use only the invented stories on the Newsroom cards (a 120-year-old tortoise, a piano in the train station, a library book returned 50 years late). Don't bring in real headlines, and don't ask learners to report news from their home countries. In the causative, prefer services (had my hair cut, got my phone fixed) over misfortunes; if you teach the misfortune meaning, use had my flight canceled, not theft or violence. The meetings in C9 are about a laundry room, a café schedule and a community garden. Never role-play a police interview, a court, an immigration or asylum interview, or a family argument. For every personal task, offer three choices out loud: your real life, a new identity card, or something invented. Opinion roles in the meeting are assigned: learners play the card, not themselves.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): the passive-pattern cards and the service picture cards (C7), the participle cards and place picture cards (C8), and the reporting-verb cards, color-coded by pattern (C9). Card stock helps. Cut them before C7.
  • For C7: the Newsroom A/B story cards (6 stories, one A and one B card each) and one bulletin frame per pair. Optional: a table bell or a phone timer for the "on air" signal.
  • For C8: one Editor's Desk draft per group of 3 (4 different drafts), one envelope of combine-and-reduce strips per pair, and one Spot the Difference A/B pair per pair of learners.
  • For C9: Meeting role cards (3 meetings × 4 roles; one meeting per group of 4–5) and one minutes sheet per learner. The Report It! board game (1 board per group, 1 die, counters) is for early finishers in C9 and for the C10 warmer.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking.
  • Optional: bring one real English-language local newspaper or community newsletter. Learners enjoy finding "It is expected that…" and "Located near…" in real print. Pick light pages (events, sports, the weather, the library).

Lesson Plans

3.1 · C7

The Passive — Advanced & Impersonal

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • form the passive in any tense or with any modal by changing only be: is done, was being done, has been done, will be done, must be done, should have been done
  • report a general belief without naming who believes it, in two ways: Pattern A It is said / believed / expected that… and Pattern B She is said to be…
  • choose the right infinitive in Pattern B for timing: to be (same time), to be + -ing (in progress now), to have + past participle (earlier)
  • say that someone else did a job for them with have / get + object + past participle: I had my car repaired.
  • choose the passive on purpose for a formal or news register, and switch back to the active when the doer matters
Materials
  • Workbook 3.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, News Report Rewrite)
  • Slides 3–7 · Passive-pattern flashcards · Service picture cards (causative) · Newsroom A/B story cards (6 stories) + bulletin frames · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.1-A (Riverside Community Radio bulletin) and 3.1-B (dictation)

Left: the ladder (only BE moves). is done · is being done · was done · was being done · has been done · had been done · will be done · is going to be done · must / can / should be done · should / must / might have been done.

Center: two patterns. People say (that) he is rich. → A It is said that he is rich. → B He is said to be rich. Timing strip under B: now → to be · in progress → to be living · earlier → to have left / to have been. Verbs: say · believe · think · know · report · expect · consider · understand.

Right: causative. I had my car repaired. (a mechanic did it) · I had repaired my car ✘ (= I did it, earlier) · get = more informal. Phrase bank: It is widely believed that… · It has been reported that… · … is expected to…

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: getting used to it7S–SReview C6 (2.3, used to / be used to / get used to). Board: "When I started this class / my job / living here, I wasn't used to… Now I'm getting used to… I used to…, but now…" Pairs share two true or invented sentences (new identity cards available), then tell the class one thing their partner said. Note one error and one good example for later.
Lead-in: headlines5T–SBoard: three invented headlines: "RIVERSIDE ZOO TORTOISE THOUGHT TO BE 120" · "MYSTERY PIANO LEFT IN STATION" · "LIBRARY BOOK RETURNED 50 YEARS LATE." Ask: "Who thinks the tortoise is 120? Who left the piano? Do we know? Does it matter?" Elicit: the news focuses on the event, not the person. "Today you're going to be newsreaders."
The passive ladder8T–SSlide 3. Fast review, not re-teaching: elicit the ladder with do from learners, writing only what they can't produce. Focus on the B2 forms: modal perfect (should have been done) and the two continuous passives (is / was being done). CCQs (below).
Impersonal reporting passives15T–SSlides 4–5. Write the active source sentence "People say he is rich." Build Pattern A, then B. Then the timing strip: to be / to be living / to have left. Board three more transformations with learners (believe, expect, report). Meaning, form, pronunciation, CCQs.
Drill7T–S → STransformation drill (see Drill script): A → B, active → A, then timing. Choral, then individuals, then open pairs.
Listening: the 3 o'clock bulletin7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 3.1-A twice in a calm "radio" voice. First listening: "How many stories? Which one is the funniest?" Second: learners complete the seven passive forms. Pairs compare. Point out that the bulletin is almost all passive: why? (The events matter, not the people.)
Causative: a first look5T–SSlide 6. Hold up the service picture cards: "I didn't fix my car. A mechanic did. So… I had my car repaired." Elicit 4–5 more from the pictures (had a key cut, got my shoes repaired, had my passport renewed). One CCQ on word order. Tell learners: "We'll do much more with this in Lesson 5.2."
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone (the ladder, Pattern A and B, the timing choice, the causative), then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on. Early finishers start the "Rewrite it formally" box in the News Report Rewrite section.
Freer: News Report Rewrite16S–S → T–SSlide 7. Pairs get a Newsroom story: A has what people are saying (the rumors), B has the official facts. They tell each other their half without showing the card (3 min), then write a 6-sentence formal bulletin on the frame (7 min) with at least 2 impersonal passives, 1 modal passive and 1 other tense. Then (6 min) pairs go "on air": each reads the bulletin in a newsreader voice; the class asks one follow-up question. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction. Board 3 errors you heard (no names): "It is said him to be…" · "The piano is thought to leave by a musician." · "It should have done yesterday." And 1–2 range examples to praise ("is widely believed," "is expected to be completed"). Learners fix and discuss.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 3.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 5 + 8 + 15 + 7 + 7 + 5 + 10 + 16 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: why the passive. The passive moves the doer out of the spotlight. We use it when the doer is unknown, obvious or unimportant, or when we want to keep the topic in the subject position. It is the normal voice of news, science and official notices.

"The road has been closed." Do we know who closed it? (No / not important.) What's the important thing? (The road: you can't use it.) · "The novel was written by a teenager." Why add "by a teenager"? (It's surprising: real information.)

Meaning: the modal perfect passive.

"The form should have been sent yesterday." Was it sent? (No.) Is this about the past or the future? (The past.) Is it a criticism? (Yes, a mild one.) · "The package might have been delivered to the wrong address." Are we sure? (No, it's a possibility.)

Meaning: impersonal reporting passives. We report a general belief or claim without naming who believes it. It sounds neutral and careful: the reporter doesn't say it's true.

"The tortoise is thought to be 120." Who thinks so? (People / experts: we don't say.) Is the reporter 100% sure? (No.) Is it a fact or a belief? (A belief.) · "It is expected that the station will reopen in May." Has it reopened? (No.) Is it certain? (Not completely: it's the plan.)

Meaning: timing in Pattern B.

"He is said to be rich." Is he rich now? (Yes, people say so now.) · "He is said to have been rich." Now or before? (Before: he was rich in the past.) · "She is thought to be living in Florida." Right now? (Yes, in progress.) · "The piano is believed to have been left by a musician." Did the leaving happen before people started believing it? (Yes.) Did the musician leave it, or did the piano leave? (The musician left it: passive.)

Meaning: the causative.

"I had my hair cut." Did I cut it? (No, a hairdresser did.) Did I pay / arrange it? (Yes.) · "I had cut my hair." Who cut it? (I did, before another past moment. Different meaning!) · "I got my phone fixed." Same meaning as "had"? (Yes, more informal.)

Form.

Any tenseform of be + past participle (only be changes)It has been done. · It was being done.
Modalsmodal + be + pp · modal + have been + ppIt must be done. · It should have been done.
Pattern AIt + passive reporting verb + that + clauseIt is believed that she is honest.
Pattern BSubject + passive reporting verb + to-infinitiveShe is believed to be honest.
B · timingsame time: to be / to do · in progress: to be doing · earlier: to have done / to have beenHe is thought to have left the country.
Causativehave / get + object + past participleWe had the roof repaired. · I'm getting my hair cut.

Pronunciation. In the passive, be and been are weak and the past participle carries the stress: "It's been closed" /ɪts bɪn ˈkloʊzd/, "It should've been done" /ʃʊdəv bɪn ˈdʌn/ (the have is only /əv/: never write "should of"). In Pattern A, It is is often "It's": "It's said that…"; in formal news reading, keep it full and slow. Pattern B: stress the reporting verb and the key word: "He's thought to be a millionaire." Newsreader voice: steady pace, falling intonation at the end of each sentence, a short pause after the place and time ("In Riverside today | a mystery piano…").

Drill script
  1. Up the ladder (point at the board): T: "They clean the room. Present perfect passive?" → S: "The room has been cleaned." T: "Past continuous?" → S: "The room was being cleaned." T: "Should, past?" → S: "The room should have been cleaned." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. A to B: T: "It is said that the castle is haunted." → S: "The castle is said to be haunted." T: "It is believed that the economy is recovering." → S: "The economy is believed to be recovering." T: "It is known that she was a brilliant scientist." → S: "She is known to have been a brilliant scientist."
  3. Now or earlier? T gives only the timing clue: T: "He's rich. Now." → S: "He is said to be rich." T: "He stole the money. Earlier." → S: "He is said to have stolen the money." T: "She's living in Florida. Right now." → S: "She is thought to be living in Florida."
  4. Newsroom voice: T reads a chatty line, S says it on air: T: "Everyone thinks the tortoise is about 120." → S: "The tortoise is thought to be about 120 years old." / "It is thought that…"
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 gives a chatty rumor about an invented person or place ("People say the new bakery uses a secret recipe."); S2 reports it on air in Pattern A; S3 in Pattern B. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
News Report Rewrite — how to run it
  • Pairs. Give each pair one Newsroom story: learner A gets the "What people are saying" card (chatty rumors: "Everyone says…", "Apparently…", "People think…"), learner B gets the "Official facts" card (what the town, the zoo or the library has confirmed, with plans and dates). Don't let them show the cards.
  • Share (3 min): A and B tell each other their half, in normal spoken English. They take notes.
  • Rewrite (7 min): together they write a six-sentence bulletin on the frame: a headline, then the story. Required: at least 2 impersonal passives (one Pattern A, one Pattern B), at least 1 modal passive (must be, will be, should have been) and at least 1 other tense (has been, was being). The frame has a tick-box for each. Rumors become Pattern A / B; facts become ordinary passives.
  • On air (6 min): each pair reads its bulletin in a newsreader voice (one sentence each, alternating). Ring a bell or say "You're on air." Listeners ask one follow-up question, which the pair answers in character.
  • Roles: in a group of 3, the third learner is the editor: checks the tick-boxes and the to be / to have been timing but doesn't read on air. Give this job to a dominant speaker.
  • All the stories are invented and light. Say before you start: "These are not real places. Nobody gets hurt in these stories."
ICQs before the News Report Rewrite
Can you show your card to your partner? (No, tell them.) · How many sentences in the bulletin? (Six.) · How many "It is said that / is said to" sentences? (At least two.) · The rumors: passive reporting verb or ordinary passive? (Reporting verb: "is said to…") · Who reads on air? (Both, one sentence each.) · Are these real stories? (No, invented.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on Pattern B with to be and three verbs only: said, thought, believed. Give a sentence strip: "The ___ is said to be ___. · The ___ is thought to be ___." In the newsroom, pair the learner with a supportive partner and make them the reader of 2 sentences (after practicing them with you). Fewer items in the workbook: Ex 2 items 1–4, Ex 3 items 1–2. Target: 2 correct impersonal passives, said aloud.

Stretch (toward C1)

Add adverbs of attitude and hedging: widely believed, generally accepted, reportedly, allegedly. Use Pattern B with the passive infinitive: The station is expected to be reopened in May. Ask: "Is the passive the best choice in every sentence of your bulletin? Change one back to the active and say why." Early finishers turn their bulletin into a two-sentence "spoken" version for a friend and compare the registers.

L1 watch-outs
  • No dummy "It": "Is said that he is rich." Spanish, Portuguese, Italian and Arabic don't need a subject here (se dice que, diz-se que, si dice che, yuqāl inna). Point at the empty subject position: "English needs a subject: It is said…"
  • Mixing A and B: "It is said him to be rich." / "He is said that he is rich." Common for everyone. Show the two patterns as two different doors: It… that or He… to, never both.
  • "They say" / "One says" everywhere: Russian and Polish (govoryat, mówią), German (man sagt) and French (on dit) report beliefs with an active impersonal subject. That's fine in speech. In the bulletin, ask for the passive.
  • Missing be / been: "The bridge built in 1890." (as a whole sentence) · "It should have done." Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Indonesian speakers often drop the auxiliary. Underline be in a different color on the ladder.
  • The passive = something bad: Chinese (被 bèi), Korean and Japanese passives often suggest an unpleasant event, so some learners avoid the passive for neutral news. Show neutral examples ("The results are expected to improve").
  • "I cut my hair" for a haircut: Spanish me corté el pelo, Portuguese cortei o cabelo, Italian, Tagalog and others say "I cut my hair" when a hairdresser did it. In English that means you held the scissors. Teach "I had / got my hair cut."
Homework

Online lesson 3.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_07.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Formal Briefing" project (record it on a phone if possible). Workbook 3.1: finish the News Report Rewrite (write a second bulletin from the other story on your card, or an invented one), "My new words" and the journal (three chatty sentences rewritten as impersonal passives + one causative sentence about something you had done recently, real or invented).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B The manager is thought to have left the company last year. · Q2 C I had my brakes fixed yesterday. · Q3 (new) B The form should have been sent yesterday. Radio bulletin (Ex 1): 1 is thought to be · 2 was found · 3 is believed to have been left · 4 has been asked · 5 was returned · 6 is said to have been borrowed · 7 will be held.

3.2 · C8

Relative & Participle Clauses

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • tell defining clauses (no commas, that OK) from non-defining clauses (commas, no that) and know which ones can be reduced
  • reduce a defining relative clause to a participle: active → -ing (the man standing there), passive → past participle (the letter written by John), using the test "Does the noun do the action or receive it?"
  • join two sentences with a participle clause that shows time (Walking home, I…), reason (Feeling tired, … · Not knowing…, …) or sequence (Having finished, …)
  • find and fix a dangling participle by checking that the participle and the main clause have the same subject
  • decide when not to reduce: single finished past actions, non-defining clauses in careful writing, and relaxed speech
Materials
  • Workbook 3.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Sentence Compression Workshop)
  • Slides 8–12 · Participle flashcards (-ing = does · -ed / 3rd form = receives · having + pp · Not + -ing) · Place picture cards · Editor's Desk drafts (4) · Combine-and-reduce strips (1 envelope per pair) · Spot the Difference A/B scenes · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.2-A (Old Town audio tour) and 3.2-B ("Dangler or not?")

Left: reduce it. The man who is standing there · The woman who lives living next door · The letter which was written by John · Test: does the noun DO it? → -ing · RECEIVE it? → past participle. The books selling ✘ sold here ✔.

Center: join it. ⏱ time: Walking home, I saw… · 💡 reason: Feeling tired, he went to bed. · Not knowing the way, we got lost. · Made of glass, it breaks easily. · ➡️ sequence: Having finished, she left.

Right: the subject rule. Walking home, the rain started. ✘ (the rain walked?) · Walking home, I got caught in the rain. ✔ · Check the first noun after the comma. Don't reduce: the man who called me yesterday (one finished action) · My sister, who lives in Ohio, … (careful writing).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: the rumor mill8S–SReview C7. Each learner writes one light rumor about an invented place or person on a slip ("People say the new gym has a swimming pool on the roof."). Mingle: swap slips; each partner reports the slip on air in Pattern A and B. Then 3 volunteers read the best ones. Note errors (to be vs to have been) for the feedback stage.
Lead-in: signs and captions5T–SBoard: "Made in Mexico." · "Products sold here are final sale." · "Passengers traveling with children may board first." Ask: "What words are missing?" (which were / that are / who are). "Why do signs cut them?" (short, clear, space). "Today: fewer words, same meaning."
Quick recap: two kinds of clause7T–SBoard only (no slide). Fast check of B1 knowledge: "The woman who lives next door is a doctor" vs "My sister, who lives next door, is a doctor." Commas? that? How many sisters? Keep it to 7 minutes: they know this.
Reducing clauses12T–SSlides 8–9. The core move: delete the pronoun (+ be) and keep the participle. Build the does / receives test with 4 examples each, then hold up the place picture cards and give reduced-clause clues for learners to guess: "It's a place full of people waiting for trains" (station), "a place filled with stalls selling fruit" (market), "a building located in every town, used for sending packages" (post office). Learners then give one clue each. CCQs. Then "when not to reduce" (the man who called me yesterday).
Drill6T–S → SReduction drill and "do or receive?" (see Drill script). Choral, then individuals.
Participle clauses & danglers12T–S → S–SSlides 10–11. Time, reason, sequence (having + pp), negatives (Not knowing…). Then the dangler: read "Walking home, the rain started" with a puzzled face; let learners laugh and explain. Listening Script 3.2-B: learners hold up ✔ or ✘ cards (or thumbs) for "Dangler or not?"
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1 (Script 3.2-A, the Old Town audio tour: read it once), then Ex 2–5 alone (reduce, combine, fix the dangler, reduce or not?). Pairs check. Board-check the disagreements only.
Freer: Sentence Compression Workshop20S–S → T–SSlide 12. Round 1 (6 min), strips: pairs open the envelope of combine-and-reduce strips and join each set of 2–3 short sentences into one, reading each result aloud. Round 2 (10 min), Editor's Desk: groups of 3 get a wordy draft (a community notice, a museum label, a mini-biography, an apartment ad) of about 100 words and must cut it to 60 words or fewer without losing any fact, using at least 4 participle clauses. The word-counter counts. Round 3 (4 min): each group reads its version aloud; the class votes for the clearest one and checks it for danglers. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SBoard 3 errors (no names): "The books selling here…" · "Having finished the report, the printer broke." · "The man calling me yesterday was angry." And 1–2 range examples ("Surrounded by trees, the park…"). Learners fix them and say why.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 3.2 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below), including Spot the Difference if you didn't have time (it works as a C9 warmer too). Learners tick "I can…".
Total908 + 5 + 7 + 12 + 6 + 12 + 10 + 20 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: reduction. A reduced clause says exactly the same thing as the full clause, with fewer words. Neither is "more correct." The reduced one is tighter and more written; the full one is more relaxed and more spoken.

"The man standing by the door is my uncle." Which man? (The one by the door.) Is he standing there now? (Yes.) Full version? (The man who is standing by the door.) · "The car parked outside has been there all week." Did the car park itself? (No, someone parked it: passive → parked.) Full version? (The car which is / was parked outside.)
Does or receives? "The people ___ outside look cold." (wait: they do it → waiting) · "The paintings ___ in 2004 were found." (steal: they received it → stolen) · "The books ___ here are second-hand." (sell: do the books sell? No, they are sold → sold.)

Meaning: when not to reduce. -ing reduction works for something in progress, a habit or a state (the woman living next door), not for one finished action in the past.

"The man who called me yesterday was angry." Can I say "the man calling me yesterday"? (No: one finished call. Keep who called.) · "Passengers who are traveling with children may board first." Reduce? (Yes: Passengers traveling with children.) · "My sister, who lives in Ohio, is a nurse." Reduce in careful writing? (No: keep the commas and who.)

Meaning: participle clauses for time, reason and sequence.

"Feeling tired, he went to bed." Why did he go to bed? (Because he felt tired.) · "Walking home, I saw an accident." When did I see it? (While I was walking home: same time.) · "Having finished work, she went home." Which happened first? (Finishing work.) Could we say "After finishing work"? (Yes.) · "Not knowing the way, we got lost." Did we know the way? (No.) Where does not go? (At the start.)

Meaning: the subject rule.

"Reading the letter, tears filled her eyes." Who is reading, grammatically? (The tears!) Can tears read? (No: it's a dangler.) How do we fix it? ("Reading the letter, she began to cry." or "While she was reading the letter, tears filled her eyes.")

Form.

Active → -ingwho / which / that + verb → verb-ingThe train that leaves at noon → The train leaving at noon
Passive → ppwho / which / that + be + pp → ppThe bridge which was built in 1890 → The bridge built in 1890
Time / reason-ing clause, comma, main clause (same subject)Driving to work, he heard the news.
Reason (passive)pp clause, comma, main clauseMade of glass, it breaks easily.
SequenceHaving + pp, main clause · After + -ingHaving lost the map, they turned back.
NegativeNot + participleNot having a plan, we improvised.

Pronunciation. A fronted participle clause is its own tone unit: pause at the comma and let the voice rise slightly, then fall at the end of the main clause: "Feeling tired ↗ | he went to bed ↘." The -ed of reduced passives follows the usual rule: /t/ parked, finished · /d/ closed, delivered · /ɪd/ painted, located, surrounded; irregular forms (built, written, stolen, sold) need practice. In "Having finished," having is short and the participle takes the stress: /hævɪŋ ˈfɪnɪʃt/.

Drill script
  1. Cut it: T: "The man who is standing there." → S: "The man standing there." T: "The woman who lives next door." → S: "The woman living next door." T: "The letter which was written by John." → S: "The letter written by John." T: "Products that are made locally." → S: "Products made locally."
  2. Do or receive? (point at the -ing / pp cards): T: "books · sell · here" → S: "books sold here." T: "people · wait · outside" → S: "people waiting outside." T: "a car · park · outside" → S: "a car parked outside." T: "students · take · the test" → S: "students taking the test."
  3. Join it: T: "She didn't feel well. She stayed home." → S: "Not feeling well, she stayed home." T: "They had finished dinner. They went for a walk." → S: "Having finished dinner, they went for a walk."
  4. Dangler doctor: T: "Running for the bus, my bag fell open." → S: "Running for the bus, I dropped my bag." / "As I ran for the bus, my bag fell open."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 gives two short facts about a place in town (real or invented): "The market is near the river. It is open on Sundays." S2 joins them: "Located near the river, the market is open on Sundays." Then all pairs, 1 minute.
Sentence Compression Workshop — how to run it
  • Round 1: strips (pairs, 6 min). Each envelope has 12 strips; each strip holds 2–3 short sentences. Pairs join each set into one sentence with a reduced clause or a participle clause, and say it aloud before they write it. The target sentence is on your answer key (materials, set D); accept any correct version.
  • Round 2: Editor's Desk (groups of 3, 10 min). Each group gets a wordy draft of about 100 words (A: a community center notice, B: a museum label, C: a mini-biography of an invented local baker, D: an apartment ad). The "editor-in-chief" says: 60 words or fewer, no fact lost, at least 4 participle clauses, no danglers. Roles: a writer, a word-counter (counts after each sentence and says "over / under"), a fact-checker (ticks each fact on the draft as it survives). Give the word-counter job to a dominant speaker.
  • Round 3: read-out (4 min). Each group reads its version. Class votes on "clearest," not "shortest." Anyone who hears a dangler says "Dangler!" and the group fixes it.
  • Honesty rule: if a reduced sentence sounds worse, un-reduce it. "Learning when not to reduce is a B2 skill too." Praise a group that explains why they kept one full clause.
  • If time is short, skip Round 1 and go straight to the Editor's Desk. Spot the Difference (A/B scenes, set E) is the spoken extension: A describes a street scene using reduced clauses ("a man wearing a red hat standing at the bus stop"), B finds 5 differences. Use it for early finishers or as the C9 warmer.
ICQs before the workshop
Strips: how many sentences at the end? (One.) · Say it first or write it first? (Say it first.) · Editor's Desk: maximum words? (Sixty.) · Can you delete a fact? (No! Every fact stays.) · How many participle clauses? (At least four.) · Who counts the words? (The word-counter.) · Which version wins: the shortest or the clearest? (The clearest.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on the reduction move only (no fronted participle clauses yet): who is -ing → -ing, which was + pp → pp. Give the does / receives cards to hold during Ex 2. In Round 1, give the learner 6 strips instead of 12 and let them cross out words with a pencil instead of rewriting. In the Editor's Desk, make them the fact-checker (tick the facts) rather than the writer. Target: 4 correct reductions, said aloud.

Stretch (toward C1)

Use participle clauses in the middle and end of sentences: "The storm hit the coast, leaving thousands without power" (result). Use having been + pp: "Having been told the news, she called her sister." In the Editor's Desk, aim for 50 words and justify one clause they chose not to reduce. Early finishers rewrite the "Old Town" script as a relaxed spoken version and compare the registers.

L1 watch-outs
  • -ing for passive meaning: "the books selling here," "a house building in 1900." Very common for all L1s. Always ask the does / receives question.
  • Loose participle clauses (danglers): Spanish and Portuguese use the gerundio / gerúndio more freely, with a different subject ("Llegando a casa, el teléfono sonó"), and French en + participe is sometimes used loosely too. Danglers are also common in native-speaker writing, so present them as a writing check, not a sign of a weak learner.
  • Modifier before the noun: Chinese, Japanese and Korean put the whole describing phrase before the noun ("the at-the-door-standing man"), so learners may avoid post-noun participles. Praise every attempt.
  • Extra pronoun: "The man standing there he is my uncle." Arabic, Hebrew, Persian and some Chinese speakers add a resumptive pronoun. Cross it out.
  • Irregular past participles: "the bridge builded," "the paintings stealed," "the letter wrote by John." Keep an irregular-verb list handy; Spanish and Tagalog speakers may pronounce -ed as an extra syllable (/ˈpɑrkɛd/).
Homework

Online lesson 3.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_08.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, pausing at the commas; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Describe It Concisely" project (6+ sentences, 4 participle clauses). Workbook 3.2: finish the Sentence Compression Workshop (your own 60-word version of a second draft, or of a real notice or label you find), "My new words" and the journal (three sentences of your own reduced, and one honest line: better or not?).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C The report prepared by the committee is on your desk. · Q2 B Sleeping deeply, the alarm didn't wake me. (the dangler) · Q3 (new) B The books sold here are second-hand. Old Town tour (Ex 1): 1 Built · 2 standing · 3 Located · 4 painted · 5 Having climbed · 6 selling · 7 Surrounded.

3.3 · C9

Reporting Verbs & Patterns

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • report the function of what someone said (an offer, a refusal, an apology, a denial, a suggestion) with one precise verb instead of said / told
  • use each verb with its pattern: + to-infinitive (agree, refuse, offer, promise), + object + to-infinitive (advise, warn, remind, persuade), + (that) clause (admit, explain, insist, deny), + -ing (deny, admit, suggest, recommend), + preposition + -ing (apologize for, accuse sb of, insist on, blame sb for)
  • avoid the classic B2 errors: suggested me to…, apologized to be…, accused me to…, reminded to call (no object)
  • keep the backshift inside that-clauses, and use suggest / insist that + base verb for proposals and demands
  • write and read aloud accurate meeting minutes with at least six different reporting verbs
Materials
  • Workbook 3.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Meeting Minutes sheet)
  • Slides 13–18 · Reporting-verb cards (color-coded by pattern) · Meeting role cards (3 meetings × 4 roles) + minutes sheets · Report It! board game (early finishers / C10 warmer) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 3.3-A (the Riverside Apartments residents' meeting) and 3.3-B (dictation)

Left: vague → precise. She said she'd help. → She offered to help. · He said he didn't take it. → He denied taking it. · She said sorry for being late. → She apologized for being late.

Center: five patterns (five colors). ① + to: agree, refuse, offer, promise, threaten · ② + person + to: advise, ask, warn, remind, persuade, invite · ③ + (that): admit, explain, claim, deny, insist, complain · ④ + -ing: deny, admit, suggest, recommend, mention · ⑤ + prep + -ing: apologize for, accuse sb of, insist on, blame sb for, congratulate sb on, warn sb against.

Right: the meeting six. suggested going / suggested (that) we go · insisted on paying / insisted (that) she had paid · denied taking · admitted forgetting / (that) he had forgotten · agreed to help · refused to sign. Trap: suggested me to ✘ · recommended me to ✘.

Stage plan

Warmer: compress it / Spot the Difference7S–SReview C8. Board three pairs of short sentences; pairs join them with participle clauses in 3 minutes ("The café is located near the park. It serves great coffee." → "Located near the park, the café serves great coffee."). Or, if C8 ran out of time, run Spot the Difference (set E) in pairs.
Lead-in: what was she doing?6T–SSlide 13. Two learners read the Dana and Marco dialogue aloud. Show the "vague" report (all said). Ask: "What was Dana doing with her words in line 1?" (apologizing) "…in line 3?" (promising). Show the precise report. "Which report is more useful for someone who wasn't there?"
Patterns: meaning & form18T–S → S–SSlides 14–15. Build the five-pattern table on the board with the color-coded verb cards: learners hear a direct line and decide the function, then the pattern. Spend the most time on the meeting six: suggest, insist, deny, admit, agree, refuse, each with its two possible patterns and the suggest trap. CCQs (below). Pairs then sort a spare set of verb cards into the five columns (2 min).

What do they decide?" Second: learners match each speaker to a verb and complete the model minutes. Then show slide 16: the model minutes. This is the model for the freer task.

Each group gets one meeting: Riverside Apartments (the laundry room), Brightside Café (the new schedule), or Green Street Community Garden (August watering). Each learner has a role card with 3 secret "moves" (e.g., suggest…, refuse to…, admit…). The chair (card A) opens and closes the meeting. 8 minutes to reach a decision. Learners play their card, not themselves. ICQs first.

Then they swap with a group member and check the patterns against the cheat-sheet (tick = correct pattern).
Share4T–SOne learner from each group reads its minutes aloud. Other groups listen for the decision and one precise verb they liked.
