Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 3.3 and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below). Remind learners that Module 3 is in Progress Test 1 (C13).
Total907 + 6 + 18 + 7 + 6 + 10 + 12 + 10 + 4 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: reporting the function. A reporting verb tells the listener what the speaker was doing with the words (offering, refusing, admitting), so we don't repeat the words themselves. It also shows the reporter's interpretation, so choose carefully: claimed suggests you doubt it; admitted suggests it was hard to say.

"I'll carry that for you." Is he giving an order or an offer? (An offer.) → He offered to carry it. · "No, I won't sign." → She… (refused to sign.) Do we need "not"? (No: refuse is already negative.) · "OK, it was my mistake." Was that easy to say? (No.) → He admitted (that) it was his mistake / admitted making the mistake.

Meaning: the meeting six.

suggest: "Why don't we change the day?" Is it an order? (No, an idea.) Who will change it? (We, all of us.) → She suggested changing the day / suggested (that) we change the day. Can I say "suggested us to change"? (No!)
insist: "No, really, I'll pay. I insist." Did he accept a "no"? (No.) → He insisted on paying. · "I paid! I definitely paid." → She insisted (that) she had paid. Is this about a future action or a fact? (A fact: she is sure it's true.)
deny vs refuse: "I didn't take the key." → He denied taking the key. Is it about the past or the future? (The past: he says it didn't happen.) · "I won't give you the key." → He refused to give me the key. Past or future? (Future: he says no to doing it.) Can I say "He denied to give"? (No: deny is about facts; refuse is about actions.)
admit: "Yes, OK, I forgot to order the soap." → He admitted forgetting / admitted (that) he had forgotten to order the soap. · agree: "Fine, I'll do the Saturday shift." → She agreed to do the Saturday shift. Can I say "She was agree"? (No! agree is a verb: she agreed.)

Form.

① + to-infinitiveagree, offer, promise, refuse, threaten, claim, decideShe refused to sign.
② + object + toadvise, ask, tell, warn, remind, encourage, persuade, invite, orderHe reminded us to lock the door.
③ + (that) clauseadmit, explain, claim, deny, insist, complain, promise, agree (backshift)She admitted (that) she had forgotten.
④ + -ingadmit, deny, suggest, recommend, mention, regretHe denied taking the key.
⑤ + prep + -ingapologize for, accuse sb of, insist on, blame sb for, congratulate sb on, warn sb againstHe insisted on paying.
suggest / insist thatfor a proposal or demand: that + subject + base verb (American) · should + base (also OK)She suggested (that) we meet on Friday. · He insisted that we stay.

Pronunciation. The reporting verb carries the meaning, so stress it: "She refused to sign." "He denied taking it." Word stress to check: suggest /səgˈdʒɛst/ (not SUG-gest), insist, admit, refuse (verb; REF-use /ˈrɛfjus/ is the noun "trash"), apologize, recommend. Past -ed: /d/ in apologized, refused, denied, agreed, accused, offered, warned, blamed · /t/ in promised. The ones to drill are the verbs that add a syllable, /ɪd/: suggested, insisted, admitted, recommended, reminded. Learners often swallow that syllable ("He suggest going"), and then the listener hears the present tense.

Drill script
  1. Pattern snap (hold up a verb card): T: "refuse" → S: "refuse to do." T: "suggest" → S: "suggest doing." T: "remind" → S: "remind someone to do." T: "apologize" → S: "apologize for doing." T: "deny" → S: "deny doing." T: "insist" → S: "insist on doing / insist that…" Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Name the act: T (acting): "Sorry, sorry, I'm late!" → S: "She apologized for being late." T: "I didn't eat your sandwich!" → S: "He denied eating my sandwich." T: "Let's order pizza." → S: "She suggested ordering pizza." T: "OK, I'll bring the drinks." → S: "He agreed to bring the drinks." T: "No. I won't work on Sunday." → S: "She refused to work on Sunday." T: "Yes, I broke the printer." → S: "He admitted breaking the printer."
  3. Two ways: T: "He admitted breaking it." → S: "He admitted that he had broken it." T: "She suggested going out." → S: "She suggested that we go out."
  4. Trap check (T says a wrong one, learners shout the fix): T: "She suggested me to go." → S: "She suggested going! / suggested that I go!" T: "He apologized to be late." → S: "He apologized for being late!"
  5. Chain: S1 says a line of direct speech with a clear function; S2 reports it with a precise verb; S3 names the pattern (① to ⑤). Then all pairs, 1 minute.
Meeting Minutes — how to run it
  • Groups of 4 (or 5 with a minute-taker). Each group gets one meeting and four role cards (A = chair, B, C, D). Each card has the situation, the role and 3 moves, each labeled with a function (SUGGEST · REFUSE · ADMIT · DENY · INSIST · AGREE plus one free move). Learners say the move in their own words in the meeting; they must not say the function word ("I suggest…" is fine; "I refuse!" is fine too, but encourage natural lines like "No, I'm sorry, I can't do that").
  • The meeting (8 min + 4 min buffer): the chair opens ("OK, let's start. The problem is…"), makes sure everyone speaks and closes with a decision ("So we agree that…"). The chair's card has a talking-turn rule: nobody speaks twice before everyone has spoken once.
  • The minutes (10 min): everyone writes their own minutes on the sheet (date, present, the problem, what each person did, the decision). No said or told. At least 6 different reporting verbs. The five-pattern cheat-sheet is on the sheet.
  • Peer check: swap minutes; the partner ticks each verb whose pattern is right and circles any suggested me to. Then one reader per group shares.
  • Minute-taker: in a group of 5, give the fifth learner (or a dominant speaker) the minute-taker job during the meeting. They don't speak until the end, when they read the draft and the group corrects it ("No, I didn't refuse! I agreed to do it on Tuesday!"). This correction round is excellent practice.
  • All roles are assigned. Nobody has to share their own opinions. The stakes are small on purpose (a laundry room, a café schedule, watering tomatoes).
ICQs before the meeting
Do you play yourself or the card? (The card.) · How many moves on your card? (Three.) · Can you show your card? (No.) · Who opens and closes the meeting? (The chair, card A.) · Can someone speak twice before everyone has spoken once? (No.) · In the minutes, can you write "said"? (No!) · How many different reporting verbs? (At least six.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on four verbs and two patterns: agree to / refuse to (①) and suggest -ing / deny -ing (④). Give a card with four sentence frames: "___ agreed to ___. · ___ refused to ___. · ___ suggested ___-ing. · ___ denied ___-ing." In the meeting, give the learner card D (the shortest moves) and let them read their moves from the card. For the minutes, target 4 sentences with the frames. Fewer items in Ex 3–4.

Stretch (toward C1)

Report attitude as well as function: claimed (the reporter doubts it), conceded, acknowledged, urged, proposed, objected to -ing. Use the passive infinitive and perfect -ing: "She denied having been told." "He admitted having forgotten." In the minutes, add one sentence with an impersonal passive from C7 ("It was agreed that…") and one participle clause from C8 ("Having discussed the options, the committee decided…"). That's the whole module in one text.

L1 watch-outs
  • "suggested me to" / "recommended me to": French suggérer à qqn de, Spanish sugerir a alguien, Italian, Portuguese, German (jdm vorschlagen) and Russian (predlozhit' komu + infinitive) all allow a person + infinitive. The most common error in this lesson for almost everyone. Write it on the board crossed out and keep it there.
  • deny vs refuse: Spanish negarse a and Portuguese negar-se a mean refuse, while negar means deny, so learners say "He denied to pay." Use the past / future CCQ every time.
  • "I am agree" / "She was agree": Spanish estar de acuerdo, French être d'accord, Portuguese estar de acordo. Drill "I agree / She agreed" as a verb.
  • "explained me the problem": Spanish me explicó, French il m'a expliqué, Italian mi ha spiegato. Teach "explained the problem to me" / "explained (to me) that…". Same for "suggested me."
  • Wrong preposition: "insist in" (Spanish insistir en), "accuse for" / "blame of" (mixing), "apologize to be" (French s'excuser de + infinitive). Teach the verb + preposition as one chunk, and the rule "after a preposition → -ing."
  • No backshift in that-clauses: "He admitted that he forgets." Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Russian and Vietnamese keep the original tense. This is B1 language; recast gently.
Homework

Online lesson 3.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_09.html. Listen to the five model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3, the Quick Quiz and the "Report a Conversation" project (a light, everyday conversation: planning a trip, sharing a bill, a work schedule). Workbook 3.3: finish the Meeting Minutes (a clean final copy with 6+ verbs), "My new words" and the journal (five reporting verbs from five different patterns, one sentence each about a real or invented conversation from your week).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B She warned us not to walk on the ice. · Q2 C He suggested eating out that night. · Q3 (new) A He denied breaking the printer. Residents' meeting (Ex 1): 1 Ms. Park · suggested · 2 Mr. Diaz · denied · 3 Tom · admitted · 4 Mr. Diaz · refused · 5 Ms. Okafor · agreed · 6 Tom · insisted on · 7 Ms. Park · reminded.

Closing the Module

End of C9: Module 3 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "shift, cut, name": It is said that… / He is thought to have… (shift the focus) → the man standing there · Having finished, she… (cut the words) → She refused to sign. He denied taking it. They suggested meeting on Friday. (name the act). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C10 warmer (the Report It! board game is ready for that) and again in the C13 progress-test catch-up.

Watch for in Module 4: linking words in 4.1 (despite, owing to) take -ing after a preposition, just like the pattern-⑤ verbs (despite feeling tired · apologize for being late): make that connection out loud. Cleft sentences in 4.2 ("What she suggested was…") work well with reporting verbs. And the 4.3 review project (present, argue and discuss) is the natural place to recycle impersonal passives ("It is often said that…") in formal openings.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it tests: passives across tenses and modals (including should have been + pp), impersonal passives in both patterns with the right infinitive (to be / to have been), the causative word order, reducing relative clauses (-ing vs past participle), participle clauses for time, reason and sequence, spotting a dangling participle, and reporting verbs with their patterns (especially suggest, deny / refuse, admit, insist on, apologize for, accuse of).

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 4 — Fluency & Style

Three classes that finish the first half of B2 and move learners from correct English to effective English. First comes linking ideas: contrast, cause and result, and above all the grammar that follows each linker (although + clause, despite + noun / -ing, however + new sentence). Then emphasis: it-clefts, what-clefts and other cleft frames, and basic negative inversion (Never have I…, Not only did…, No sooner had…than). Last, a task-based review, Present, Argue & Discuss: learners give a short signposted presentation and take part in a small-group discussion with assigned sides, while you score a speaking checklist that feeds Progress Test 1 (C13).

3 lessons Lessons 4.1–4.3 Classes C10–C12 Weeks 5–6 90 min per class CEFR B2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, discussion) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework. C12 is task-based: the tasks come first and the language work comes out of them.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-4-fluency-style.html. Print materials/module-4/classroom-materials.html before C10. It holds the flashcards, the linker sort cards, the Argument Builder kit (topic cards, point cards, linker play cards, listener tally), the Plain → Dramatic rewrite cards, the Set the Record Straight A/B cards, the emphatic-opinion prompts, the C12 presentation topics, side cards and persona cards, the C12 speaking checklist, the Module 1–4 Review Race board game and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. At B1 (Module 3 and 4.3) learners met because, so, although, however, relative clauses and the basic opinion phrases. They know what most linkers mean. The B2 work in 4.1 is the grammar after the linker (clause, noun / -ing, or a new sentence) and the register and punctuation of connectors. Clefts and negative inversion (4.2) are genuinely new for most learners; keep inversion to the triggers the lesson teaches (Never, Not only, No sooner…than, Hardly / Scarcely…when, Rarely / Seldom, Little, Only then / Only after).
  • Fluency before accuracy. In every freer task, listen and take notes; don't stop a speaker to fix a linker or an inversion. The feedback stage is where you fix errors, without names. At B2, feed back on accuracy and range: one or two "fix it" sentences, and one or two "upgrade it" sentences (a correct but plain sentence that a cleft or a better linker would make stronger). Praise the argument first ("That was really convincing"), then the form.
  • Dominant speakers. Argument tasks reward the loudest voice, so build in turn-taking: talking tokens in the discussions, fixed-order rounds before open rounds, and jobs for strong speakers (the chair who must invite everyone, the listener who keeps the linker tally, the devil's advocate who may speak only after two others). Say it kindly: "Great point, Ivan. Let's hear the other side first."
  • Stretching strong learners toward C1. Don't hand them new C1 grammar. Stretch them with choice and control: vary linkers (no linker twice in one talk), match register to the situation (therefore in the talk, so in the chat), use the rarer triggers the lesson teaches (Seldom, Little did…, Only after…) sparingly and exactly, hedge their claims (arguably, to some extent), summarize the other side fairly before countering, and self-correct in real time.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British forms (emphasise, apologise, realise, practised, organised, programme, cancelled, film, holiday, moved house, full stop). Teach emphasize, apologize, realize, practiced, organized, program, canceled, movie, vacation, moved, period. Accept the British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Connect it → stress it → use it all. In C10 learners stop producing lists of facts and start building arguments: Although remote work saves time, it can feel isolating, so many teams keep an office day. In C11 they learn to put the spotlight exactly where they want it: It was Maria who booked it, not me. What we really need is more time. Not only did the deadline move, but we also lost two people. In C12 everything from Modules 1–4 goes to work in two real tasks: a short signposted presentation and a small-group discussion in which learners concede, counter and persuade. Every class ends with a speaking task where the argument matters as much as the grammar.

Trauma-informed: argue the card, not your life

Opinion tasks can feel risky for adults who have lived where disagreeing in public was dangerous, and some topics touch raw nerves. The online lesson 4.3 suggests broad topics ("technology & society, the environment, education… the media"). In class, keep every topic everyday: work, the neighborhood, shopping, technology at home, school and learning, health habits, free time. No politics, religion, immigration policy, war or national identity; if a learner raises one, thank them and steer back: "Interesting, but let's keep today's topics local." Every opinion task has assigned sides (the side cards and Argument Builder cards), a devil's advocate role and persona cards, so nobody has to reveal what they really think. Say it every time: "Good speakers can argue any side. Today you argue your card." Anyone can say "Pass" on a personal question, and anyone may present to you alone instead of to a group.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (Set A, one set for the board): the linker cards color-coded by what follows them, the emphasis-frame cards and the topic picture cards. Card stock helps. Cut them before C10.
  • For C10: one set of linker sort cards (Set B) per pair, and one Argument Builder kit (Set C) per group of 3: a topic card, its six point cards, a set of 12 linker play cards and a listener tally. An envelope per group keeps the pieces together.
  • For C11: one set of Plain → Dramatic cards (Set D) per pair, one pair of Set the Record Straight A/B cards (Set E) per pair, and the emphatic-opinion prompts (Set F) on a side table.
  • For C12: one Review Race board (Set I) per group of 3–4 with a die and counters; the presentation topic cards, side cards, persona cards and talking tokens (Set G); one speaking checklist class grid for you and one score slip per learner (Set H). A phone timer for each group.
  • Optional: learners' phones for recording their C12 presentation (useful for anyone you don't manage to hear live).

Lesson Plans

4.1 · C10

Linking Ideas — The Argument Builder

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • express contrast and concession with although, though, even though, despite, in spite of, whereas, while, however, nevertheless, on the other hand
  • express cause with because, since, as (+ clause) and because of, due to, owing to (+ noun)
  • express result and purpose with so, so + adjective / adverb + that, such (a / an) + noun + that, therefore, consequently, as a result, so as to, in order to
  • choose the right grammar after each linker (clause, noun / -ing, or a new sentence) and punctuate sentence connectors correctly
  • give a balanced two-minute mini-talk on an everyday trade-off, from an assigned side or a balanced view
Materials
  • Workbook 4.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the Argument Builder planner)
  • Slides 3–8 · Linker flashcards and topic pictures (Set A) · Linker sort cards (Set B, 1 set per pair) · Argument Builder kits (Set C, 1 per group of 3)
  • Listening scripts 4.1-A (Two speakers), 4.1-B (City or countryside? mini-talk) and 4.1-C (dictation)

Left: three columns with headings. + CLAUSE (subject + verb): although · though · even though · whereas · while · because · since · as · so · + NOUN / -ING: despite · in spite of · because of · due to · owing to · NEW SENTENCE + COMMA: However, · Nevertheless, · On the other hand, · Therefore, · Consequently, · As a result, (Stick the Set A linker cards in the right column as you teach them.)

Center: the bridge: Despite the rain ✔ · Despite being wet ✔ · Despite it was raining ✘ → Despite the fact that it was raining ✔ · Although the rain ✘. Under it: so + adj / adv + that (so cold that…) · such + (a / an) + noun + that (such a cold day that…).

Right: punctuation: It was late. However, we stayed. · It was late; however, we stayed. · Although it was late, we stayed. Register ladder: so (chat) → that's why → therefore / consequently (talk, essay).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Report the complaint8S–S → T–SReview C9 (3.3, reporting verbs and patterns). On the board: "Your coworker ___ (deny / admit / suggest / insist on / apologize for / warn) …". Pairs invent a small office drama in three reported sentences ("Sam denied taking the last coffee. Rita insisted on checking the camera. In the end, Sam apologized for lying."). Two pairs share. Note one pattern error for the feedback slot (typically "suggested me to…").
Lead-in: Two speakers6T–SRead Script 4.1-A: the same four facts about remote work, first as a flat list, then linked. "Which speaker was easier to follow? Why?" Elicit that the second speaker told us in advance what kind of idea was coming. Underline although, because, however, as a result on the slide (slide 3). "You already know these words. Today is about the grammar after them."
Contrast: clause, noun or new sentence?14T–S → S–SSlides 4–5. Present the three-column board. Meaning first (contrast vs concession, CCQs below), then form: although + clause, despite / in spite of + noun / -ing, the bridge the fact that, however as a sentence connector, whereas / while for comparing two things. Then pairs do the linker sort (Set B): 18 linker cards go under the right column heading (+ clause / + noun / -ing / new sentence), and the pair says one quick sentence for each card; check against the board.
Drill: switch it6T–S → STransformation drill (see Drill script): although → despite, because → because of, but → however, so → such…that. Choral, then fast individuals. Keep the pace up; the point is automatic grammar after the linker.
Cause, result & register10T–SSlides 6–7. because / since / as + clause vs because of / due to / owing to + noun; so…that vs such…that ("if a noun follows, use such"); therefore / consequently (formal, new sentence) vs so (spoken, same sentence); so as to / in order to + verb for purpose. The register ladder and the punctuation line. CCQs.
Listening: City or countryside?7T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 4.1-B twice. First listen: "Does the speaker prefer the city or the countryside?" (Neither: it depends on your stage of life.) Second listen: learners write the ten missing linkers and label each C (contrast), Ca (cause) or R (result). Pairs compare.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone (choose the linker, join the ideas, so / such, fix the mistake), then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on. Early finishers start the Argument Builder planner.
Freer: The Argument Builder19S–S (groups of 3) → T–SSlide 8. See "How to run it" below. Round 1 (7 min): in each trio, A argues FOR the topic and B argues AGAINST (1 minute each), using the point cards and playing a linker card face up each time they use it correctly; C is the listener with the tally and checks the grammar after each linker. Then C gives a 30-second verdict: "Although A made a good point about…, B convinced me because…". Round 2 (10 min): new topic card; everyone gives a balanced two-minute mini-talk (hook → side 1 → pivot with a contrast linker → side 2 → balanced conclusion) while the others tally. Rotate roles. ICQs first (2 min).
Feedback5T–SDelayed error correction: write 4 sentences you heard (no names): 2 to fix, 2 to upgrade. E.g. "Despite it is cheaper, many people drive." · "It was so a long journey that I slept." · "Because of the rent is high, people share." (fix) · "It's cheap. But it's far." → Although it's cheap, it's far. (upgrade). Praise the most convincing argument first.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: contrast vs concession. Contrast = two things are different (He likes tea, whereas she prefers coffee.). Concession = "Yes, this is true, but the other thing is true anyway" (Although it was raining, we went out.).

"Although it was raining, we went out." Was it raining? (Yes.) Did we go out? (Yes.) Is that surprising? (A little: we expected them to stay in.) · "Even though I warned him, he did it." Stronger or weaker than although? (Stronger: more surprise.)
"He likes tea, whereas she prefers coffee." Is one fact surprising because of the other? (No, we're just comparing two people.) Can I say "Although he likes tea, she prefers coffee"? (It sounds strange: there's no surprise. whereas / while is better.)

Form: what comes after the linker. This is the heart of the lesson.

"Despite ___, we went out." Can I put "it was raining" there? (No: despite takes a noun or -ing.) What can I put? (the rain · the bad weather · being tired.) And if I really want the full sentence? (Despite the fact that it was raining… or change to Although.) · "Although ___, we went out." "the rain"? (No: although needs subject + verb.)
"It was late. However, we stayed." How many sentences? (Two.) What's after However? (A comma.) Can I say "However it was late, we stayed"? (No, that's although's job.)
"We were late because of the traffic." "…because the traffic was terrible." Same meaning? (Yes.) Same grammar? (No: because of + noun; because + clause.) "The match was canceled due to rain." Formal or informal? (More formal; common in announcements and writing.)
"It was so cold that the lake froze." "It was such a cold day that we stayed in." What comes after so? (An adjective: cold.) After such? (a / an + noun: a cold day.) "They were such kind people." Why no a? (Plural noun.) · "She left early so as to catch the train." Why did she leave early? (To catch the train: purpose.)
+ clausealthough · though · even though · whereas · while · because · since · as · so · so + adj / adv + that · such + (a / an) + noun + thatAlthough he had trained for months, he didn't finish the race.
+ noun / -ingdespite · in spite of · because of · due to · owing toDespite the heavy rain, the concert went ahead. · In spite of being tired, she finished.
the bridgedespite / in spite of + the fact that + clauseDespite the fact that it was raining, we went out.
new sentenceHowever, · Nevertheless, · On the other hand, · Therefore, · Consequently, · As a result, (after a period or a semicolon)Sales fell sharply; therefore, the launch was postponed.
purposeso as to / in order to + base verbShe left early in order to catch the train.

Pronunciation. In speech, a sentence connector is its own small chunk with a pause after it: "It was expensive. | However, | we bought it." Stress: alTHOUGH, deSPITE, howEVer, THEREfore, CONsequently, WHEREas. Though at the end of a sentence is light and falling: "We bought it, though ↘." Due to /ˈdu tə/ (American) — accept /ˈdjuː/. In so…that and such…that, the stress goes on so / such and the adjective, and that is weak /ðət/: "It was SO COLD that we stayed in." Teach the "pivot" intonation for the mini-talk: a fall at the end of side 1, a pause, then a fresh, higher start on the contrast linker.

Drill script
  1. Although → despite (T gives a clause, S gives the noun version): T: "Although it was raining…" → S: "Despite the rain…" T: "Although she was tired…" → S: "Despite being tired… / Despite her tiredness…" T: "Although the tickets were expensive…" → S: "Despite the expensive tickets… / Despite the price of the tickets…"
  2. Because → because of / due to: T: "because the traffic was bad" → S: "because of the bad traffic." T: "because it was foggy" → S: "due to the fog." T: "because the rent is high" → S: "because of the high rent."
  3. But → However (two sentences, say the pause): T: "It's cheap, but it's far." → S: "It's cheap. | However, | it's far."
  4. So or such? T: "cold" → S: "so cold that…" T: "a cold day" → S: "such a cold day that…" T: "friendly people" → S: "such friendly people that…" T: "quickly" → S: "so quickly that…"
  5. Chain the argument (open pairs → closed pairs): S1 says a plain fact ("Online shopping is convenient."); S2 adds a contrast ("…whereas shopping in person is more fun."); S3 adds a cause ("…because you can touch things."); S4 adds a result ("…so I still go to the market."). Then all trios, 1 minute.
The Argument Builder — how to run it
  • Groups of 3, one kit per group (Set C). Each kit has a topic card (e.g., Working from home is better than working in an office.), 6 point cards (3 FOR, 3 AGAINST, written as short notes, not sentences: "no commute → more free time"), 12 linker play cards and a listener tally.
  • Round 1 — assigned sides. A takes the three FOR cards, B the three AGAINST cards. Each speaker holds 4 linker cards (dealt at random). When a speaker uses a linker with the right grammar after it, they put the card down. C (the listener) says "✔" or "Check the grammar!" and ticks the tally. Most cards played wins a point, but C's verdict ("…convinced me more, because…") is the real prize.
  • Round 2 — the balanced mini-talk. Draw a new topic. Each learner plans for 1 minute with the point cards, then speaks for up to 2 minutes following the shape on slide 8: hook → side 1 → pivot (contrast linker) → side 2 → balanced conclusion (with a result linker). Listeners tally linkers and write one "best sentence" they heard.
  • Assigned sides make it safe. Nobody argues their real opinion in Round 1, and the Round 2 talk is balanced by design. Topics are everyday trade-offs only (work, city and country, phones, shopping, school, transportation).
  • Dominant speakers get the listener role first: they have to stay quiet for two minutes and judge fairly. Quieter learners speak second in Round 1 (they hear a model first).
  • Walk around with a notepad. Write down 2 sentences to fix and 2 to upgrade for the feedback stage. Don't correct during the talks.
ICQs before the Argument Builder
Round 1: Do you give your real opinion? (No, you argue your side's cards.) · When do you put a linker card down? (When I use it with the right grammar.) · What does the listener do? (Checks the grammar, ticks the tally, gives a verdict.) · Round 2: How long is the talk? (Up to two minutes.) · How many sides? (Both.) · Where does the contrast linker go? (In the middle: the pivot.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with one linker from each column only: although (+ clause), because of (+ noun), However, (new sentence), plus so. Give a sentence frame strip: "Although ___, ___. · ___ because of ___. · ___. However, ___. · ___, so ___." The point cards have an arrow picture ("no commute → free time"), so learners can speak from the picture. In the Argument Builder, the learner is the listener in Round 1 (they hear two models) and gives a one-minute talk in Round 2 with the frame strip. Target: 4 linked sentences with the right grammar.

Stretch

No linker twice in one talk. Use the more formal set (nevertheless, owing to, consequently, whereas) in Round 2 and the informal set (though at the end, that's why, so) in Round 1, and notice the difference in tone. Use despite / in spite of + -ing and + the fact that in the same talk. In Workbook Ex 3, write each answer two ways. For homework, write the Round 2 talk as a 120-word paragraph with correct connector punctuation.

L1 watch-outs
  • "Although…, but…" and "Because…, so…": Chinese (suīrán… dànshì, yīnwèi… suǒyǐ), Vietnamese (tuy… nhưng), Korean, Thai and Indonesian speakers often use both halves of a pair. In English, one linker is enough: Although it was cheap, but I didn't buy it.
  • "Despite of": Spanish a pesar de, Portuguese apesar de and Italian nonostante + the English in spite of mix into "despite of." Say: "in spite of, but despite alone."
  • "Despite + clause": learners whose L1 linker takes a full clause (French bien que, Spanish aunque, Arabic raghma anna) put one after despite. Point at the middle column every time.
  • "However" as a clause linker / comma splice: Spanish sin embargo, Portuguese no entanto and many other L1s allow "…, however, …" joining two clauses with commas. In writing, however needs a period or semicolon before it.
  • "so a good movie" / "such good": Spanish tan, French si / tellement, Portuguese tão and Russian takoy / tak map onto so and such differently. The quick test: a noun follows → such.
  • Long "and" chains: Arabic (wa) and Tagalog (at) storytelling style links many clauses with "and." Praise the fluency, then offer one upgrade: "and" → whereas / so / as a result.
  • "since" = time or reason? German seit, Spanish desde and French depuis are time only. Show both: "Since 2020…" (time) · "Since you're here, let's start." (reason).
Homework

Online lesson 4.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_10.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Record your Round 2 mini-talk on your phone (or a new topic) and listen back: did despite take a noun? Did however start a new sentence? Workbook 4.1: finish the Argument Builder planner, "My new words" and the journal (a short opinion paragraph with one contrast, one cause and one result linker, each labeled clause / noun / new sentence).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B Despite the delay, we caught the train. · Q2 C such an … that · Q3 (new) A The shop was losing money; therefore, it closed. Mini-talk (Ex 1, Script 4.1-B): 1 because (Ca) · 2 since (Ca) · 3 However (C) · 4 so (R, so high that) · 5 whereas (C) · 6 Despite (C) · 7 due to (Ca) · 8 such (R, such a peaceful place that) · 9 Even though (C) · 10 Therefore (R).

4.2 · C11

Adding Emphasis — Make It Emphatic

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • spotlight a person, thing, time or place with an it-cleft (It was Maria who booked it. · It was on Monday that I called.), especially to correct a wrong assumption
  • build up to a key idea with a what-cleft (What I need is a vacation. · What he did was (to) apologize.)
  • use other cleft frames: The thing (that)… is · The reason (why)… is · The place where… is · All (that) I want is…
  • use negative inversion with question word order after the triggers the lesson teaches (Never, Not only, No sooner…than, Hardly / Scarcely…when, Rarely / Seldom, Little, Only then / Only after), and know that it is formal and dramatic
  • give a 60–90 second emphatic opinion with at least two clefts and, optionally, one inversion
Materials
  • Workbook 4.2 (New phrases, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Make It Emphatic planner)
  • Slides 9–13 · Emphasis-frame flashcards (Set A) · Plain → Dramatic cards (Set D, 1 set per pair) · Set the Record Straight A/B cards (Set E, 1 pair per pair) · Emphatic-opinion prompts (Set F) · Linker sort cards from C10 (warmer)
  • Listening scripts 4.2-A (The team lunch) and 4.2-B (dictation)

Left: the plain sentence John broke the window yesterday. and three spotlights: It was John who broke the window yesterday. (…not Sam) · It was the window that John broke. (…not the door) · It was yesterday that John broke the window. (…not last week). Frame: It + be + [SPOTLIGHT] + who / that + the rest.

Center: the build-up: What I need is → a vacation. · What annoys me is → the noise. · What he did was → (to) apologize. Cousins: The thing that… is · The reason… is · The place where… is · All I want is… Arrow: "the important part comes LAST."

Right: the flip: I have never seen such a mess. → Never have I seen such a mess. Trigger + auxiliary + subject (question order!) · no auxiliary? add do / does / did: Rarely does she complain. Partners: No sooner… than · Hardly… when. Label: ⚠️ formal / dramatic: use like a spice.

Warmer: Linker tennis8S–SReview C10. Pairs, one set of linker sort cards (Set B) face down. A says a clause ("I love my job"); B turns a linker card and must continue with the right grammar ("…even though the pay is low" / "Despite the low pay…"). Then swap. 1 point per correct return. Listen for despite + clause and however without a new sentence; save one for feedback.
Lead-in: "So you booked the hotel?"5T–SAct out a mistake: "So, Ana, I heard you broke the printer yesterday!" Ana (primed, or you play both roles): "No! It was Luis who broke it!" Write the reply. "Why not just say 'Luis broke it'?" Elicit: it corrects the wrong idea and puts a spotlight on Luis. Slide 9.
It-clefts10T–SSlide 9. The John / window / yesterday board. Meaning (spotlight + implied contrast), form (It + be + X + who / that; be matches the time: It is… / It was…), and the key pronunciation point: the spotlight word carries the main stress. CCQs.
What-clefts & other frames10T–S → S–SSlide 10. What + clause + be + key idea; the "save the best for last" idea; actions with What he did was (to) + verb; the cousins The thing that…, The reason…, All I want…. CCQs. Quick pairs: A says a plain complaint ("The noise annoys me."), B says it as a what-cleft and then as a frame.
Drill: spotlight it6T–S → SSee Drill script: moving spotlight, what-cleft build-up, stress. Choral, then individual.
Negative inversion12T–S → SSlide 11. Start from a question: "Have I ever seen such a mess?" → "Never have I seen such a mess." Present the trigger table (the lesson's list only), the partner words (No sooner…than, Hardly…when), past perfect for the earlier action, and do / does / did when there's no auxiliary. Register warning: formal, dramatic, for speeches, stories and a strong close. Drill steps 4–5.

A states the wrong "facts" ("So Sam organized the party, right?"); B corrects each one with an it-cleft and adds the truth ("Actually, it was Nadia who organized it. Total908 + 5 + 10 + 10 + 6 + 12 + 6 + 9 + 14 + 5 + 5
Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the it-cleft spotlights one element and suggests a contrast.

"It was Maria who booked the hotel." Who booked it? (Maria.) Did the listener think someone else booked it? (Probably, yes: maybe me.) · "It was on Friday that she sent the report." What's the important information? (Friday.) What might the listener have thought? (Another day, like Thursday.)
"It is her decision that matters." Is this about the past? (No, now: is.) · "It was the noise that woke me." Why that, not who? (The noise is a thing.) · "It was my sister who / that paid." Both OK? (Yes: for people, who or that.)

Meaning: the what-cleft delays the key idea to the end, where English puts its weight.

"What I need is a vacation." What's the most important word? (vacation.) Where is it? (At the end.) · "What he did was ignore me." Is the focus a thing or an action? (An action: ignoring.) Can I say "What he did was to ignore me"? (Yes, both are fine.) · "All I want is a quiet weekend." Do I want a lot? (No: only that; nothing more.)

Meaning: negative inversion = drama and formality.

"Never have I seen such a mess." Is it a question? (No.) Why does it look like one? (After Never at the start, we use question word order.) Would you say this to a friend about their kitchen? (Only as a joke: it's dramatic.) Where is it natural? (Speeches, stories, formal writing, a strong ending.)
"No sooner had we sat down than the alarm went off." Which happened first? (We sat down.) How long between them? (Almost no time.) Why had sat? (Past perfect for the earlier action.) Can I say "…when the alarm went off"? (No: No sooner… than; Hardly… when.) · "Little did they know what was coming." Did they know? (No, not at all.)
it-cleftIt + be + spotlight + who / that + restIt was your support that got me through the hardest weeks.
what-cleftWhat + clause + be + key ideaWhat I really need right now is a proper night's sleep.
actionWhat + subject + did + was (to) + base verbWhat she did was (to) call the manager.
framesThe thing (that)… is · The reason (why)… is · The place where… is · All (that)… isThe thing that annoys me most is being interrupted.
inversiontrigger + auxiliary + subject + main verb (add do / does / did if needed)Not only did she apologize, but she also offered to fix it.
partnersNo sooner + had + subject + p.p. … than · Hardly / Scarcely + had … whenHardly had I arrived when it began to rain.

Pronunciation. Clefts only work if the stress lands on the spotlight: "It was MAria who booked it" (low, quick "who booked it" after the peak). In what-clefts, the first part rises and hangs, then there's a small pause and the key idea falls: "What I need ↗ | is a vaCAtion ↘." In inversions, stress the trigger and the main verb, and keep the auxiliary weak: "NEVer have I SEEN such a mess." Not only takes a strong stress on only, and but also is said as one chunk. Model a slightly slower, deliberate pace: emphasis is partly about timing.

Drill script
  1. Moving spotlight (hold up the sentence strip My colleague sent the report on Friday; point at one part): T points at my colleague → S: "It was my colleague who sent the report on Friday." T points at the report → S: "It was the report that my colleague sent on Friday." T points at on Friday → S: "It was on Friday that my colleague sent the report." Stress the spotlight.
  2. Correct me: T makes a wrong statement; S corrects with a cleft. T: "You broke the vase!" → S: "No, it was the cat that broke it." T: "You called on Tuesday." → S: "No, it was on Monday that I called."
  3. Build it up (what-clefts): T: "I need a vacation." → S: "What I need is a vacation." T: "The traffic frustrates me." → S: "What frustrates me is the traffic." T: "He just apologized." → S: "What he did was apologize." Say the rise-pause-fall together.
  4. Question first, then flip: T: "I have never eaten such good food." → S (question): "Have I ever eaten…?" → S: "Never have I eaten such good food." T: "She rarely complains." → S: "Does she complain?" → "Rarely does she complain."
  5. Two-part triggers: T: "She won. She also broke the record." → S: "Not only did she win, but she also broke the record." T: "We had just sat down. Then the alarm went off." → S: "No sooner had we sat down than the alarm went off." Then open pairs with the Plain → Dramatic cards, 1 minute.
Make It Emphatic — how to run it
  • Part 1 · Set the Record Straight. Pairs get matching A/B cards (Set E). A reads three wrong "facts" as questions with a confident face ("So Sam organized the party, right?"). B looks at the true facts and corrects each one with an it-cleft, then adds a what-cleft about what really happened ("Actually, it was Nadia who organized it. What Sam did was bring the music."). Swap to the second pair of cards. The scenarios are invented office, neighborhood and family-trip situations; nobody talks about their real life.
  • Part 2 · Plain → Dramatic. Each learner draws a card with a plain sentence and a hint (e.g., "We had just arrived when the power went out." → No sooner…). They say two versions aloud: the one on the hint, and a cleft of their choice. The partner says "✔" or "Try the question first."
  • Part 2 · The emphatic opinion. Trios. Each learner takes a Set F prompt (e.g., Something about public transportation that should change) or their own everyday topic, plans for 1 minute, and speaks for 60–90 seconds: open with a frame ("The thing that really bothers me is…"), give a reason, correct a common view with an it-cleft ("People think it's the price, but it's the waiting that…"), and close with a what-cleft or one inversion ("Never have I…").
  • Keep inversion honest. One inversion per talk is plenty. If a learner stacks three, praise the control and ask: "Which one is the strongest? Keep that one." That is a real B2/C1 skill: choosing.
ICQs before Make It Emphatic
Part 1: Who has the true facts, A or B? (B.) What structure does B use to correct? (It was… who / that…) · Part 2: How many versions do you say for each card? (Two.) · How long is the opinion? (60 to 90 seconds.) · How many inversions must you use? (Zero or one: it's optional.) · What do listeners do when they hear a cleft? (Raise a hand, silently.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on two frames: "It was ___ who / that ___." and "What I need / want / like is ___." plus one inversion chunk learned by heart: "Never have I seen…". Give a frame strip and color the spotlight part. In Set the Record Straight, the learner plays B with the facts already written as clefts on the back of the card (read, then say without looking). Target: three correct it-clefts and one what-cleft, spoken with stress on the spotlight.

Stretch

Use the rarer triggers the lesson teaches, correctly and sparingly: Seldom…, Scarcely…when, Little did…, Only after…did. Rewrite one Plain → Dramatic card in three ways and explain the difference in focus. In the emphatic opinion, use The reason… is that…, an it-cleft that corrects a common view, and All I'm asking is…. Write the talk as a short speech for homework and mark where your voice goes up and pauses.

L1 watch-outs
  • "Never I have seen…": in Spanish (Nunca he visto), Portuguese, Italian and Arabic the subject can disappear or stay after the adverb without inversion, so learners keep statement order. Always go through the question first: "Have I ever seen…? → Never have I seen…"
  • Over-inversion from German / Dutch (verb-second rule): learners may invert after any fronted adverb ("Yesterday went I…", "Often do I go…"). English inverts only after the negative / limiting triggers.
  • Topic-fronting instead of a cleft: Chinese, Japanese, Korean and Vietnamese speakers often front the topic ("The window, John broke it."). Accept it in speech and show the English cleft.
  • "What I need it is…" (an extra pronoun): Arabic, Persian and some Romance-language speakers repeat the subject. Rule: no it between the what-clause and is.
  • Tense of be in the cleft: French c'est… qui and Spanish es… quien are often present even about the past, giving "It is John who broke it yesterday." Match be to the time: It was.
  • "No sooner… when" / "Hardly… than": many learners from all L1s swap the partner words. Teach them as fixed pairs on one card each.
  • Stress: speakers of syllable-timed languages (Spanish, French, Tagalog, Turkish, Cantonese) may give every word the same weight, which flattens the cleft. Exaggerate the peak in the drill and let learners overdo it at first.
Homework

Online lesson 4.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_11.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and copy the stress; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 4.2: finish the Make It Emphatic planner, "My new words" and the journal (one true, plain sentence from your week, rewritten four ways: an it-cleft on the person, an it-cleft on the time, a what-cleft and an inversion). For C12: choose a presentation topic (from the Set G cards or your own everyday topic) and jot down three points.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C It was the cat that broke the vase. · Q2 B Never have I felt so proud. · Q3 (new) A What I need is a quiet weekend. The team lunch (Ex 1, Script 4.2-A): 1 Tomás · 2 on Monday · 3 the service · 4 the price · 5 Never have I seen… · Not only was it… · No sooner had I sat down than….

4.3 · C12

Review Project — Present, Argue & Discuss

90 min
A task-based review, not a new-grammar class

There is no new grammar today. The new language is functional: signposting a talk, building a case, and the discussion moves (concede, then counter; persuade; hedge). The class is built around two real tasks: a two-minute signposted presentation given to a small group, followed by questions, and a small-group discussion in which learners argue assigned sides. Learners plan, rehearse, then perform while you listen with the speaking checklist (Set H). Language work comes from the tasks: a short focus-on-form "clinic" between them, not a presentation. The checklist results feed C13 (Progress Test 1): they are your spoken evidence for Modules 1–4, and they tell you who goes to which catch-up station.

Objectives — learners can…
  • structure a short presentation: open and state the topic, preview the structure, move between points, conclude (I'd like to talk about… · I'll cover three points… · Moving on to… · To sum up…)
  • build a case with reasons, ranking, balance, evidence and hedging (One key advantage is… · On the one hand… on the other hand… · The evidence suggests… · It could be argued that… · arguably)
  • answer a challenge by conceding, then countering (That's a fair point, but… · I take your point; however,… · Granted, but we shouldn't forget that…)
  • take part in a 12-minute group discussion from an assigned side, responding to the previous speaker and inviting others
  • use a range of B2 structures from Modules 1–4 in extended, fluent speech
Materials
  • Workbook 4.3 (phrase banks, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, the talk planner, the discussion card, the Module 4 "I can" checklist)
  • Slides 14–18 · Review Race board game (Set I) + dice + counters · Presentation topic cards, side cards, persona cards, talking tokens (Set G) · Speaking checklist: one class grid + score slips (Set H) · a phone timer per group
  • Listening script 4.3-A (the model presentation and discussion: the four-day week) · optional: phones for recording

Left: the talk map: ① OPEN I'd like to talk about… / I'd like to argue that… → ② PREVIEW I'll cover three points… → ③ POINTS Firstly… · Moving on to… · Turning now to… · Finally… (each point = claim + reason + example / evidence) → ④ CLOSE To sum up… / So, to bring it all together…

Center: 🤝 concede → counter: That's a fair point, but… · I take your point; however,… · You're right to an extent, though… · Granted, but we shouldn't forget that… 🎯 persuade (save for your best point): The fact is… · What we can't ignore is… · The bottom line is… 🌫️ hedge: arguably · to some extent · It seems to me that… · up to a point

Right: the B2 toolkit, one line per module: M1 had done / had been doing · If I had…, I would have… / would now… · I wish I had… M2 can't afford to… / stop -ing · must have / can't have · be used to -ing M3 It is widely believed that… · Having considered… · Critics have argued that… M4 Despite this, … · Consequently, … · What really matters is… · Not only… but also…

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Review Race10S–S (groups of 3–4)Review C1–C11 with the Review Race board game (Set I). Roll, move, answer the square in full sentences. The group says "Good!" or helps. Stop after 10 minutes; the player nearest the finish wins. Walk around and note which squares (which modules) are hard: early evidence for C13.
Presenting & discussion language12T–S → S–SSlides 14–15. Elicit "how a good talk sounds." Present the talk map (signposts) and the concede → counter / persuade / hedge banks. CCQs. Drill with intonation (see notes). Quick pairs: T reads a claim ("Cash will disappear in ten years."); A concedes and counters, B hedges. Swap. Workbook Ex 1 fast.
Model: listen7T–SRead Script 4.3-A twice (the presentation, then the two questions; ask a confident learner to read Dana and Leo). First listen: "What's the proposal? How many points? What objection comes up?" Second listen: Workbook Ex 2: number the signposts in order and tick the B2 structures you hear.
Plan the talk10SSlide 16. Learners fill the talk planner (Workbook 4.3): notes, not a script. A topic from the Set G cards (drawn or chosen) or their own everyday topic from the C11 homework. Each point = claim + reason + example. Plus two likely objections and a concede-and-counter answer to each. Check that each planner has at least one M1–M3 structure and one M4 linker or cleft. Early finishers rehearse under their breath with a timer.
Presentation circles + checklist16S–S (groups of 4) · T–SGroups of 4. Each speaker presents for up to 2 minutes (planner turned over; a quick look is OK), then takes 1–2 questions or objections (1 minute) and answers by conceding and countering. Listeners each ask or challenge using a question starter (Set G). A timekeeper in each group. You sit with one group at a time and score criteria 1, 2 and 5 of the speaking checklist.
Focus-on-form clinic5T–SWrite 4 sentences you heard (no names): 2 to fix, 2 to upgrade (e.g., "It is believe that…" · "Not only it saves money…" → fix; "Cars are bad. Also they are expensive." → Not only are cars bad for the air, but they're also expensive. → upgrade). Learners fix them in pairs, then we check. Keep it short and positive.
Discussion prep5S (then S–S)Slide 17. New groups of 4. Each group takes one discussion question (from the presentation topic cards). Deal the side cards face down: For, Against, Devil's advocate (argues whichever side is losing) and Chair (manages turns, speaks too, sums up). Optional persona cards. Each learner fills the discussion card in the workbook: 2 points for their side + 1 concession they're ready to make.
Group discussion + checklist15S–S (groups) · T–SRound 1 (4 min, fixed order): each speaker opens their side in 45 seconds. Round 2 (8 min, open, talking tokens): every turn starts by responding to the last speaker (concede, then counter). Round 3 (2 min): the chair sums up both sides fairly: "On the one hand… on the other hand… Most of us felt…". You sit with one group at a time and score criteria 3 and 4 (and finish 1, 2 and 5 for learners you didn't hear in the presentations).
Feedback + module wrap5T–SContent first: "Who changed your mind, even a little? Which argument was the strongest?" Then 2–3 sentences from your checklist notes, no names. Learners tick the Module 4 "I can…" lists. Celebrate: halfway through B2.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 4.3; record the full 3–4 minute version of your talk at home; the journal. Tell learners plainly about C13: "Next class is a friendly check of Modules 1–4. It helps me help you." Hand out score slips in C13, not today.
Total9010 + 12 + 7 + 10 + 16 + 5 + 5 + 15 + 5 + 5
Hearing everyone

In 31 minutes of tasks you can score about 8–10 learners well. Choose one of these for the rest, and tell the class which one before the presentations: (a) learners record their presentation on a phone during the circles and play it for you during the C13 catch-up time; (b) the rest present to you in the C13 catch-up stations; (c) in a large class, score the presentations in C12 and the discussion skills in C13 (a 5-minute three-person discussion at your station). Anyone who prefers to present only to you, not a group, may do so. That is a normal choice, not a problem.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation (review focus)

Meaning: signposts are a map for the listener (and for you).

"I'll cover three points: the cost, the benefits and one objection." What does the listener know now? (How many parts, and what they are.) Who else does it help? (The speaker: it's a plan.) · "Moving on to…" Is the speaker starting, changing or finishing? (Changing to the next point.) · "To sum up…" How much longer will the talk be? (Very little: it's the end.)

Meaning: concede, then counter.

"That's a fair point, but the evidence suggests they still emit less overall." Does the speaker agree completely? (No.) Do they agree at all? (Yes, partly.) Why start with "That's a fair point"? (It shows they listened; it keeps it polite; it makes their own view sound reasonable.) · "No, you're wrong." Same effect? (No: it sounds combative, and the discussion stops.)
"Arguably, the office is still important." How sure is the speaker? (Quite sure, but not claiming it's a fact.) Is hedging weak? (No, in formal English it sounds careful and credible.) · "The bottom line is, a rested team costs less." Hedge or persuade? (Persuade: their strongest point.) How often should you use it? (Once or twice, or it loses force.)

Form: the review table.

SignpostI'd like to talk about… · Today I'll cover three points… · Let me start by… · Firstly, · Moving on to… · Turning now to… · This brings me to… · Finally, · To sum up, · In conclusion,Let me start by looking at the cost.
Build a caseThere are several reasons for this. · One key advantage is… · A further point is that… · The evidence suggests… · It could be argued that… · The main drawback is…Perhaps the most important factor is time.
Concede → counterThat's a fair point, but… · I take your point; however,… · You're right to an extent, though… · I see where you're coming from, yet… · Granted, but we shouldn't forget that… · There's some truth in that; even so,…Granted, but we shouldn't forget the cost of overtime.
Persuade / hedgeSurely you'd agree that… · The fact is,… · What we can't ignore is… · The bottom line is… / It seems to me that… · to some extent · arguably · up to a point · as far as I can tellWhat we can't ignore is how tired people are.
Module 1narrative tenses · third and mixed conditionals · wish / if only / would rather / it's timeIf we had started sooner, we wouldn't be so far behind now.
Module 2gerunds & infinitives · past deduction (must / might / can't have) · used to / be used to / get used toPeople are used to working from home now.
Module 3advanced & impersonal passive · relative & participle clauses · reporting verbsIt is widely believed that… · Having looked at the data, I… · Critics have argued that…
Module 4contrast / cause / result linkers · clefts · negative inversionDespite this, support has grown. · What really matters is the long term.

Pronunciation. Signposts are chunks followed by a clear pause: "Moving on to | the cost." Slow down for the preview and the conclusion; speed up a little in the examples. The concede-and-counter move has two tunes: a warm, falling "That's a fair point ↘" and then a fresh, higher start on "but…" / "however…". Hedges are said lightly and quickly ("arguably" /ˈɑrɡjuəbli/), the persuade phrases slowly and with weight ("The BOTtom line IS…"). Remind learners of C11: the spotlight word in a cleft carries the stress.

Drill script
  1. Signpost chain: T: "Topic: city bikes." → S1: "I'd like to talk about city bikes." → S2: "I'll cover three points: …" → S3: "Firstly, …" → S4: "Moving on to…" → S5: "To sum up, …" Round the room; say the pause after each signpost.
  2. Concede → counter: T reads a claim ("Online classes are just as good as real ones."); learners answer only with the frame and one reason: "That's a fair point, but you can't practice speaking as much." Rotate the frames: I take your point; however… · Granted, but… · You're right to an extent, though…
  3. Hedge it / push it: T: "Cars are the main problem in cities." → S (hedge): "Arguably, cars are one of the main problems." → S (persuade): "The fact is, cars are the main problem."
  4. Upgrade with a module: T says a plain sentence and names a module; S upgrades it. T: "People think the plan is too expensive. M3." → S: "It is widely believed that the plan is too expensive." T: "We didn't plan, so we're late now. M1." → S: "If we had planned, we wouldn't be late now." T: "This matters: the long term. M4." → S: "What really matters is the long term."
Presentations and the discussion — how to run them
  • Two minutes, not four. The online project asks for 3–4 minutes; in class, two minutes per speaker keeps the circles moving and still shows structure. The full version is homework (a phone recording).
  • Topics. Presentation topics are everyday proposals and trade-offs (the four-day week, phones at the dinner table, cashless shops, homework for children, self-checkout machines, learning online vs in person). A learner's own topic is welcome if it stays everyday. The presenter may argue either side of their topic; the side they choose is not their personal opinion unless they want it to be.
  • Questions after each talk. Every listener has a question starter (Set G: "What about people who…?" · "Isn't there a risk that…?" · "How would that work for…?"). The presenter answers at least one by conceding, then countering. The timekeeper stops the Q&A after one minute, kindly.
  • Side cards make it safe. In the discussion, learners argue the card's side (they may swap inside the group if a topic feels personal). The devil's advocate argues whichever side is losing and speaks third in Round 1. The chair keeps time, invites quiet speakers ("Mei, where do you stand on this?"), stops long turns ("Thanks, Pavel. Let's hear the other side.") and sums up fairly. Give the chair card to a dominant speaker: the job needs listening.
  • Talking tokens in Round 2: 3 each; put one in the middle every time you speak. No tokens left? Listen until everyone has used theirs, then everyone gets 3 more.
  • Persona cards (a night-shift nurse, a café owner, a college student, a parent of three, a retired bus driver, a delivery driver) give learners a point of view that isn't theirs: "As a café owner, I'd have to say…". Great for quieter learners and for anyone who doesn't want to speak for themselves.
  • If a group gets heated, the chair (or you) says: "Great energy. Let's hear one reason from each side and sum up." Push the idea, never the person.
The speaking checklist — how to score it (feeds C13)

Five criteria, 0–3 points each, /15: 0 = no response · 1 = not yet B2 · 2 = B2 · 3 = strong B2 (moving toward C1). Score the whole performance across the presentation, the Q&A and the discussion. Tick the module boxes (M1–M4) when you hear a structure from that module used correctly. The full grid and descriptors are in the classroom materials (Set H).

CriterionWhat 3 points sounds like
1 · Task & organizationPresentation: clear opening and preview, 2–3 points each with a reason and an example or evidence, a real conclusion; signposts guide the listener. Discussion: contributions are relevant and developed, not one-liners.
2 · Range (Modules 1–4)Structures from at least 3 of the 4 modules, used naturally and without prompting; varied linkers; at least one well-placed cleft or inversion.
3 · AccuracyGood control of the B2 grammar; errors are occasional, rarely cause misunderstanding, and are often self-corrected.
4 · Interaction & discussion movesConcedes before countering; hedges claims appropriately; responds directly to the previous speaker; invites others; answers challenges calmly.
5 · Fluency & pronunciationKeeps going at an even pace with natural pauses at signposts; stress highlights key and contrasting information; easy to follow throughout.

Feeding C13. Record each learner's scores and module ticks on the class grid. In C13, send learners to a catch-up station for any module with no tick and to the station for their lowest criterion: no M1 tick → narrative & hypotheticals station (past perfect, third / mixed conditionals, wish); no M2 tick → verb patterns & modality station (gerund / infinitive, must have / can't have, be / get used to); no M3 tick → passive, clauses & reporting station; no M4 tick → linkers & emphasis station (Sets B and D of this pack); criterion 4 ≤ 1 → discussion-moves station (Workbook 4.3 Ex 1 and 5, a 3-minute concede-and-counter round); criterion 5 ≤ 1 → shadowing station (the 🔊 model sentences of lessons 4.1–4.3). If Progress Test 1 has its own speaking task, keep this grid anyway: it is your evidence of extended speaking for Modules 1–4, and it lets you give each learner one kind, specific next step.

ICQs before the presentations and the discussion
Presentations: How long do you speak? (Up to two minutes.) · Can you read your notes? (Only a quick look.) · What do the listeners do? (Ask one question or challenge.) · How do you answer a challenge? (Concede, then counter: "That's a fair point, but…") · Do you have to present to the group? (No, you can present only to the teacher.)
Discussion: Do you argue your real opinion? (No, your side card's.) · What does the devil's advocate do? (Argues the side that's losing.) · What does the chair do? (Keeps time, invites everyone, sums up both sides.) · How do you start each turn in Round 2? (By responding to the last speaker.) · What's a token for? (One turn.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give a talk strip with 5 lines: "I'd like to talk about… · I'll make two points. · Firstly, … because … · Secondly, … For example, … · To sum up, …" and a reply strip: "That's a fair point, but… · I see where you're coming from, yet…". A 60–90 second talk with two points is a complete task. In the discussion, take the For or Against card (not chair or devil's advocate) and speak second in Round 1. Score on the same checklist: a clear, well-signposted short talk can earn full marks on criteria 1 and 5.

Stretch

Be the chair or the devil's advocate. In the presentation, preview three points, use one structure from each module, hedge one claim and use one persuade phrase for your strongest point only. In the Q&A, summarize the challenge before answering ("So your concern is… That's fair. However…"). In the discussion, sum up the other side so fairly that they agree with your summary, then counter it. Homework: the 3–4 minute version with no notes.

L1 watch-outs
  • "I am agree" / "Are you agree?" (Spanish estar de acuerdo, French être d'accord, Portuguese, Italian): still common at B2 under pressure. Drill "I agree. · Do you agree?" once, then leave it for delayed feedback.
  • "We discussed about…" (Spanish discutir sobre, Arabic, Russian obsuzhdat' o): discuss has no preposition. Note that Spanish discutir can mean argue (have a fight).
  • False friends in signposts: "Resuming…" (Spanish resumiendo, French en résumé) → To sum up; "Actually…" meaning "currently" (Spanish actualmente, French actuellement, German aktuell); "Eventually…" meaning "possibly" (Spanish eventualmente, French éventuellement, German eventuell). "At the end" → Finally / In the end.
  • "make a presentation": fine in American English; also give a presentation. Not "do a speech" → give a speech.
  • "According to me…" (French selon moi, Spanish según yo, Indian English): according to is for other sources ("according to a recent study"). Offer "As far as I can tell…" or "It seems to me that…".
  • Directness differs. Dutch, German, Russian and Hebrew speakers may counter without conceding ("No, this is wrong."); Japanese, Korean, Thai and Filipino speakers may concede without countering, so their view disappears. Teach the English middle path, concede and then counter, as a technique, not a personality change. Both styles are normal; English discussion just has its own.
Homework

Online lesson 4.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_12.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Record the full 3–4 minute version of your talk on your phone, plus answers to your two objections, and listen back with the project checklist. Workbook 4.3: the journal (one paragraph of argued English with three B2 structures underlined, then two sentences to the learner you were at the start of this course). Review the Module 1–4 "I can…" lists before C13.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B "That's a fair point; however, the bigger picture tells a different story." · Q2 C "Not only does it reduce costs, but it also improves safety." · Q3 (new) A "Moving on to my second point, …" Model presentation (Ex 2a, Script 4.3-A): signposts in order: I'd like to talk about (1) · I'll cover three points (2) · Let me start with (3) · Moving on to (4) · Finally (5) · To sum up (6). Ex 2b structures heard: impersonal passive (It is widely believed that), what-cleft (What most people need is), participle clause (Having spoken to…), mixed conditional (if we had tried… we might already be seeing), inversion (Not only would it…). Not heard: used to, must have.

Closing the Module

End of C12: Module 4 wrap-up and the halfway point

Recap on slide 18: Although… / Despite… / However, · because of… / due to… · so… that / such… that · Therefore, · It was… who… · What I need is… · Never have I… · I'd like to talk about… · That's a fair point, but… Learners tick the three "I can…" lists. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Use those notes, the Review Race observations and your speaking checklist to plan the C13 catch-up stations.

Celebrate the halfway point. Learners have finished half of B2. Ask each person to say one thing they can do now that they couldn't do in Week 1, with an emphasis structure if they like ("What I'm proudest of is…", "Never have I talked so much in English!"). Keep it short and warm.

Progress Test 1 (C13) covers Modules 1–4. From this module it can test: the grammar after each linker (although / despite / because of / due to / however), despite the fact that, so…that vs such…that, connector punctuation, it-clefts and what-clefts, the other cleft frames, negative inversion with the lesson's triggers (question word order, do / does / did, No sooner…than / Hardly…when), and signposting and concede-and-counter phrases. The C12 speaking checklist gives you the spoken evidence.

Watch for in Module 5: the future perfect and future continuous (5.1) bring new auxiliary chains (will have finished, will be working); learners who have just practiced inversion may over-apply it ("Rarely will have I…"). The causative (5.2) looks like the passive from 3.1 (have it done vs it was done). Keep a 2-minute "linker tennis" or "spotlight it" warmer in C14–C16: it is quick, and it keeps Module 4 range alive in speech.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 5 — Precision Grammar

Three classes about the small choices that make English exact. First, the future continuous and future perfect place an action precisely on a future timeline: still happening (This time next year we'll be running evening classes) or already finished (By June we'll have painted every classroom). Second, the causative shows exactly who did the work: I fixed the sink vs I had the sink fixed, plus the "misfortune" meaning and get someone to / have someone do. Third, determiners and quantifiers say exactly how much of a group you mean: all / whole, every / each, both / neither / either, most (of), none of, with correct agreement. The module starts right after Progress Test 1, so C14 opens by handing the tests back in a warm, low-stress way.

3 lessons Lessons 5.1–5.3 Classes C14–C16 Weeks 7–8 90 min per class CEFR B2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, mingle) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-5-precision.html. Print materials/module-5/classroom-materials.html before C14. It holds the service picture cards and grammar cards, the project and character cards with the timeline frame, the Errand Week information gap, the Services & Errands role-play cards, the class survey cards with the tally sheet and report frame, the "Precision Path" board game, and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. B1 Lesson 5.3 taught the future continuous (This time tomorrow I'll be flying) and time clauses with the present (when I land). B1 learners also met some / any / much / many / a few / a little. Check what they know in each lead-in and move quickly where they are strong. The new B2 work is the future perfect and the continuous-vs-perfect choice against one reference point, the causative (new for almost everyone), and the near-neighbor contrasts in 5.3 (all / whole, every / each, both / neither / either, most / most of) with their verb agreement.
  • Precision is the theme, but fluency still comes first. In every freer task, listen and take notes; don't stop a speaker to fix will have or every people. The feedback stage is where you fix errors, without names. Praise the message first ("Your timeline was so clear, I could picture opening day"), then the form. At B2, give feedback on accuracy and range: one thing to fix, and one "upgrade" (a better word or structure the speaker could have used). Write the upgrades in a separate column on the board ("You said: a lot of us → Try: the majority of us").
  • Dominant speakers. All three freer tasks have built-in roles. Use them: the strongest speaker is the timekeeper or the "council member" who must ask questions (C14), the service provider who has to explain problems (C15), or the data checker who verifies the tally before the pair reports (C16). Use talking chips (3 per learner; you spend one each time you speak) in group discussions. Say it kindly: "Thanks, Omar. Let's hear from someone who hasn't spoken yet."
  • Stretching strong learners toward C1. Each class has a "Stretch" box with C1-leaning language (the future perfect continuous, be due to / be set to, the stress contrast in service vs misfortune causatives, the vast majority of / hardly any / each and every / none of which). Offer it; don't require it.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use many British forms in this module: flat, film, trousers, tyres, neighbour, the underground, valeted, boiler, "sitting an exam," "book it in," "travelling," "timetable," "enquiries," "recognise," "memorise". Teach apartment, movie, pants, tires, neighbor, the subway, detailed, water heater / furnace, taking a test, make an appointment, traveling, schedule, inquiries, recognize, memorize. Accept the British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

When exactly → who exactly → how many exactly. In C14 learners place actions precisely on a future timeline, against one reference point: still happening, or already done? In C15 they say precisely who did the work: me, or someone I paid (or someone I didn't want to)? In C16 they say precisely how much of a group they mean, and make the verb agree. Each class ends with a task where one small grammar choice changes the message: a project timeline presented to a "town council," a service-counter role-play where the customer must be clear about what they need done and by when, and a class survey reported as data.

Trauma-informed: futures, misfortunes and surveys

Futures. For some adults the future is uncertain: a visa or asylum decision, a job search, family far away, health. The online lesson's project and journal ask learners to picture "your life in five years." In class, don't press personal futures. The C14 timeline task is about work and community projects (a community center, a food co-op, a bike-share launch) or a fictional character on a card. The only personal option is small and safe: "this time tomorrow" or "by the end of this week." Never ask "Where will you be living next year?", "Will you have gotten your papers by then?" or "When will you see your family?"

Misfortunes. The causative also describes bad things that happen to you (She had her bag stolen). Keep every misfortune light and invented (a canceled flight, a car towed, a phone screen cracked) and on the cards. Never ask "Has anything ever been stolen from you?" Keep "documents" in C15 to a print shop (flyers, copies, photos); don't use immigration, court or benefits paperwork as examples.

Surveys. The C16 survey questions are light on purpose (coffee or tea, bus or bike, mornings or evenings). No questions about age, family, income, home country, religion or health. "Pass" is a real answer and gets its own column in the tally. Reports use the group, not names ("Only one of us…", never "Only Ana…"), and anyone can ask to take their answer out. Offer these choices every time, so choosing a card or passing is normal and not a sign that something is wrong.

Before the Module

  • Mark Progress Test 1 before C14. Write a short, positive comment on every paper: one thing they did well + one thing to practice. Collect 4–5 common errors from different papers for the C14 board (no names). Note which learners scored below about 60% so you can offer a quiet 1:1 chat at the break.
  • Print the flashcards (Set A): 12 service picture cards and the grammar cards (future markers, causative patterns, determiners). Card stock helps.
  • For C14: one project or character card per group of 3 (Set B) and one timeline frame per group. Put the cards in two piles: "projects" and "characters." Groups choose.
  • For C15: one Errand Week A/B pair per two learners (Set C) and one set of Services & Errands role-play cards (Set D). Cut A and B apart.
  • For C16: one survey card and one tally sheet per pair (Set E), a report frame for anyone who wants it, and the "Our class in numbers" poster (enlarge it to A3 / tabloid, or copy it onto the board). One Precision Path board (Set F) per group of 3–4, a die or a coin, and a counter for each player (coins, paper clips).
  • Have a phone ready to "play" the voicemails in C14 and C15 (read the script while holding the phone to your ear).

Module at a Glance

5.1C14 · wk 7Progress Test 1 return · future continuous will be + -ing (in progress at a future moment; neutral, polite inquiries: Will you be using the car tonight?) · future perfect will have + past participle (finished before a future point) · markers by, by then, by the time (+ present), in + period, this time next…, at + time · by vs untilpresent a project or character timeline to a group, with at least three things that will be happening and three that will already be done, and answer neutral questions about it
