Lesson Plans

5.1 · C14

Future Perfect & Future Continuous

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • reflect on their Progress Test 1 results and set one personal goal
  • use the future continuous (will be + -ing) for an action in progress at a future moment: At ten tomorrow the mayor will be cutting the ribbon.
  • use the future continuous for neutral, polite inquiries: Will you be coming to the meeting?
  • use the future perfect (will have + past participle) for an action finished before a future point: By June we'll have painted every classroom.
  • choose between them with by, by then, by the time (+ present), this time next…, at + time, in + period, and not confuse by with until
  • present a project timeline with three or more of each form and answer questions about it
Materials
  • Marked Progress Test 1 papers (in folders or face-down) · sticky notes · Workbook "My goal after Progress Test 1" box
  • Workbook 5.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Project Timeline frame)
  • Slides 3–8 · Future-marker cards (by, by then, by the time, until, this time next…, at + time, in + period) · Project & character cards (1 per group of 3) + timeline frames
  • Listening scripts 5.1-A (Lena's project update) and 5.1-B (dictation)

Left: a long arrow (the future), with NOW at the left and a big dot labeled 8 p.m. A wavy line runs across the dot: "We'll be eating." (still at the table). A short solid line ends before the dot with a ✔: "We'll have eaten." (plates cleared).

Center: will be + -ing · I'll be working · Will you be using…? (polite)  |  will have + p.p. · I'll have finished · won't have arrived · Will you have eaten?

Right: markers in two columns: continuous ← this time tomorrow / next year · at 10 a.m. · in two hours (landing)    perfect ← by · by then · by the time + present · in two years (qualified). Under it: by = no later than ✔ · until ✘ with the future perfect.

Far right (upgrades column): left empty for feedback.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Welcome & Progress Test 1 return12T–S · S–S · SSlide 3. See "Returning Progress Test 1" below: strengths on the board, papers back privately, pairs fix 4 anonymous errors, each learner writes one goal in the workbook box.
Lead-in: opening day6T–S"Our community center is opening on June 14th. It's March now. Look at the timeline." Draw March → June 14th. Ask: "On June 14th at ten, what will the mayor be doing?" (cutting the ribbon) "And the roof, the painting: will they still be happening on June 14th?" (No, finished.) Elicit or give: "By June 14th, they'll have finished the roof." Get 3–4 learners to repeat both. Don't explain yet.
Future continuous11T–SSlides 4–5. Meaning 1: in progress across a future moment (wavy line). Meaning 2: neutral, polite inquiries (compare "Will you use the car?" and "Will you be using the car?"). Form and contractions. CCQs (below). Recycle B1: "Who remembers This time tomorrow I'll be…?"
Future perfect + markers12T–SSlide 6. The deadline idea ("finished by when?"), form, by / by then / by the time + present, by vs until. Then the contrast table on slide 4 again: same reference point, two meanings. CCQs. Stick the marker cards on the board in two columns as you go.

(3) One volunteer per group tells the class their most impressive milestone (1 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed correction: 4 sentences you heard, no names (2 good, 2 with errors), e.g. "By the time the center will open, we'll have hired six teachers." · "We'll have finished the roof until April." · "This time next year we'll have run evening classes." (meaning: in progress) · plus one upgrade ("We'll finish soon" → "We'll have finished by the end of May"). Praise one clear timeline.

Total9012 + 6 + 11 + 12 + 6 + 6 + 10 + 17 + 5 + 5
If you run long

The test return must stay at 12 minutes. Cut Workbook Ex 5 and the dictation (Script 5.1-B) and set them as homework. If time is short in the freer task, groups present to each other without the class round-up. Never cut the presentations: the council's questions are where the polite Will you be…? becomes real.

Returning Progress Test 1 (12 min) — keep it warm

1. Celebrate first (3 min). Before any paper comes out, write "We can…" on the board and list three things the whole class did well (for example: tell a story with the past perfect continuous · imagine a different past with the third conditional · report what people said with suggest, deny, admit · add emphasis with What I need is…). Say it out loud.

Four modules of B2! You can tell stories with layers of time, imagine a different past, report exactly how people said things, and argue a point in a discussion. That's upper-intermediate English. Give yourselves a hand.

2. Return privately (2 min). Hand papers back face-down or in folders. Never read scores aloud and never compare learners. Everyone gets a written comment: one strength + one thing to practice.

3. "Let's fix it!" (5 min). Show 4 anonymous errors from the tests. Use your own learners' errors if you can; typical ones at this point: If I would have known, I would have called you. · I wish I didn't say that yesterday. · She suggested me to apply for the job. · I'm used to get up at five. (Also common: She must have forget her keys. · Despite of the rain, we went. · Never I have seen such a mess.) Pairs fix them. Take answers. Praise the fixing, not the mistake-free papers.

4. One goal (2 min). Each learner writes one goal in the workbook box ("My goal after Progress Test 1"): "I want to practice ___. Every week I will ___." Give two examples: "I want to practice the third conditional. Every week I'll write three 'If I had…' sentences about my week." / "I want to speak more in discussions. I'll listen to one English podcast a week and summarize it to a friend."

A test is a map. It tells us where we are. It doesn't tell us who we are.

Some adults carry hard memories of school and tests. Keep your voice light and move on quickly. Offer a 1:1 chat at the break to anyone who struggled (below about 60%), and plan catch-up with the Module 1–4 online lessons. Don't let the test return grow past 12 minutes. The best encouragement is a good, successful lesson straight after it, and today's grammar is kind: the forms are completely regular, and learners already know will, -ing and past participles.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: one reference point, two camera angles. Fix a future moment first (a time, a date, an event). Then ask: is the action still happening at that moment (continuous), or already finished by then (perfect)?

"At 8 p.m. we'll be eating dinner." Will we be at the table at 8? (Yes.) Did dinner start before 8? (Yes, probably.) Will it continue after 8? (Yes, probably.) · "At 8 p.m. we'll have eaten." Are we at the table at 8? (No, finished.) Are the plates clean? (Yes, cleared.)
"This time next year Maya will be working at the clinic." Is she working there now? (No, not yet.) Next year, is the job finished or in progress? (In progress.) · "By next June she'll have finished her training." Will she still be training in July? (No, finished before June.)

Meaning: neutral, polite inquiries. The future continuous asks about someone's plans, not for a decision, so it sounds softer.

"Will you use the car tonight?" Could this sound like I want the car? (Yes, a bit.) · "Will you be using the car tonight?" Am I asking for a decision or just asking about your plans? (Just plans: softer.) · Where do you hear "Will you be paying by card?" (Hotels, stores, restaurants.) Is it rude? (No, very polite.)

Meaning: by vs until.

"I'll have finished by six." Can I finish at five? (Yes: no later than six.) · "I'll be working until six." Do I stop before six? (No, I work all the time up to six.) · "I'll have finished until six." Correct? (No! by.)
"By the time you arrive, we'll have left." Which happens first: you arrive, or we leave? (We leave.) Why not "you will arrive"? (Time clause: present tense, like when and before.)

Form.

Future continuouswill be + -ing · 'll be · won't be · Will you be…?This time tomorrow, I'll be flying to Tokyo. · We won't be using the meeting room.
Polite inquiryWill you be + -ing…? · How long will you be + -ing?Will you be joining us? · How long will you be staying?
Future perfectwill have + past participle · 'll have · won't have · Will you have…?By 2030, I'll have finished my degree. · He won't have arrived by six.
by the timeby the time + present, + future perfectBy the time you read this, I'll have left.
Markerscontinuous: this time tomorrow / next week, at + time · perfect: by, by then, by the time · either: in + periodIn two hours we'll be landing. / In two years she'll have qualified.
Recognize (stretch)future perfect continuous: will have been + -ing (how long, up to a future point)By December, I'll have been working here for ten years.

Pronunciation. In speech will have is almost always contracted: I'll have /ˈaɪləv/, we'll have /ˈwiləv/, won't have /ˈwoʊntəv/. Learners often hear it as "I'll of" and some write "will of": show the written form next to the sound. I'll be is /aɪl bi/: make sure the dark /l/ is there, or it becomes "I be." by the time is one chunk /baɪðəˈtaɪm/. Neutral inquiries sound friendly with a gentle rise at the end: "Will you be paying by card? ↗" Main stress falls on the main verb, not on will / have / be: "By June we'll have painted every classroom."

Drill script
  1. 8 p.m. gesture drill (point at the board dot; wavy hand = in progress, flat "done" hand = finished): T: "eat" + wavy hand → S: "At 8 we'll be eating." T: "eat" + done hand → S: "By 8 we'll have eaten." Repeat with work, watch the game, clean up, leave. Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Contraction drill: T: "I will have finished" → S: "I'll have finished" /ˈaɪləv/. T: "They will not have arrived" → S: "They won't have arrived." Back-chain: "…finished" → "have finished" → "I'll have finished" → "By Friday I'll have finished."
  3. Make it polite: T (as a receptionist): "Pay by card?" → S: "Will you be paying by card?" T: "Stay long?" → S: "How long will you be staying?" T: "Use the projector?" → S: "Will you be using the projector?" Model the gentle rise.
  4. By the time: T: "you / arrive · we / leave" → S: "By the time you arrive, we'll have left." T: "the movie / start · I / buy the popcorn" → S: "By the time the movie starts, I'll have bought the popcorn."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 names a future moment ("This time tomorrow…" / "By Sunday night…"); S2 finishes it with the right form, real or imagined. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
Project Timeline — how to run it
  • Groups of 3. Put two piles of cards on the front table: projects (a community center renovation, a food co-op opening, a city bike-share launch, a new clinic wing, a grocery store's new app, a school library makeover) and characters (Maya, a trainee nurse; Dev, who is starting a food truck; Rosa, a new bus driver; Sam, who is building a small web-design business). Each card gives real-sounding milestones and dates.
  • Groups fill in the timeline frame (key words only): next month (what will be happening?) · by the time… (a milestone) · this time next year · in five years' time. Each point needs one will be + -ing idea and one will have + p.p. idea. They may invent details.
  • Two groups meet. Group 1 gives a "status forecast": each learner presents one or two points on the timeline, in order. Group 2 is the town council (for a project) or the character's friends (for a character) and must ask at least two neutral questions: "Will you be hiring local people?" · "Will you have finished the parking lot by opening day?" Then they swap.
  • Dominant speakers: give the strongest speaker in each group the council chair job: they may not present, but they must ask the most questions and give the other group a "vote" at the end ("We approve your project!"). Quieter learners present the first and last points, which are easiest to prepare.
  • The personal option stays small and safe: a learner who wants to may add one sentence about this time tomorrow or the end of this week. No one talks about their own long-term future.
ICQs before the Project Timeline
Do you talk about your own life in five years? (No, a project or a character from a card.) · How many points on the timeline? (Four.) · For each point, how many sentences? (Two: one "will be -ing," one "will have done.") · Do you write full sentences on the frame? (No, key words.) · What does the council do? (Listens and asks at least two "Will you be…?" questions.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with two frames on a strip: "At ___, we'll be ___ing." and "By ___, we'll have ___ed." Highlight be in one color and have in another on the board and on the strip. Choose the project card with the shortest text (the bike-share launch) and read it aloud to the group. In the presentation, the learner takes the first point only. Target: 2 sentences of each kind, spoken; in writing, Workbook Ex 2 items 1–4 only.

Stretch

Add the future perfect continuous for duration ("By June we'll have been working on it for two years") and C1-style planning language: be due to, be set to, be on track to ("The center is due to open on June 14th"). Use by which time to join ideas ("We open in June, by which time we'll have hired six teachers"). As council members, ask the harder questions: "Will you have secured the funding by then?" Early finishers rewrite Lena's voicemail as a formal email to volunteers.

L1 watch-outs
  • "By the time you will arrive": French (quand tu arriveras), Russian, Polish and Ukrainian (future perfective in time clauses), Greek, and Portuguese (future subjunctive quando chegares, which feels "future") often put a future in the time clause. Spanish speakers use the subjunctive (cuando llegues) and are usually fine. Keep pointing at "time clause = present."
  • by / until: German bis, Dutch tot, Russian and Polish do, Arabic ḥattā and Turkish -e kadar can mean both "by" and "until," so "I'll have finished until six" is very common. Use the "no later than" test: if it fits, it's by.
  • Dropping have or be: "By 2030 I will finished," "This time tomorrow I will flying." Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Indonesian speakers (no verb inflection) and learners who "hear" only the main verb in contracted speech. Show the three-part form with three colors: will + have + finished.
  • Overusing the continuous: Indian and Pakistani English and many Hindi, Urdu and Tamil speakers use will be + -ing for simple future promises ("I will be sending it tomorrow"). It's understood and often fine, but show when plain will or the perfect is clearer.
  • Present for future: Spanish, Portuguese, Italian, Japanese and Korean speakers may use the present where English needs a future form ("At 8 we eat dinner" for "we'll be eating"). The meaning is often clear; recast with the camera image.
Homework

Online lesson 5.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_13.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz. For the online "Postcard from the Future" project, learners may choose the project or character from class instead of their own life. Workbook 5.1: finish the Project Timeline (on the back of the page, write 6 full sentences from your group's frame: 3 will be + -ing, 3 will have + p.p., one with by the time), "My new words" and the journal (a project, a character, or "this time tomorrow / by Sunday night").

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A will be having · Q2 A By the time you arrive, I'll have cooked dinner. · Q3 (new) B Will you be paying by card? Lena's update (Ex 1): 1 will have finished · 2 will be working · 3 starts · 4 will have painted · 5 will have arrived · 6 will be cutting · 7 will be serving · 8 Will you be coming.

5.2 · C15

The Causative — have / get something done

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • tell the difference between doing something yourself (I cut my hair) and arranging for someone else to do it (I had my hair cut)
  • form the causative have / get + object + past participle in any tense and after modals (You should get your eyes checked), keeping the object before the participle
  • choose have (neutral) or get (informal, sometimes "finally managed")
  • understand and use the misfortune meaning (They had their flight canceled) and tell it apart from the past perfect (He had stolen a wallet)
  • use get someone to do and have someone do, and recognize have someone doing
  • arrange everyday services in a role-play and report a week of errands
Materials
  • Workbook 5.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, "My services" table)
  • Slides 9–13 · Service picture cards (12) · causative pattern cards · Errand Week A/B cards (1 pair per 2 learners) · Services & Errands role-play cards (8 scenarios, A and B)
  • Listening scripts 5.2-A (Marco's busy Saturday) and 5.2-B (dictation)

Left: two stick figures. Figure 1 with scissors: "I cut my hair." (me ✂️). Figure 2 in a chair, a hairdresser behind: "I had my hair cut." (someone else ✂️, I arranged it).

Center: the recipe in three boxes: have / get → thing → done. Below it the "only have changes" ladder: I have it serviced · I'm having it serviced · I had it serviced · I've had it serviced · I'll have it serviced · You should get it serviced.

Right: ✅ I had my car repaired. (service) · ⚠️ She had her bag stolen. (misfortune) · ❌ I had repaired my car. (= past perfect: I did it!) · get him to help · have him help (no to).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: by the end of today…8S–S → T–SReview C14. Pairs finish on the board: "By the end of today's class, we'll have… · At 9 tonight, I'll be… (real or imagined) · Will you be…?" Then two groups give their C14 project's best milestone in one sentence. Correct by the time + present on the spot only if the whole class is making the error.
Lead-in: who did it?5T–SPoint at your hair (or show a photo of a clean car): "Look! New haircut! Did I cut it?" (No!) "Who cut it?" (A hairdresser / a barber.) "So I don't say 'I cut my hair.' I say…" Elicit or give: "I had my hair cut." Draw the two stick figures (board left). "Who cut it? Who paid? Who's the subject of the sentence?"
Causative: M / F / P14T–SSlides 9–10. Meaning (someone else does a service; I arrange it), the recipe, the "only have changes" ladder through tenses and modals, the service picture cards (car serviced, sink fixed, keys copied, pants shortened, documents printed…). CCQs (below). Word order vs past perfect with the ❌ example.
Drill6T–S → SPicture-card drill and tense ladder (see Drill script). Keep a fast pace.
have vs get · misfortune · people10T–SSlides 11–12. have vs get (register, and "finally got it fixed"). Misfortune: "She had her bag stolen." (Did she arrange it? No!) The past perfect trap. Then the person patterns: I got my landlord to fix it (persuaded) · I'll have the porter bring your bags (arranged, no to) · She had everyone laughing. Model dialogue on slide 12 in pairs, once each way.
Listening: Marco's Saturday6T–S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1. Read Script 5.2-A (voicemail) twice. Learners complete the gaps and decide who did each job. Pairs compare. Ask: "What did Marco do himself?" (washed the dog)
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone, then check in pairs. Board-check Ex 3 (myself or a professional?) and Ex 4 (service / misfortune / past perfect), which carry the meaning.
Freer 1: Errand Week10S–S (pairs)Slide 13. Information gap. A has Sam's to-do list; B has Sam's receipts and texts. A asks: "Did Sam get the oil changed?" B answers from the receipts: "Yes, he had it changed on Monday. And he had the brakes checked." Together they decide what Sam did himself, what he had done, what went wrong (a misfortune), and what he still needs to get done. They write 3 sentences for the last group. ICQs first.
Freer 2: Services & Errands13S–S (pairs, rotate)Slide 13. Role-play at the counter or on the phone: A = customer, B = service provider (auto shop, building manager, hair salon, print shop, phone repair, alterations, dry cleaner, key counter). A must arrange two things with the causative, ask when it will be ready (recycle C14: "Will you have finished it by Friday?"), and ask one neutral question ("Will you be needing my phone number?"). B has information on the card (price, time, one small problem). 4 minutes per role-play; swap roles and cards; 3 rounds. Then pairs report: "I'm getting my pants shortened, and they'll have finished them by Thursday."
Feedback5T–SBoard: 4 heard sentences, e.g. "I had repaired my car at the shop." · "I got fixed my phone." · "The manager will have the plumber to come." · "I cut my hair at the salon yesterday." (natural in casual speech, but upgrade: "I got my hair cut / I got a haircut"). Learners fix them. Praise one great role-play from each part of the room.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 5.2; workbook "My services" and the journal.
Total908 + 5 + 14 + 6 + 10 + 6 + 8 + 10 + 13 + 5 + 5
If you run long

This is the busiest class of the module. Do only 2 role-play rounds, and move Workbook Ex 5 to homework. Never cut the Errand Week gap: it is where learners must decide "did he do it, or did someone do it for him?", which is the whole point of the lesson. With a very strong class, shorten the drill instead.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: who actually does it? The causative keeps me as the subject, but someone else does the work, usually a professional I arrange and pay.

"I had my car repaired." Did I repair it? (No.) Who did? (A mechanic, a garage.) Who arranged it? (I did.) Do we know the mechanic's name? (No, and we don't need it.) · "I repaired my car." Who had the tools in their hands? (I did.)
"I'm getting my hair cut this afternoon." Is it finished? (No, later today.) Will I use the scissors? (No, the hairdresser.) · "You should get your eyes checked." Is this advice? (Yes.) Who checks the eyes? (An eye doctor.)

Meaning: have vs get. The same meaning; get is more informal and common in speech, and can suggest effort.

"It took weeks, but I finally got the heater repaired." Was it easy? (No, it took effort.) · A formal email to a customer: "We will ___ your order delivered by Friday." have or get? (have: more neutral / formal.)

Meaning: misfortune, and the past perfect trap.

"She had her bag stolen on the train." Did she arrange this? (No!) Did she steal it? (No, someone stole it from her.) · "He had stolen a wallet." Who was the thief? (He was.) What changed? (The order: object before the participle = causative.)

Meaning: the person patterns.

"I got my brother to help me move." Did my brother want to help at first? (Maybe not; I persuaded him.) Is there a to? (Yes, with get.) · "I'll have the porter bring your bags up." Who says this? (A hotel receptionist.) Is there a to? (No, with have.) · "She had everyone laughing." Did she ask them to laugh? (No, she made it happen: funny story.)

Form.

Recipehave / get + object + past participleI had my car repaired. · We got the carpets cleaned.
Tenseschange only have / get: have · am having · had · have had · will have · am going to haveShe's having her hair cut right now. · I've just had my eyes tested.
Modalsmodal + have / get + object + p.p. · should have had + object + p.p.You should get your teeth checked. · We should have had it fixed sooner.
Misfortunesame form; something bad happened to youThey had their flight canceled at the last minute.
Person: persuadeget + person + to + verbI got him to help me move the sofa.
Person: arrangehave + person + verb (no to)The manager had her assistant reschedule the meeting.
Recognizehave + person + -ing (cause an ongoing action)She soon had everyone laughing.

Pronunciation. The past participle ending is easy to lose: fixed /fɪkst/, serviced /ˈsɝvɪst/, checked /tʃɛkt/, cleaned /klind/, painted /ˈpeɪntɪd/. If the ending disappears, "I had my car fix" sounds like a mistake, so drill the final sound. Linking: had it /ˈhædɪt/, got it /ˈgɑɾɪt/ (American flap t), get it done /ˈgɛɾɪt dʌn/. Stress usually falls on the thing: "I had my hair cut." Stretch: in a misfortune, the stress often moves to the participle because it's the surprising news: "She had her bag stolen!"

Drill script
  1. Picture cards (hold up a service card): car → S: "I had my car serviced." · sink → "I had the sink fixed." · keys → "I had a key copied." · pants → "I had my pants shortened." · phone → "I had the screen replaced." Choral, then individuals.
  2. Tense ladder (T says a time word; learners change only have): T: "every year" → S: "I have my car serviced every year." T: "right now" → "I'm having my car serviced right now." T: "last week" → "I had…" T: "just" → "I've just had…" T: "next week" → "I'll have / I'm going to have…" T: "should" → "I should get my car serviced."
  3. Pronunciation: choral fixed /fɪkst/, serviced /ˈsɝvɪst/, painted /ˈpeɪntɪd/; then "I had it fixed" /aɪ ˈhædɪt fɪkst/ ×3, fast.
  4. Me or them? T describes; learners choose: T: "My neighbor painted my kitchen for money." → S: "You had your kitchen painted." T: "I painted my kitchen on Sunday." → S: "You painted it yourself!" T: "Someone broke my window." → S: "You had your window broken." (misfortune)
  5. Get him to / have him: T: "My landlord / fix the heater (persuade)" → S: "I got my landlord to fix the heater." T: "The hotel / a porter / bring the bags (arrange)" → S: "The hotel had a porter bring the bags."
Errand Week — how to run it
  • Pairs. Card A is Sam's to-do list for the week (8 jobs: oil change and brakes, flyers for the block party, the dripping kitchen faucet, a haircut before Saturday's wedding, new suit pants that are too long, a spare key for his mom, photos for a new work ID badge, a flat bike tire). Card B has Sam's receipts, texts and calendar notes for the same week. Neither partner can see the other card.
  • A asks about each job with the causative: "Did Sam get the flyers printed?" B answers from the evidence: "Yes, he had 200 flyers printed on Tuesday." Some jobs Sam did himself (B's text says "Fixed the faucet myself! YouTube!"), one went wrong (a misfortune: "Got my car towed from the store parking lot. Ugh."), and some are still open.
  • Together, pairs sort the 8 jobs into four columns on the back of card A: did it himself · had it done · something went wrong · still needs to get it done. Then they write 3 sentences for the last column: "He still needs to get his pants shortened. He should have them done before the wedding on Saturday."
  • Check with the model answers on the materials sheet. A pair that gets the meaning right with one word-order slip has succeeded; fix the slip at feedback.
Services & Errands role-play — how to run it
  • Eight scenarios, each with a customer card (A) and a service card (B): the auto shop, the building manager (apartment repairs), the hair salon / barber shop, the print & copy shop, the phone repair kiosk, the alterations shop, the dry cleaner, and the key counter at the hardware store.
  • A (customer) must: (1) explain two things they need done, with the causative ("I'd like to have the oil changed and get the brakes checked"); (2) ask when it will be ready, with the future perfect or continuous ("Will you have finished by five?"); (3) ask one neutral question ("Will you be calling me when it's ready?"). B (service provider) uses the card: price, time, and one small problem to explain ("We'll have to order the part, so we won't have finished until Thursday").
  • Rounds of 4 minutes. After each round, the customer swaps cards with another pair. Swap roles every round, so everyone is a customer at least once.
  • Finish with a 1-minute report to a new partner: "I'm getting my jacket dry-cleaned. They'll have done it by Saturday, and they're having a new button sewn on too."
  • Dominant speakers make good service providers: give them the cards with a "problem" and the job of explaining it clearly. Quieter learners start as customers, with the phrase bank on the card.
ICQs before Errand Week and the role-play
Errand Week: Can you show your partner your card? (No.) · Who asks, A or B? (A.) · What do you find out? (What Sam did himself, what he had done, what went wrong, what he still needs to get done.) · How many sentences do you write? (Three, for the last column.)
Role-play: Customer, how many things do you need done? (Two.) · What else do you ask? (When it will be ready, and one "Will you be…?" question.) · Service provider, where's your information? (On my card.) · When do you swap? (After 4 minutes.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with the past simple only and three picture cards: "I had my ___ fixed / cleaned / cut." Give a strip with the three boxes (have / thing / done) and let learners build sentences by putting the picture card in the middle box. In the role-play, take the customer role with the auto shop or hair salon card (picture on the card) and one required sentence plus "When will it be ready?" Target: 4 spoken causative sentences; in writing, Workbook Ex 2 items 1–4.

Stretch

Use the passive-like and C1 forms: get + object + -ing ("I finally got the engine running"), have something done with the future perfect ("I'll have had the roof repaired by winter"), and need + -ing for services ("The car needs servicing" = needs to be serviced). Contrast stress in service vs misfortune. In the role-play, handle a complaint politely: "I had my suit dry-cleaned here, but the stain is still there. Could you have it looked at again?" Early finishers write a short, polite complaint email.

L1 watch-outs
  • "I cut my hair" for a salon haircut: Spanish me corté el pelo, Portuguese cortei o cabelo, Russian я подстригся, Chinese 我剪头发了 and Arabic use the plain verb (or a reflexive) for both meanings. Note: American speakers also say "I cut my hair" casually for a salon visit, but I got my hair cut / I got a haircut is clearer. Accept it in speech; teach the causative for precision.
  • Word order: "I had repaired my car" / "I got fixed my phone." French j'ai fait réparer ma voiture, Italian ho fatto riparare, and Spanish mandé arreglar el coche put the verb before the object, so learners write the past perfect by mistake. Point at the three boxes: have → thing → done.
  • "make" for "have / get": French faire, Japanese -saseru, Korean -게 하다, Chinese 让 and Turkish -dır causatives often come out as "I made the mechanic repair my car," which sounds like force in English. Show: make = force · get … to = persuade · have = arrange.
  • "have someone to do": very common across languages. have + person + verb (no to); get + person + to + verb.
  • Lost endings: Vietnamese, Cantonese, Thai and Korean speakers often drop final /t/ /d/ /st/: "I had it fix." Drill the final sound and link it to the next word ("fixed_on Monday").
Homework

Online lesson 5.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_14.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz. For the online project's "misfortune" example, learners may invent one or use a character. Workbook 5.2: finish "My services" (6 sentences in at least three tenses, one with get, one plain active "I do it myself"), "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A I had my hair cut yesterday. · Q2 A had her bag stolen · Q3 (new) B Ana got her brother to help. Marco's Saturday (Ex 1): 1 serviced · 2 tires · 3 printed · 4 made · 5 cut · 6 to fix · 7 look · 8 Marco (himself).

5.3 · C16

Determiners & Quantifiers — all, every, both & more

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • choose all or whole (all the books · the whole book · all day · the whole day) and avoid "all the day"
  • choose every (the group as a whole) or each (one by one, often two), with a singular noun and verb, and avoid "every people"
  • use both (plural verb), neither and either (singular verb) for two things, and both … and, neither … nor, either … or
  • use most, all, some, none with and without of: most people (general) vs most of the people here (specific) · most of them
  • run a class survey and report the results with a range of quantifiers and correct agreement
Materials
  • Workbook 5.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Survey & Report frame, "Look back at my goal")
  • Slides 14–18 · Determiner cards · Precision Path board game (1 per group of 3–4, a die or coin, counters) · survey cards + tally sheets (1 per pair) · report frames · "Our class in numbers" poster
  • Listening scripts 5.3-A (the staff survey) and 5.3-B (dictation)

Left: a vertical scale from 100% to 0%: all of us · almost all / nearly every one of us · most of us · about half of us · some of us · a few of us · only one of us · none of us. Next to it: 2 people → both (2/2) · either (1 of 2) · neither (0/2).

Center: all + the + plural / uncountable · the whole + singular · all the day → all day / the whole day. every / each + singular noun + singular verb: Every room has… · Each of the rooms has… · every people → every person / all people.

Right: the "of" rule: of + the / my / them ✔ (most of the people, most of them) · no the? no of! (most of people → most people). Verb check: both → are · neither / either → is · none of → is / are.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: Precision Path9S–S (groups)Review C14–C15. Groups of 3–4 play the Precision Path board game (future perfect / continuous and causative squares). Stop after 8 minutes wherever they are; the player furthest along wins. 1 minute: each group reads one "Fix it" square they solved.
Lead-in: every people?4T–SBoard: "Every people in this class ___ a phone." "Is this good English?" Let learners argue. Elicit two correct versions: "Every person in this class has a phone." / "All the people in this class have phones." "Today: small words, big precision."
all / whole · every / each11T–SSlide 14. all vs whole (position, "the whole cake" vs "all the cakes," "all day"). every vs each (group vs one by one; each for two; almost every). Singular noun + singular verb. CCQs (below).
both / neither / either8T–SSlide 15. Hold up two pens. "Both pens are blue." (plural) · "Neither pen is red." · "Either pen is fine." (singular) Then both … and, neither … nor, either … or, and neither of them is (formal) vs are (relaxed speech). The model dialogue on slide 15 in pairs.
most / none · the "of" rule8T–SSlide 16. General vs specific: "Most people like weekends." / "Most of the people in this room take the bus." With pronouns, of is compulsory: most of us, none of them. The two classic errors. none of + is / are.
Drill5T–S → SSee Drill script: the scale drill with the class, the "two pens" drill, the of or no of drill.

Freer: Class Survey & Report25S–S (pairs, mingle) → S–S → T–SSlide 17. (1) Each pair gets one survey card and a tally sheet (1 min). (2) Mingle: ask everyone in the class, tally the answers, including "Pass" (7 min). (3) Pairs turn the tally into a 4–5 sentence report with the frame: at least one all / most / none of us, one every / each, and one both / neither / either about the two of them or two options (6 min). The data checker in each pair checks the numbers add up and the verbs agree. (4) Pairs read their report to the class; everyone else listens for one fact that surprised them (7 min). (5) Each pair writes its best sentence on the "Our class in numbers" poster (4 min). ICQs first.
Feedback + module check7T–SDelayed correction from the reports: "Most of students take the bus." · "Every people in this class have a phone." · "Neither of us drink coffee." (formal: drinks; accept drink in speech) · "All the class came by bus." (upgrade: the whole class) · plus 2 good sentences. Then learners tick the three Module 5 "I can…" lists and look back at their Progress Test goal (private).
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 5.3; the "compare two groups" writing; preview C17: "Next class: happy → unhappy → happiness → happily. One word, a whole family."
Total909 + 4 + 11 + 8 + 8 + 5 + 8 + 25 + 7 + 5
If you run long

Stop the board game after 6 minutes, and move Workbook Ex 3–4 to homework. In the survey, let pairs report to one other pair instead of the whole class, and only the "best sentence" goes to the poster. Never cut the report: it is where the verb agreement and the of rule get used for a real purpose.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: all vs whole. whole = one complete, unbroken unit; all = everything in a set or an amount.

"I ate the whole cake." How many cakes? (One.) Is any left? (No, every piece.) · "I ate all the cakes." How many? (More than one.) · "It rained all the day." Correct? (No: "all day" or "the whole day.") · Where does whole go: before or after the? (After: the whole day.)

Meaning: every vs each. Same verb (singular), different view of the group.

"Every student passed." Am I thinking of the group together or one by one? (Together: all of them.) · "Each student gets a personal report." One report for the whole class? (No, one for each person, individually.) · "She had a bag in every hand." Correct? (No, two hands: each.) · "Every people needs water." Correct? (No: every person or all people.)

Meaning: both / neither / either. Only for two.

"Both restaurants are good." How many restaurants? (Two.) Are they both good? (Yes.) · "Neither restaurant is cheap." How many are cheap? (Zero.) · "Either day works." Can I choose Monday? (Yes.) Tuesday? (Yes, one or the other.) · "Neither of my three brothers…" Correct? (No! Three → none of.)

Meaning: general vs specific (the "of" rule).

"Most people like music." People in this room or people in general? (In general.) · "Most of the people here like jazz." General or specific? (Specific: the people here.) · "Most of teachers work hard." Correct? (No: no the, no of. "Most teachers" or "most of the teachers.") · "All us are here." Correct? (No: "all of us.")

Form.

all / wholeall (+ the) + plural / uncountable · the / my + whole + singular · all day (no the)all the books · all the information · the whole book · It rained all day / the whole day.
every / each+ singular noun + singular verb · each of + the + plural + singular verbEvery room has a view. · Each of the candidates was interviewed.
bothtwo together · plural verb · both … and …Both options are good. · Both of us agree.
neither / either0 of 2 / 1 of 2 · singular verb · neither … nor · either … orNeither option is cheap. · Either day works for me.
+ ofmost / all / some / none / both / neither / each + of + the / my / this / them / usmost of the class · none of us · all of my friends
no ofgeneral statement, bare nounMost people love music. · All children need sleep.
none of+ uncountable → singular · + plural → singular (formal) or plural (everyday)None of the water was clean. · None of the students was / were late.

Pronunciation. whole sounds exactly like hole /hoʊl/: the w is silent. each /itʃ/ is a long vowel, not itch /ɪtʃ/. neither and either can be /ˈniðər/ or /ˈnaɪðər/; most Americans say /ˈniðər/, and both are correct. none /nʌn/ rhymes with fun, not phone. Linking in the "of" phrases: all of us /ˈɔləvəs/, most of them /ˈmoʊstəvðəm/ (often /ˈmoʊsəvəm/ in fast speech), none of them /ˈnʌnəvəm/. Stress the quantifier when it's the news: "None of us drink coffee!"

Drill script
  1. Scale drill (with the class, true facts): T: "Who has a pen? Hands up." Count. S: "All of us have a pen." / "Most of us have a pen." T: "Who is wearing a hat?" → "None of us is / are wearing a hat." / "Only one of us is…" Choral, then individuals.
  2. Two pens (hold up two objects): T: "blue?" → S: "Both pens are blue." T: "red?" → "Neither pen is red." T: "can I use one?" → "Either pen is fine." T: "Neither of them…" → S: "Neither of them is red."
  3. Of or no of? T: "most / people" → S: "most people." T: "most / the people here" → "most of the people here." T: "all / us" → "all of us." T: "none / them" → "none of them." T: "all / children" → "all children."
  4. Singular check: T: "every room / have" → S: "Every room has…" T: "each of the rooms / have" → "Each of the rooms has…" T: "both rooms / have" → "Both rooms have…"
  5. whole: T: "all the day" → S: "all day / the whole day." T: "the whole cakes" → S: "all the cakes." T: "the whole team" → S: "Correct!"
Class Survey & Report — how to run it
  • Pairs. Each pair gets one survey card with a light question and 2–4 answer options (e.g. "Do you drink coffee, tea, both, or neither?" · "How do you usually get to class?" · "Have you ever had your hair cut by a friend or family member?"). Twelve cards, so every pair has a different question. The tally sheet has a column for each answer and a "Pass" column.
  • Mingle: pairs split up and ask everyone, including the teacher. Tally marks only, no names. Then they meet and add their tallies. The data checker (the stronger learner in the pair) checks that the total equals the number of people in the room.
  • The report (4–5 sentences) uses the frame on the tally sheet: "We asked every person in the class… · Most of us… / None of us… / All of us except two… · Each of us… · Both of us (the two in our pair)… / Neither of us… · The whole class…" Encourage the upgrade phrases from the workbook: the majority of us, just over half of us, only a handful of us, not a single person.
  • Reports go to the class. Listeners note one fact that surprised them. Then each pair writes its best sentence on the "Our class in numbers" poster. Keep the poster on the wall for the C17 warmer.
  • Anonymity: no names in reports. If only one person gave an answer, say "one of us," never who. Anyone can ask a pair to take their answer out. "Pass" is counted and reported like any other answer: "Two of us preferred not to say."
ICQs before the survey
Who do you ask: three people or everyone? (Everyone, even the teacher.) · Do you write names? (No, tally marks only.) · Can someone say "Pass"? (Yes, and we count it.) · What's the data checker's job? (Check the numbers add up and the verbs agree.) · How many sentences in the report? (Four or five.) · Which words must you use? (One all / most / none of us, one every / each, one both / neither / either.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on three quantifiers and the scale: all of us · most of us · none of us, plus both of us / neither of us for the pair. Give the report frame with those five sentence starts only. Choose a survey card with a picture (coffee / tea) and two answers. In the mingle, the learner asks and their partner tallies. Target: 3 report sentences, spoken; in writing, Workbook Ex 1 items 1–5.

Stretch

Use a wider range: the vast majority of us, hardly any of us, not one of us, each and every one of us, all but two of us. Use neither … nor with proximity agreement ("Neither my partner nor I am… / Neither I nor my partner is…", then rephrase to sound natural). Try relative quantifiers (C1): "We asked fourteen people, most of whom drink coffee, none of whom drink it black." Early finishers write the class report as a short, neutral paragraph for a newsletter.

L1 watch-outs
  • "all the day": Spanish todo el día, French toute la journée, Italian tutto il giorno, Portuguese o dia todo put "all" before the article with a singular noun. Teach all day and the whole day as chunks.
  • "every people / each students": Arabic kull means all, every and each and takes plural nouns in some uses; Chinese 每…都 and Korean 모든 / 마다 don't mark number the same way. Point out that people is already plural.
  • "the most of people / most of people": Spanish la mayoría de la gente, French la plupart des gens, Portuguese a maioria das pessoas, Italian la maggior parte delle persone have an article or "of the" in every case. No the in English → no of; never "the most" for quantity.
  • Plural verbs after every / each / neither: many speakers follow the meaning (many people) rather than the grammar. "Every student have…" is very common. Mark it in writing; in speech, neither of them are is normal English, so don't over-correct.
  • both / neither for more than two: languages without a "two" form (Tagalog, Vietnamese, Chinese, Japanese) may use both for three or more ("both of my three kids"). Hold up two fingers every time.
  • "none" vs "no one" vs "nobody": "None came to the party" (for people) sounds odd in everyday English; nobody / no one came or none of them came.
Homework

Online lesson 5.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_15.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, listening for the singular verb after every, each and neither; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 5.3: copy the pair's survey report neatly, then "My new words," the journal ("compare two groups": 6 sentences about two teams, two stores, two apps, two workplaces or two neighborhoods, with one every / each, one both / neither / either and one most of / none of / all of) and "Look back at my goal."

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Every student has a locker. · Q2 A Neither of the plans is ready. · Q3 (new) C Each of the rooms has a window. Staff survey (Ex 5): 1 All · 2 Most · 3 Each · 4 None · 5 Neither · 6 Every / whole · 7 Both.

Closing the Module

End of C16: Module 5 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "when, who, how many": This time next year we'll be… / By then we'll have… (placing actions on a future timeline) → I had it fixed. I got him to help. (who did the work) → Every person has… · Neither of us is… · Most of us… (how much of a group). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C17 warmer.

Check the C14 goals. At the end of C16, invite learners to read their Progress Test goal again (the "Look back at my goal" box in Workbook 5.3): "Did you practice it? What will you do next?" This is private: no one reports to the class.

Watch for in Module 6: word formation (6.1) builds naturally on today's data language (majority, minority, individual, personally); advanced comparison (6.2) pairs with quantifiers ("far more of us take the bus than drive"). Learners who just learned every + singular may over-apply it ("all the student"). Keep a 2-minute warmer in C17–C19 with one line from each lesson: a future perfect deadline, a causative errand, and one fact from the "Our class in numbers" poster.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 6 — Words & Questions

Three classes about precision: the right form of a word, the right size of a difference, and the right shape of a question. First comes word formation: word families, the suffixes that signal a word's job, the prefixes that flip or shade its meaning, and the stress that moves as a word changes form. Then advanced comparison: far / much / slightly / nowhere near as…, twice as… as, the more…, the more… and better and better. Last, asking skillfully: indirect questions for politeness (Could you tell me where the station is?), negative questions for surprise, agreement and persuasion (Isn't it…? Don't you think…?), and subject questions with no auxiliary.

3 lessons Lessons 6.1–6.3 Classes C17–C19 Weeks 9–10 90 min per class CEFR B2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (pairs, groups, role-play) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-6-words-questions.html. Print materials/module-6/classroom-materials.html before C17. It holds the affix, scale and opener flashcards, the job and product picture cards, the new identity cards, the Word Family Grid A/B cards, the Prefix/Suffix Race cards, the Comeback Story bingo, the Compare & Recommend fact sheets and client cards, the Polite Enquiry role-play cards and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons (with a US or UK voice and a slower speed), so learners can hear the model sentences again at home.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. Learners already know simple comparatives and superlatives (A2), as… as (A2), reported questions with statement word order and if / whether (B1 3.2), Could you…? requests (B1 7.2) and question tags (B1 8.1). Check in each lead-in and go faster where they are strong. The new B2 work is choosing the exact form (a gap-fill skill), saying how big a difference is, the parallel comparative, and the register and tone of questions: when to wrap a question in an opener, and what a negative question really means.
  • Fluency before accuracy. In every freer task, listen and take notes; don't stop a speaker to fix a suffix or a word order. The feedback stage is where you fix errors, without names. Praise the message first ("That recommendation really convinced me."), then the form. At B2, give feedback on accuracy and range: note one error to fix and one upgrade ("You said much cheaper three times. Try considerably or nowhere near as expensive.").
  • Dominant speakers. All three freer tasks have built-in turns: use them. Give every group a talking-turn token (you speak only when you hold it), give the strongest speaker a job (the Race referee, the "client" who must be convinced, the role-play observer with the checklist), and use "your partner's idea first" when groups report back. Say it kindly: "Thanks, Omar. Let's hear from someone who hasn't spoken yet."
  • Stretching strong learners toward C1. Each lesson has a Stretch box with C1-edge language (less transparent suffixes, by far / every bit as… as, Would you happen to know…? / I don't suppose you could…). Offer it as an option, not a requirement; the class target is solid B2.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British forms (recognise, modernise, economise, practise, behaviour, flat, motorway, queue, film, loo, full stop, skilfully). Teach recognize, modernize, economize, practice (verb and noun), behavior, apartment, highway, line, movie, restroom, period, skillfully. Accept the British forms when learners use them. Lesson 6.1 itself says that -ise and -ize are both correct: use this to make the point.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

The right form → the right size → the right shape. In C17 learners reshape one idea into four jobs (decide → decision → decisive → decisively) and use the forms to tell a short "comeback story." In C18 they weigh options precisely (far more reliable, only slightly heavier, nowhere near as cheap, twice as big) and make a recommendation to a "client." In C19 they ask for information at a front desk and in a job interview in a way that sounds considerate (I was wondering whether…), and they check and persuade with negative questions (Isn't it cheaper online? Don't you think the annual plan is better value?). The C18 comparison language comes back naturally in the C19 role-plays. Point this out: it is the moment learners feel their B2 tools working together.

Trauma-informed: stories, comparisons and interviews

Three things in this module need care. (1) Comeback stories (C17) are about "something that went wrong and then went right." Keep them light: a team project, a cake, a garden, a driving test, a small business on a scenario card. Never ask about a personal failure, a loss, or a hard journey. (2) Comparisons (C18): the cities, jobs and products are invented on purpose. Don't ask learners to compare "your country and the US" or "your old life and your life now"; it can bring up grief or politics, and the course avoids immigration topics. Pay also needs care: the job cards give the numbers, so no one discusses their own salary. (3) Interviews (C19): the lesson's role-plays are a job interview and customer-service calls only. Never role-play an immigration, asylum, police, court or benefits interview, and don't let a learner play "the officer." The questions in role-play come from the cards, never "Where are you from?" or "Why did you come here?" For every personal task, offer three choices out loud: your real life, a new identity card (in the classroom materials), or something imagined. Opinion tasks always allow an assigned side ("You're on Team Kestrel today"). Anyone can say "Pass!"

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (one set for the board): the suffix and prefix cards (color-code them if you can: nouns blue, adjectives green, adverbs orange, verbs red), the difference-scale cards and the question-opener cards. Card stock helps. Cut them before C17.
  • For C17: one pair of Word Family Grid cards (A + B) per pair; one set of Prefix/Suffix Race cards (20 base cards + 27 affix cards) per team of 3–4 (an envelope per team); one Comeback Story bingo grid per learner (two versions, so neighbors differ) and one set of comeback scenario cards.
  • For C18: one Compare & Recommend fact sheet per learner (A and B sheets for three topics: phones, jobs, towns) and one set of client cards per pair. Put the job and product picture cards on the board.
  • For C19: one set of Polite Enquiry role-play cards per group of 3 (A = customer or candidate, B = staff or interviewer, C = observer with the checklist). Tape the opener cards on the wall where everyone can see them.
  • Print the new identity cards and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking. In C19 they double as the candidate profiles for the interview role-play.
  • Optional: a desk bell or a phone for the role-plays ("Good morning, Riverside Community Center, how can I help?"). Learners enjoy the realism, and it makes the register clear.

Module at a Glance

Lesson Plans

6.1 · C17

Word Formation — Prefixes, Suffixes & Word Families

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • recognize and build word families: the noun, verb, adjective and adverb from one base (decide → decision → decisive → decisively)
  • use a suffix to identify a word's job: -tion / -sion, -ment, -ness, -ity, -er / -or (nouns) · -ful, -less, -able / -ible, -ive, -ous, -al (adjectives) · -ly (adverbs) · -ize, -en, -ify (verbs)
  • choose the right negative prefix (un-, in-, im- before m / p, il- before l, ir- before r, dis-, non-) and use re-, over-, under-, mis-, pre-
  • fill a gap with the correct form in two steps: (1) what job does the slot need? (2) build the form
  • hear and say the stress shift in a family (PHOtograph → phoTOgraphy → photoGRAPHic) and tell a short comeback story using six or more family members
Materials
  • Workbook 6.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Comeback Story)
  • Slides 3–7 · Suffix and prefix flashcards (color-coded) · Word Family Grid A/B cards (1 pair per pair) · Prefix/Suffix Race cards (1 envelope per team) · Comeback Story bingo (1 per learner) + scenario cards · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 6.1-A (where's the stress?) and 6.1-B (Race round 3: gap sentences)

Left: the word sum, big: un + success + ful + ly = unsuccessfully. Under it, the family tree: decide → decision (N) · decisive (Adj) · decisively (Adv) · indecisive (Neg).

Center: four colored columns, "The ending tells you the job": N -tion -ment -ness -ity -er · ADJ -ful -less -able -ive -ous -al · ADV -ly · V -ize -en -ify. Under it the stress rule: -tion, -ic, -ity pull the stress to the syllable just before them: PHOtograph → phoTOgraphy → photoGRAPHic.

Right: in- in disguise: impossible (p) · illegal (l) · irregular (r) · incorrect. Others: re- (again) · over- (too much) · under- (too little) · mis- (wrongly) · pre- (before). A red box: ⚠️ invaluable = VERY valuable!

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: all, both, neither7S–S → T–SReview C16 (5.3, determiners and quantifiers). Pairs find things in common and write three true sentences about themselves (or their identity cards) with both of us / neither of us / all of us in this class / none of us / each of us: "Neither of us likes getting up early." Two pairs read their best sentence. Quick board fix of any "Both of us doesn't…".
Lead-in: one word becomes four5S–S → T–SWrite DECIDE on the board. Teams of 3 have 60 seconds to write every word they know built on it. Elicit: decision, decisive, decisively, indecisive, undecided, decided(ly). "How many words did we get from one? That's today: one word, a whole family." Show slide 3 (the word sum).
Word families & suffixes12T–SSlides 3–4. Meaning (same idea, different job), the family table (decide, success, create, strength, compete, economy), then the suffix columns with the colored flashcards. Pronunciation: the stress rule for -tion / -ic / -ity and the sound changes (decide → decision, strong → strength). CCQs (below). Play Script 6.1-A: learners mark the stressed syllable in the 👂 "Where's the stress?" box under the Workbook 6.1 Grammar box.
Drill5T–S → SFamily chain and stress drill (see Drill script). Keep it fast and rhythmic: clap the stressed syllable.
Word Family Grid (info gap)11S–S (pairs)Slide 6. Pairs get Grid A and Grid B (the same 10 families, different cells filled in). Without showing their card, they ask and spell: "What's the adjective from rely?" "Reliable: R-E-L-I-A-B-L-E." Both complete Workbook Ex 1. Then they check the one negative per row together. ICQs first. Early finishers add the stress marks.
Prefixes9T–SSlide 5. Negative prefixes and "in- in disguise" (say inpossible aloud and let learners feel why it became impossible). Then re-, over-, under-, mis-, pre-: prefixes change the meaning, not the job. The trap: invaluable = very valuable; flammable = inflammable. CCQs.
Prefix/Suffix Race11S–S (teams)Slide 6. Teams of 3–4 with an envelope of base and affix cards. Round 1 (3 min) "Flip it": T reads a base, the first team to hold up the right negative prefix card scores. Round 2 (4 min) "Build it": teams build as many real words as they can; other teams may challenge; an invented word (beautiless) loses a point. Round 3 (4 min) "Fit it": T reads Script 6.1-B sentences with a base in capitals; teams write the form that fits. ICQs first.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 2–5 alone (form the word, negative prefix, other prefixes, the comeback text), then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on. For every gap ask the class: "What job does the slot need?" before the answer.
Freer: Comeback Story12S → S–S (groups)Slide 7. (1) Each learner picks a scenario card (the night-market stall, the community garden, the soccer team, the bake sale, the neighborhood app, the second driving test) or a light real story, and writes 6 key words from the families (3 min). (2) Groups of 3–4: each learner tells the story in about 1 minute: what went wrong, how it was fixed, how it ended. Listeners mark every form they hear on their Comeback bingo (7 min). (3) The learner with the most bingo marks from one story nominates it; that storyteller retells the "turning point" sentence to the class (2 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SDelayed error correction: write 4 sentences you heard (2 good, 2 with errors, no names): "It was a success project." · "He explained it very clear." · "It's unpossible." · "The team was very disappointing." (= disappointed). Learners fix them and say what job the slot needed. Then one range upgrade: a plain sentence you heard, rebuilt with a family member ("They improved it" → "They made several key improvements").
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 6.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button and the voice/speed control. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total907 + 5 + 12 + 5 + 11 + 9 + 11 + 8 + 12 + 5 + 5
If you run long

This class has two games and a story task. If time is tight, cut Race Round 2 (Build it) and move Workbook Ex 4 to homework. Never cut the Comeback Story: it is where learners use the forms in connected speech instead of just recognizing them. With a very strong class, shorten the prefix presentation to 5 minutes; they will know most negatives already.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: a word family. One idea, several grammatical jobs. The idea stays the same; the ending changes the job in the sentence.

"She made a quick decision. She's very decisive. She acted decisively." Is the idea the same in all three? (Yes, deciding.) Which one is a thing, a noun? (decision) · Which describes a person? (decisive, an adjective) · Which describes how she acted? (decisively, an adverb) · So what changed? (Only the job, not the idea.)

Meaning: the suffix tells you the job.

"Kindness", "agreement", "ability": nouns or adjectives? (Nouns.) How do you know without a dictionary? (The endings: -ness, -ment, -ity.) · "Hopeless": full of hope or without hope? (Without.) · "Widen": a verb or an adjective? (A verb: make wider.) · "Carefully": how many l's? (Two: careful + ly.)

Meaning: prefixes. A prefix usually changes the meaning, not the job.

"Happy" and "unhappy": both adjectives? (Yes.) · "Overcook": too much or too little? (Too much.) · "I misread the email." Did I read it correctly? (No, wrongly.) · "Rebuild": for the first time? (No, again.) · "Her help was invaluable." Was her help useless? (No! Extremely valuable.)

Meaning: choosing the form for a gap.

"It was a ___ launch." (SUCCESS) What comes after the gap? (A noun, launch.) So what job does the gap need? (An adjective.) The answer? (successful) · "They finished it ___." What does the gap describe? (How they finished: a verb.) So? (An adverb: successfully.) · "The first attempt was ___." (SUCCESS) The story says it failed. So? (unsuccessful: adjective + negative.)

Form.

Nouns-tion / -sion · -ment · -ness · -ity · -er / -ordecision · development · kindness · ability · teacher
Adjectives-ful · -less · -able / -ible · -ive · -ous · -alcareful · careless · reliable · creative · dangerous · natural
Adverbsadjective + -ly (-y → -ily; -ful → -fully)quickly · easily · carefully
Verbs-ize (US) / -ise (UK) · -en · -ifymodernize · strengthen · simplify
Negativesun- · in- / im- (m, p) / il- (l) / ir- (r) · dis- · non-unfair · impossible · illegal · irregular · dishonest · non-smoker
Meaningre- again · over- too much · under- too little · mis- wrongly · pre- beforerewrite · overwork · underpaid · misunderstand · preview

Pronunciation. Teach one powerful rule: -tion / -sion, -ic and -ity pull the stress to the syllable just before them: eDUcate → eduCAtion · eCOnomy → ecoNOmic · creAtive → creaTIvity · PHOtograph → phoTOgraphy → photoGRAPHic. Endings like -ful, -less, -ness, -ment, -ly, -able usually don't move the stress: CAREful, CARElessly, HAPpiness, deVELopment. Sound changes to model: decide /dɪˈsaɪd/ → decision /dɪˈsɪʒən/ (the vowel changes and d → /ʒ/) · succeed → success · strong → strength /strɛŋkθ/ (hard: let learners say "streng-th" slowly) · -tion is always weak /ʃən/, never "tee-on." Prefixes are normally unstressed (unHAPpy, imPOSsible), but we stress them to contrast: "I said POSsible, not IMpossible!"

Drill script
  1. Family chain (hold up a base card): T: "decide" → S1: "decision" → S2: "decisive" → S3: "decisively" → S4: "indecisive." Then succeed, create, strong, care. Choral first, then around the room.
  2. Clap the stress: T: "PHOtograph" (clap on PHO) → S repeat. "phoTOgraphy" → "photoGRAPHic" → "phoTOgrapher." Then "eCOnomy → ecoNOmic," "comPETE → compeTItion → comPEtitive." Learners clap with you.
  3. What's the job? T says a word; learners hold up a colored card (or say N / V / ADJ / ADV): "happiness" → N · "widen" → V · "dangerous" → ADJ · "easily" → ADV · "agreement" → N · "simplify" → V.
  4. Flip it: T: "possible" → S: "impossible." "legal" → "illegal." "regular" → "irregular." "honest" → "dishonest." "correct" → "incorrect." "fair" → "unfair." Fast, then individuals.
  5. Fit it (open pairs → closed pairs): S1 says a base; S2 says a sentence frame; S3 fills it: "CARE" + "Please drive…" → "Please drive carefully."
Word Family Grid — how to run it
  • Pairs sit face to face. A holds Grid A, B holds Grid B. Both grids have the same 10 bases (decide, succeed, create, strong, compete, rely, care, attract, employ, responsible) with columns Noun · Verb · Adjective · Adverb · Negative. Each card shows about half the answers; the missing cells are on the partner's card.
  • Learners ask, answer and spell: "What's the noun from compete?" "Competition: C-O-M-P-E-T-I-T-I-O-N." They write the answers in Workbook Ex 1. No showing the card. (Spelling aloud is useful: many learners have never spelled -tion or -ible aloud.)
  • The Negative column is empty on both cards: pairs must agree on the right prefix together. This is where discussion happens ("Is it unresponsible?" "No, r, so ir-!").
  • Two cells are marked ✱ on both cards: families with two nouns (creation / creativity; employer / employee / employment). Ask pairs what the difference is.
  • Dominant speakers: A asks all the Noun and Verb questions, B all the Adjective and Adverb questions, so both partners talk equally.
Prefix/Suffix Race — how to run it
  • Teams of 3–4. Each team has an envelope: 20 base cards (possible, legal, regular, honest, agree, fair, correct, understand, cook, pay, write, view, care, hope, help, use, kind, create, rely, danger) and 27 affix cards (prefixes un-, in-, im-, il-, ir-, dis-, non-, re-, over-, under-, mis-, pre- and suffixes -tion, -ment, -ness, -ity, -er, -ful, -less, -able, -ive, -ous, -al, -ly, -ize, -en, -ify).
  • Round 1 · Flip it (3 min): T reads a base; the first team to hold up the correct negative prefix card and say the word ("Illegal!") scores 1. Wrong prefix = the next team can steal.
  • Round 2 · Build it (4 min): teams lay out as many real words as they can with base + affix cards (up to two affixes, e.g., un + help + ful). 1 point per real word. Then teams walk around and challenge words they don't believe: a phone dictionary settles it. An invented word (beautiless, unpossible, helpness) = −1 point. The answer key has the full list of accepted words.
  • Round 3 · Fit it (4 min): T reads the 8 sentences in Script 6.1-B, saying "BLANK" for the gap and the base in capitals. Teams write the form on paper and hold it up. 2 points for the right form, 1 for the right job with a spelling slip.
  • Give the strongest speaker the referee job for Round 2 (with the answer list): they check challenges and add scores. It keeps them busy and gives the quieter learners the building.
Comeback Story — how to run it
  • The model is the online lesson's passage: "Despite careful preparation, the first launch was a complete failure. The team felt disappointed, but they responded decisively…". Read it aloud and ask: "How many forms can you hear?"
  • Scenario cards give a story frame with invented people: the night-market stall that got no customers in the first month, the community garden that flooded, the soccer team that lost every game, the bake sale with burned cakes, the neighborhood app that nobody downloaded, the second driving test. Learners can also tell a real, light story, or use their identity card.
  • Each teller must use at least six forms from two or more families (e.g., fail, failure, disappointed, decision, decisively, improvement, succeed, successful). Listeners mark every form they hear on their bingo grid. Three in a row = say "Bingo!" quietly after the story, not during it.
  • Talking-turn token: only the teller speaks; after the story each listener asks one follow-up question. The learner who talked longest in the last round tells their story last.
ICQs before the Grid, the Race and the Story
Grid: Can you show your card? (No.) · What do you do after you say the word? (Spell it.) · Who decides the negative? (Both of us, together.)
Race: Round 1: what do you hold up? (The negative prefix card.) · Round 2: do invented words score? (No, minus one!) · Who checks a challenge? (The dictionary / the referee.) · Round 3: what do you write? (The form that fits the sentence.)
Story: How many forms from the families? (Six or more.) · What do listeners do? (Mark the forms on the bingo.) · When do you say "Bingo"? (After the story.) · Does it have to be your real life? (No: a card, real, or imagined.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on four families (decide, succeed, care, strong) and four endings (-tion, -ful, -ly, un- / im-). Give the colored suffix cards as a strip on the desk: blue = thing, green = describes a person or thing, orange = describes how. In the Grid, the learner asks and their partner spells, then the learner copies. In the Race, pair them with a patient speller and let them do Round 1 (listening, no writing). Target: 4 correct gap-fills and a 3-sentence comeback story with fail → decided → successful. Spelling reminders: -ful has one l, -fully two.

Stretch

Push toward C1: less transparent suffixes (-ance / -ence, -hood, -ship, -dom: reliance, childhood, leadership, freedom); words that change job with no suffix but move the stress (a REcord / to reCORD, a PREsent / to preSENT, an INcrease / to inCREASE); and collocations with the family (make a decision, reach an agreement, a significant improvement). In the Race, they may use two affixes only. In the story, they add one -ance / -ence noun and one stress-shift pair. Early finishers write a 6-gap word-formation exercise for a classmate.

L1 watch-outs
  • Wrong part of speech: "a success project," "He explained very clear," "a decide leader." Chinese, Vietnamese and Thai words don't change form for their job, so the ending feels optional. Ask the two-step question every time: "What job does the slot need?"
  • Spanish, Portuguese, French and Italian cognates are a gift (-ción → -tion, -dad / -ité → -ity, -oso → -ous) and a trap: sensible ≠ sensible (Spanish = sensitive), actually ≠ actualmente (= currently). The prefix des- leads to "disfortunate" (desafortunado), "discomfortable," "disagradable": English uses un- (unfortunate, uncomfortable, unpleasant).
  • Arabic and Hebrew build words from roots and patterns, so learners understand families quickly, but the English suffixes are unfamiliar: expect "responsibleness," "successness." Praise the logic; give the real word.
  • Stress in the same place every time: French speakers stress the last syllable (photograPHY), Polish the second-last, Czech, Hungarian and Finnish the first. Learners from these languages often keep one stress position across the family. Clap the stress; it is a listening skill as much as a speaking one.
  • Spelling: "carefull," "successfull," "hopefuly," "responsable" (Spanish, French, Portuguese responsable). Point at the -ful / -fully rule and the -ible in responsible, possible, visible.
  • -ed / -ing adjectives slip in: "The team was disappointing" (meaning disappointed). Many languages use one form for both. Quick check: "Who felt it? → -ed. What caused it? → -ing."
Homework

Online lesson 6.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_16.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, copying the stress; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 6.1: the word family map (six bases useful for you, each with noun, verb, adjective, adverb and a negative, with the stress marked), write your comeback story in 6–8 sentences using at least six forms, "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C decisively · Q2 B irresponsible · Q3 (new) B invaluable (= extremely valuable). Script 6.1-B (Race Round 3): 1 improvement · 2 unreliable · 3 creativity · 4 strengthen · 5 competitive · 6 misunderstood · 7 carelessly · 8 employees.

6.2 · C18

Advanced Comparison

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • show how big a difference is: far / much / a lot / considerably / significantly + comparative (big) · slightly / a bit / a little / marginally + comparative (small), and never "very + comparative"
  • use not nearly as… as and nowhere near as… as (much less) and not quite as… as (almost as)
  • compare with as… as, multiples (twice / three times / half as… as), the same as, similar to, different from, choosing than or as correctly
  • link two changes with the + comparative…, the + comparative (The earlier we leave, the less traffic we'll hit.) and describe a trend with comparative and comparative (more and more expensive)
  • compare two products, jobs or towns and recommend one to a "client," using six or more different structures
Materials
  • Workbook 6.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, Compare & Recommend notes)
  • Slides 8–12 · Difference-scale flashcards · Job and product picture cards · Compare & Recommend fact sheets (A and B for phones, jobs and towns) + client cards · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 6.2-A (how big is the difference?) and 6.2-B (the highway or the coast road?)

Left: the difference line, a long arrow from tiny to huge: marginally · slightly · a bit · a little | … | a lot · much · far · considerably · significantly · (the other way: nowhere near as / not nearly as). Under it, crossed out in red: very cheaper · more better · more faster.

Center: two columns: THAN after -er / more / less · AS with as… as / the same as / twice as… as. Examples: far cheaper than · twice as big as · the same as · similar to · different from.

Right: The more you practice ↗ | the better you get ↘ (two halves, two "the"s, two comparatives). Under it: better and better · more and more expensive (a trend).

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: family relay8S–S → T–SReview C17. Pairs show each other their homework word family maps and read one sentence from their comeback story. Then a fast relay: T calls a base and a job ("SUCCESS, adverb!"); the first team to shout "successfully" scores. Finish with three stress-shift claps (PHOtograph → phoTOgraphy).
Lead-in: which would you buy?5T–SSlide 8 heading, and two invented phones on the board: Nova S $320 · Kestrel 8 $640. "Is the Kestrel more expensive?" (Yes.) "Just more expensive?" Elicit much / a lot more; then "It's exactly…?" (twice as expensive). Then battery: 48 h vs 46 h: "Is the Nova's battery much better?" (No, slightly.) "Today: not that it's different, but how much."
Grammar: the size of the difference12T–SSlide 8. Build the difference line on the board with the scale flashcards. The very trap. Then not nearly as / nowhere near as (a big gap: much less) vs not quite as (a small gap: almost). CCQs (below). Then Script 6.2-A: learners mark each sentence on the line in Workbook Ex 3.
Drill6T–S → SScale drill with the cards and the stress on the intensifier (see Drill script).
Grammar: as… as, multiples, same8T–SSlide 9. as… as (equal), not as… as (less), twice / three times / half as… as, the same as · similar to · different from. The than or as? rule. Weak forms: as /əz/, than /ðən/. CCQs.
Controlled practice8S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1, 4 and 5 alone (complete, rewrite, find the mistake), then check in pairs. Board-check Ex 5: learners come up and correct the red-pen errors.
Grammar + game: the more…, the more…10T–S → S–SSlide 10. Two halves, two thes, two comparatives; the short fixed forms (the sooner, the better; the more, the merrier); then trends: better and better, more and more expensive. CCQs. Then Consequence Chain in groups of 4 (4 min): S1 "The more you practice, the better you get." S2 "The better you get, the more confident you feel." S3 "The more confident you feel…" How long can the chain go? Workbook Ex 2 as a quick check.
Listening: the highway or the coast road?5T–S → S–SSlide 11. Read Script 6.2-B twice (two voices, or with a confident learner). First listening: which road do they choose, and why? Second: learners tick the comparison phrases they hear in the workbook dialogue box, then read it in pairs.
