Without showing their sheet, they exchange facts and compare aloud ("Mine's twice as expensive, but the camera is far better."). Then they take a client card ("a delivery driver who uses maps all day") and agree on a recommendation. Round 2 (7 min) new partner, jobs or towns. Report (4 min): pairs join into fours and each learner gives their partner's recommendation in 3–4 sentences with one the more…, the more…. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SBoard: 4 heard sentences (no names), e.g. "It's very cheaper." · "The Kestrel is two times more expensive than the Nova." (accept, then show twice as expensive as) · "The same than mine." · "More you pay, more you get." Learners fix them. Then one range upgrade: "much better" → "considerably better / nowhere near as good."
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 6.2; workbook "Compare & Recommend" write-up and the journal.
Total908 + 5 + 12 + 6 + 8 + 8 + 10 + 5 + 18 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Do only one round of Compare & Recommend (the phones) and move Workbook Ex 4 to homework. Never cut the recommendation step: comparing is only useful when it leads to a decision, and "So overall, I'd say…" is the moment the structures come together. With a strong class, shorten the as… as stage to 4 minutes (it is revision).

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: the size of the difference.

"The Kestrel is far more expensive." Is the difference big or small? (Big.) · "The Nova is slightly heavier." Would you notice it in your hand? (Probably not; it's a small difference.) · Can I say "very more expensive"? (No! much / far more expensive.) · Can I say "very expensive"? (Yes: no comparative, so very is fine.)
"The movie was nowhere near as good as the book." Which was better? (The book.) A little better or a lot? (A lot.) · "The bus is not quite as fast as the train." Is the bus much slower? (No, only a little.) · Which is the bigger gap: not quite as or not nearly as? (Not nearly as.)

Meaning: multiples and "the same".

"Rent in the city is twice as high as in the town." The town: $900. The city? ($1,800.) · On the fact sheets: $1,500 and $950. Twice as high? (Not quite: almost twice as high.) · "The trip took half as long as we expected." We expected 4 hours. It took? (2 hours.) Faster or slower than we thought? (Faster.) · "Her salary is the same as his." Is one higher? (No, equal.)

Meaning: the parallel comparative.

"The more you practice, the better you get." Two things change: what are they? (Practice and skill.) Do they change together? (Yes: more practice → more skill.) · "The older I get, the less I worry." Getting older → worrying more or less? (Less.) · "The sooner, the better." What's missing? (…we leave / it happens. The listener knows.)
"Prices are getting higher and higher." One change or a continuing trend? (A trend: it keeps going.) · "more and more expensive": why not "expensiver and expensiver"? (Long adjective → more and more + adjective.)

Form.

Big differencefar / much / a lot / considerably / significantly + comparative + thanThis laptop is far more powerful than my old one.
Small differenceslightly / a bit / a little / marginally + comparative + thanThe blue jacket is slightly cheaper than the black one.
Much lessnot nearly / nowhere near as + adjective + asThe sequel was nowhere near as good as the original.
Almost asnot quite as + adjective + asThe bus isn't quite as fast as the train.
Equal / less(not) as + adjective / adverb + asHe works as hard as anyone. · She isn't as tall as her sister.
Multiplestwice / three times / half + as… asTheir house is three times as big as ours.
Same / similarthe same as · similar to · different fromYour phone is the same as mine.
Parallelthe + comparative (+ clause), the + comparative (+ clause)The bigger the car, the more gas it uses.
Trendcomparative and comparative · more and more + long adjectiveHer English is getting better and better.
Adverbsmore quickly / more carefully · faster / harder / betterShe types more quickly than I do.

Pronunciation. Stress the intensifier, because it carries the message: "It's FAR more expensive." · "It's only SLIGHTly heavier." · "It's NOWhere near as good." than and as are weak: /ðən/, /əz/: "as tall as" = /əz ˈtɔl əz/, "cheaper than" = /ˈtʃipər ðən/. Twice as links: /ˈtwaɪsəz/. The parallel comparative has two tone units: the voice rises at the end of the first half and falls at the end of the second: "The more you PRACtice ↗ | the BETter you get ↘." A short pause at the comma helps listeners. In American English, better and better uses the flap: /ˈbɛɾər ən ˈbɛɾər/.

Drill script
  1. Scale drill (point at a card on the difference line): T: "cheaper, big" → S: "far cheaper / much cheaper." T: "cheaper, small" → S: "slightly cheaper." T: "good, much less" → S: "nowhere near as good." T: "fast, almost" → S: "not quite as fast." Choral, then individuals, stressing the intensifier.
  2. Very? No! T: "very cheaper" → S: "much cheaper!" T: "very expensive" → S: "OK!" T: "very more reliable" → S: "far more reliable!"
  3. Numbers → multiples: T: "$300 and $600" → S: "twice as expensive as." "100 and 300" → "three times as big as." "4 hours and 2 hours" → "half as long as."
  4. Than or as? T: "bigger…" → S: "than." T: "as big…" → S: "as." T: "the same…" → S: "as." T: "similar…" → S: "to." T: "different…" → S: "from."
  5. Rise–fall: choral "The sooner we leave ↗ | the better ↘." Then open pairs: S1 starts, S2 finishes: S1 "The more you read ↗…" S2 "…the more you learn ↘."
Compare & Recommend — how to run it
  • Three topics, each with an A and a B fact sheet: phones (Nova S vs Kestrel 8), jobs (Warehouse Team Lead at Brightline vs Delivery Driver at QuickRoute), towns (Riverton vs Millbrook). All invented. The numbers are designed so learners need the target language: $320 vs $640 (twice as), 180 g vs 185 g (slightly), 45 minutes vs 10 minutes (far longer / nowhere near as short).
  • Partners don't show their sheets. They tell each other the facts and compare aloud. Each sheet has a checklist of six structures at the bottom: learners tick one each time they use it.
  • Then the pair draws a client card (e.g., "a student on a tight budget," "a nurse who works nights," "a retired couple who don't drive") and must agree on one recommendation, ending with "So overall, I'd recommend… because…". If they disagree, they can each argue a side; that's good B2 practice.
  • Keep the comparisons neutral: invented products, invented job ads, invented towns. Nobody compares their own salary, their own country or their own town.
  • Dominant speakers: the partner with the quieter voice reads the client card and makes the final decision ("You've convinced me" or "Not yet!"). The talkative partner must persuade them.
ICQs before Compare & Recommend
Can you show your fact sheet? (No, tell your partner.) · How many different structures do you try to use? (Six: tick them.) · What does the client card tell you? (Who we are recommending for.) · How do you finish? ("So overall, I'd recommend…") · Do we talk about our own salary? (No, only the card.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with three frames only: "X is much ___er than Y." · "X is slightly ___er than Y." · "X is twice as ___ as Y." Give the phones sheet only, with the numbers circled and a picture cue for each row (💲 price, 🔋 battery, 📷 camera, ⚖️ weight). The learner can point at the difference line while speaking. Target: 4 spoken comparisons and one recommendation ("I recommend the Nova because it's much cheaper.").

Stretch

Push toward C1: by far the best, every bit as good as, nothing like as expensive as, a great deal more, not remotely as, and the parallel comparative with a noun (the more money you earn, the more tax you pay). Ask them to hedge the recommendation (On balance… / If budget is the main concern…). Early finishers describe the trend table on their town sheet in four sentences (Rents have become more and more expensive: they're now almost twice as high as in 2018.).

L1 watch-outs
  • than / that / as: Spanish and Portuguese use que for both "than" and "that," so learners write "bigger that." French uses que for both "than" and "as" (plus grand que, aussi grand que): "as big than." Use the board rule: -er / more → than; as / same → as.
  • "the same than" / "the same like": Spanish lo mismo que, German gleich wie, Russian такой же, как. Drill "the same as" as one chunk.
  • Dropping the "the"s: Spanish cuanto más… más, French plus… plus, German je… desto, Russian чем… тем, Chinese 越…越 and Korean -ㄹ수록 have no article: "More you practice, better you get." Point at the two "the"s on the board.
  • "two times more expensive": very common for Russian (в два раза дороже), Chinese, Spanish and Arabic speakers. It is heard in real English and is understood; accept it in speech, and teach twice as expensive as as the clear, standard form.
  • Double comparatives: "more better," "more cheaper," "very cheaper": Arabic, Tagalog and many other speakers. Also Chinese and Japanese learners may skip -er (比 / より do the job): "It's cheap than mine."
  • "The more you practice, the more you get better": logical, but English prefers "the better you get." Recast gently.
Homework

Online lesson 6.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_17.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back, stressing the intensifier and pausing between the halves of the more…, the more…; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 6.2: write up one Compare & Recommend (from class, or two things you know well, such as two apps or two stores) in 6–8 sentences with at least six different structures and a clear recommendation, "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B This one is far more expensive. · Q2 A The more · Q3 (new) C The Kestrel is twice as expensive as the Nova. Script 6.2-A (difference line): 1 big · 2 small · 3 much less · 4 almost the same · 5 big · 6 equal.

6.3 · C19

Asking Skillfully — Indirect & Negative Questions

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • turn a direct question into a polite indirect question: opener + statement word order, no do / does / did (Do you know what time it opens?)
  • use if / whether for yes/no questions, with or not in the right place (whether or not…, if… or not)
  • use negative questions to show surprise (Didn't you get my message?), invite agreement (Isn't it beautiful?), check a belief (Isn't the office closed on Mondays?) and persuade (Don't you think…? Wouldn't it be easier to…?), and answer them by the facts
  • ask subject questions with no auxiliary (Who broke the window?) and object questions with one (What did she break?)
  • sound polite with their voice (wide pitch, a gentle rise, a soft fall-rise) in a customer-service call and a job interview
Materials
  • Workbook 6.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, dialogue, role-play planner)
  • Slides 13–17 · Opener flashcards (on the wall) · Polite Enquiry role-play cards (6 customer-service + 2 job-interview scenarios, A / B / observer) · New identity cards (candidate profiles)
  • Listening scripts 6.3-A (a call to Riverside Community Center), 6.3-B (rise or fall? negative questions) and 6.3-C (the office mystery)

Left: the "sandwich": [Could you tell me] + where the station is + ? Under it: Where is the station? → …where the station is. · What time does it open? → …what time it opens. (cross out does in red) · Are they open? → …if / whether they're open.

Center: the opener ladder, from casual to careful: Do you know…? · Could you tell me…? · Do you have any idea…? · Would you mind telling me…? · I was wondering if… · I'd like to know… (statement openers → period OK).

Right: negative questions with arrows: Isn't it beautiful? ↘ (agree with me!) · Isn't the office closed on Mondays? ↗ (I think so: check) · Didn't you get my message? ↗ (surprise) · Don't you think we should wait? ↘ (persuade). Answer by the FACTS: I got it → Yes, I did. · I didn't → No, I didn't. Under it: Who broke it? (no did) vs What did she break?

Stage plan

Lead-in: two ways to ask5T–SSlide 13. Act two moments: to a friend, casually, "Where's the restroom?"; to a stranger in a hotel lobby, "Excuse me, could you tell me where the restroom is?" "Who am I talking to each time? Which is better for a stranger? What's different?" Let learners notice the opener and the word order (is moved to the end). Link: "You met this word order in B1: reported questions."
Grammar: indirect questions12T–SSlides 13–14. If you run long

This class has three grammar points. If time is tight, teach subject questions in 3 minutes (the board contrast + two examples) and move Workbook Ex 5 to homework; do two role-play rounds (one customer-service, one interview). Never cut the interview round: it is the most useful real-life task in the module, and it recycles the comparison language from C18.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: why indirect? The opener softens the question. It adds a little distance and courtesy: ideal with strangers, at work, on the phone, in emails. With friends, direct questions are normal and not rude.

"Could you tell me where the station is?" Is the speaker asking if you can tell them? (No, they want the information: where the station is.) · Is this more polite than "Where's the station?" (Yes, better for a stranger.) · Would you say it to your brother at home? (Probably not. It would sound funny, too formal.)
"Do you know what time it opens?" What was the direct question? ("What time does it open?") Where did does go? (Gone. The -s moved to opens.) · "I was wondering if the bus stops here." Is this a yes/no question or a wh- question? (Yes/no: "Does the bus stop here?") So we add…? (if / whether.)

Meaning: negative questions. They usually don't ask for new information. They show what the speaker already thinks or feels.

"Isn't it beautiful? ↘" Does she really want to know if it's beautiful? (No, she thinks it is; she wants me to agree.) · "Didn't you get my message? ↗" What did he expect? (That I got it.) How does he feel? (Surprised, maybe a little worried.) · "Don't you think we should wait? ↘" Is it really a question? (Not really: a suggestion; he wants to wait.)
"Didn't you get my message?" You did get it. Yes or no? (Yes, I did.) · You didn't get it. Yes or no? (No, I didn't.) · So what does "yes" always mean in English? (It's true / it happened.) · Why add "I did / I didn't"? (It removes any doubt.)

Meaning: subject questions.

"Who broke the window?" Do we know who broke it? (No, that's the question.) Is who the doer? (Yes.) So: did? (No.) · "What did she break?" Who's the doer? (She is.) So: did? (Yes.) · Answer both: "Maria broke the window." · "She broke the window." Where does the answer go? (Front = subject question; after the verb = object question.)

Form.

Wh- indirectopener + wh- word + subject + verb (no do / does / did)Where is the station? → Could you tell me where the station is? · How much did it cost? → Do you know how much it cost?
Yes/No indirectopener + if / whether + subject + verbDoes the bus stop here? → Could you tell me whether the bus stops here?
or notwhether or not + clause · if + clause + or notDo you know whether or not they're open? · …if they're open or not?
OpenersDo you know…? · Could you tell me…? · Do you have any idea…? · Would you mind telling me…? · I was wondering if / whether… · I'd like to know…Statement openers end with a period (a question mark is common too).
Negative questionsnegative auxiliary + subject + verbIsn't it…? · Didn't you…? · Haven't we…? · Wouldn't it be…? · Don't you think…?
Answersby the facts: Yes = true / happened · No = not true / didn't happen"Didn't you go?" (I went.) → Yes, I did. · (I didn't.) → No, I didn't.
Subject questionsWho / What + verb (no auxiliary)Who lives here? · What happened next? · Who left the door open?
Object questionsWho / What + do / does / did + subject + verbWho do you live with? · What did she break?

Pronunciation. In English, politeness lives in the voice as much as in the words. (1) Indirect questions with a question opener (Could you tell me…? Do you know…?) are grammatically yes/no questions: a gentle rise at the end sounds polite and open ("Could you tell me where the STAtion is? ↗"); a fall is also normal and sounds more businesslike. Start a little higher than usual and use a wide pitch range: a flat, low voice sounds bored or annoyed, even with perfect grammar. (2) Statement openers (I was wondering if…) often take a soft fall-rise ↘↗ at the end: tentative and very polite ("I was wondering if you had any OPEnings ↘↗"). (3) Negative questions: stress the negative auxiliary (DIDn't you…, ISn't it…, DON'T you think…). A fall means "I'm sure; agree with me" (Isn't it beautiful? ↘ · Don't you think we should wait? ↘); a rise means "I thought so; is it true?" or surprise (Isn't the office closed on Mondays? ↗ · Didn't you get my message? ↗). Link to B1 question tags: same rule. (4) Weak forms and linking: Could you /ˈkʊdʒə/, Would you /ˈwʊdʒə/, Do you know /dʒəˈnoʊ/, Didn't you /ˈdɪdn̩tʃə/, Don't you /ˈdoʊntʃə/; whether = weather.

Drill script
  1. Sandwich it (point at an opener card): T: "Where is the pharmacy? · Could you tell me…" → S: "Could you tell me where the pharmacy is?" T: "What time does the library close? · Do you know…" → S: "Do you know what time the library closes?" T: "How much did the tickets cost? · Do you have any idea…" → S: "…how much the tickets cost?"
  2. If or whether: T: "Is this seat free? · I was wondering…" → S: "I was wondering if this seat is free." T: "Do they deliver? · Could you tell me…" → S: "…whether they deliver?" T: "…or not" → S: "Could you tell me whether or not they deliver?"
  3. The polite voice: T says one indirect question twice, flat and then warm (higher start, gentle rise). "Which one gets you help?" Choral ×3 with the warm version, then individuals, using a desk bell: T rings, a learner asks.
  4. Rise or fall? T: "Isn't it beautiful? ↘" → S: "Yes, it is!" T: "Didn't you get my email? ↗" → S (card says ✔): "Yes, I did. Sorry!" / (card says ✘): "No, I didn't. Can you resend it?"
  5. Doer or not? T gives an answer; learners make the question: "Tom called." → "Who called?" "Tom called his boss." → "Who did Tom call?" "The printer broke." → "What broke?"
Polite Enquiry role-play — how to run it
  • Six customer-service cards: the Riverside Community Center gym front desk, a hotel front desk (phone), Lakeview Community College admissions, the public library, a furniture store (delivery), an internet provider (slow Wi-Fi). Card A (customer) lists 6 things to find out, including one yes/no question (→ if / whether) and one belief to check with a negative question (Isn't it cheaper online?). Card B (staff) has all the answers, plus one upsell to try with Don't you think…? or Wouldn't it be better to…?. This brings back the C18 comparisons ("The annual plan is far better value").
  • Two job-interview cards: café shift supervisor and office assistant at a dental clinic. The interviewer (B) asks at least four indirect questions (Could you tell me what you enjoy about working with customers?) and checks two things from the candidate's profile with negative questions (Didn't you say you'd trained new staff? Haven't you worked weekends before?). The candidate (A) answers from an identity card, never from their real life, and answers the negative questions by the facts. At the end the candidate asks three indirect questions (I was wondering whether there's any paid training.).
  • Observer (C) has the checklist: 6 indirect questions? statement order? no do / did? if / whether? one negative question? polite voice (rise, wide pitch)? They give the feedback: one thing that was great, one thing to fix. Give the observer job to the strongest speaker in Round 1; it is a real, demanding job.
  • Keep every scenario routine: memberships, check-in times, delivery windows, a slow connection, a job interview. No debt, eviction, police, immigration, medical or benefits calls. The interview is always for an invented job, and the candidate always uses an identity card.
  • Planning time (2 minutes) is important at B2: learners write the key words of their questions, not full sentences, then speak. Writing full sentences turns the role-play into reading aloud.
ICQs before the role-play
How many indirect questions does A ask? (Six or more.) · What happens to does / did after the opener? (It disappears.) · When do you use if / whether? (For a yes/no question.) · What does B try to do? (Sell something with "Don't you think…?") · What does the observer do? (Listen, tick, and say one great thing and one thing to fix.) · In the interview, do you talk about your real life? (No, the identity card.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Use two openers only, Could you tell me…? and Do you know if…?, on a strip, with the "sandwich" picture (bread = opener, filling = where it is). Give the gym card (the simplest) with picture cues (🕘 hours, 💲 price, 🏊 pool, 🅿️ parking). The learner plays the customer first, with a supportive partner as staff. Skip subject questions if needed. Target: 4 indirect questions with statement order, and "Yes, I did / No, I didn't" answers.

Stretch

Push toward C1: Would you happen to know…? · I don't suppose you could tell me…? · I'd be grateful if you could let me know whether… (email) · What I'd really like to know is…; whether + to-infinitive (I'm not sure whether to upgrade); negative questions with a softener (Wouldn't it make more sense to…?). In the interview, they combine comparison and questions: Could you tell me whether the evening shift is much busier than the morning? Early finishers write the follow-up email in formal register (a preview of C20).

L1 watch-outs
  • Keeping the question order: "Could you tell me where is the station?" / "Do you know what time does it open?" Almost every learner does this, because the ear expects a question. Spanish, Portuguese, French, Italian and Arabic speakers may also transfer their own embedded-question order (in Spanish ¿Sabe dónde está la estación? the verb comes before the subject). Use the red pen and the sandwich.
  • No if: "Do you know is the shop open?" Russian (ли after the verb), Arabic (hal), Chinese and Vietnamese (a question particle) have no joining word like if. Drill "Do you know if…" as one chunk.
  • Answering negative questions: Japanese, Korean, Mandarin and Cantonese speakers (and many others) answer to agree with the question: "Didn't you go?" "Yes" = "Yes, you're right, I didn't." French (si) and German (doch) have a special word for "yes, I did," which English doesn't. Always teach the full answer: "Yes, I did / No, I didn't."
  • Politeness in the words, not the tune: learners whose languages show respect with verb endings, particles or pronouns (Japanese -masu, Korean -yo, Tagalog po / opo, Spanish usted, French vous, Russian вы) may rely on "please" alone and a flat voice. English puts politeness in the opener and the intonation. Model the difference without judging.
  • Auxiliary in subject questions: "Who did break the window?" / "What did happen?" Spanish, Portuguese, Chinese and many others. The quick test: "Is the question word the doer?" (Note: "Who DID break it?" with stress is correct but emphatic; don't teach it here.)
  • Negative questions can sound like complaints: "Didn't you read my email?" with a falling, heavy tone sounds like criticism. Learners who translate directly may sound rude by accident. Model the light, surprised rise.
Homework

Online lesson 6.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_18.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and copy the polite rise; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 6.3: "My real questions" (four direct questions you might ask this week at a store, station, office or online, rewritten as indirect questions with four different openers, plus one negative question and one subject question), "My new words" and the journal. Challenge: ask one real indirect question outside class and notice the reaction.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 C Could you tell me where the station is? · Q2 A Who broke the window? · Q3 (new) B "Yes, I did." Script 6.3-A (the six questions): what time the gym opens · how much a monthly membership costs · whether there are classes for beginners · if towels are provided · where I can park · whether I can try a class for free. Script 6.3-B: 1 ↘ agree · 2 ↗ surprise · 3 ↗ check · 4 ↘ persuade · 5 ↘ admire · 6 ↗ surprise.

Closing the Module

End of C19: Module 6 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18, "the right form, the right size, the right shape": decide → decision → decisive → decisively (word families) → far cheaper · slightly heavier · nowhere near as good · twice as big · the more…, the better (comparison) → Could you tell me where it is? · Isn't it…? · Don't you think…? · Who broke it? (questions). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you. Review it in the C20 warmer.

Watch for in Module 7: Lesson 7.1 (register) builds directly on today: indirect questions are one of the main tools of formal English, and the word-formation work returns in formal nouns (a decision was made, an improvement in…). Keep a 2-minute "make it polite" warmer (one blunt question → indirect) in C20–C22. The comparison structures are ideal for 7.3 (persuasion) and the Module 8 debate: remind learners to reach for far more… / nowhere near as… when they argue a case.

Final assessment (C26) covers all eight modules. From this module it tests: choosing the correct form of a base word in a gap (noun / verb / adjective / adverb / negative), negative prefixes (im- / il- / ir- / in- / un- / dis-), intensifiers and softeners with comparatives (and "very + comparative" as an error), not nearly / nowhere near as… as, multiples, the more…, the more…, than vs as, indirect questions (statement order, no do / did, if / whether), answering negative questions by the facts, and subject questions.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 7 — Communicating with Style

Three classes about how we say things, not just what we say. First, register: the four features that shift between casual and formal English (word choice, contractions, sentence length, set phrases), polite spoken requests, and the five-part formal email, practiced in a workplace register-switching workshop. Then storytelling: hooks, scene-setting, narrative connectors, the historic present, direct speech and listener reactions, practiced in an anecdote circle. Last, persuasion: signposting, claim + reason + evidence, rhetorical techniques, concede-and-counter and handling questions, practiced in a problem–solution pitch with audience Q&A.

3 lessons Lessons 7.1–7.3 Classes C20–C22 Weeks 10–11 90 min per class CEFR B2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. Every class follows the same shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill (choral, then individual) → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice (the big communicative task) → feedback with delayed error correction → homework.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes. In this module the freer task is long (20–22 minutes) on purpose: it is the lesson. Protect it; cut controlled practice to homework before you cut the task.
  • Present from slides/module-7-style.html. Print materials/module-7/classroom-materials.html before C20. It holds the flashcards, the new identity cards, the Register Switch situation cards and register-sort strips, the anecdote prompt cards, listener-reaction cards and story-arc planner, the pitch topic cards, objection cards and pitch planner, Persuasion Bingo, and the listening scripts.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each one has 🔊 Listen buttons, so learners can hear the model sentences and dialogues again at home and slow them down.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. B1 Module 7 practiced complaints at a counter and job interviews; B1 Module 5 and B2 Lesson 1.1 taught the narrative tenses; B2 Lesson 4.3 used a few presentation phrases; Lesson 6.3 (C19) taught indirect questions (Could you tell me whether…?), which are politeness and therefore register. Check what learners know in each lead-in and move faster where they are strong. The new B2 work is control: choosing a register on purpose and holding it, shaping a story for a listener, and building an argument that survives a question.
  • Fluency before accuracy. In every freer task, listen and take notes; don't stop a speaker to fix a tense or a preposition. The feedback stage is where you fix errors, without names. At B2, give feedback on two tracks: accuracy (what was wrong) and range (what could be richer). Keep a two-column notebook page: "Fix it" and "Upgrade it" (e.g. very angry → absolutely furious; and then… and then → just as… before I knew it; I think it's good → The main advantage is…). Praise the effect first ("Everyone laughed at your ending. That's a landed story.") and then the form.
  • Dominant speakers. All three tasks give one person the floor, so structure the turns. Use a talking-turn token in the anecdote circle, a time limit with a visible timer for pitches, and jobs for strong speakers: the range spotter (notes one great phrase per speaker), the timekeeper, or the panel chair who must call on a quiet person first. Say it kindly: "Great question, Mateo. Let's hold it and hear from someone who hasn't asked one yet."
  • Stretching strong learners toward C1. Every lesson has a stretch box. The C1 direction in this module is subtlety: a register between neutral and formal (the everyday workplace email), a story with a well-timed pause and an understated ending, and persuasion that anticipates the objection before anyone raises it ("You might be thinking…"). Don't add new grammar; ask for more precise choices.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British forms and conventions: Mr Clarke (no period), enquire / enquiry, Yours faithfully / Yours sincerely, recognise, apologise, colour, flavour, practise, mate, cheers, sorted, primary school, full stop, an engineer to fix the heating. Teach Mr., inquire / inquiry, the US sign-off Sincerely, (or Best regards,), period, elementary school, a technician / repair person. Present the British faithfully / sincerely rule as "what you'll see in UK letters and in the online lesson," and accept British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Fit the reader → hold the listener → move the audience. In C20 learners take one message and dress it three ways: a text to a friend, an email to a manager, a formal letter. The skill is choosing. In C21 they keep a small group listening to a two-minute story, and they learn to be a good listener too. In C22 they stand up (or sit tall) and persuade a panel to fund a community or workplace improvement, then defend it under questions. Each class uses the one before it: the pitch needs the neutral-to-formal register of C20, and a short anecdote ("Take my coworker Ana…") is one of the best pieces of evidence in a pitch.

Trauma-informed: whose story, whose opinion?

Anecdotes (C21) invite personal memories, and some learners' most "memorable" stories are about migration, danger, loss or officials. Keep every story light: a mix-up, a coincidence, a cooking disaster, a lost-and-found, a kind stranger, a funny misunderstanding at work. Offer three choices out loud: a true story, a story from a prompt card or new identity card, or an invented story. Listeners never ask "Is it true?" (put it on the board as a rule). Never ask for the story of the journey here or a first day in the country. Register (C20): the workplace situations include calling in sick. Teach that in English you don't have to explain ("I'm unwell and unable to come in today" is complete and professional); no one describes real symptoms. Persuasion (C22): pitch topics are everyday community and workplace improvements, drawn from cards. Teams argue for their card, not their personal beliefs, and panelists ask questions from objection cards, so nobody has to reveal an opinion. No politics, religion or immigration topics, even if a learner suggests one; say "Great energy. Let's keep that one for outside class" and give them a card.

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (set A, one set for the board): the informal → formal word pairs, the register dial, story connectors and openers, extreme-adjective picture cards, and the signpost and technique cards. Card stock helps.
  • For C20: one set of Register Switch situation cards (set C, 8 cards) per 3 groups, one three-column workshop sheet per group (or use Workbook 7.1 "Register Switch Workshop"), and one set of register-sort strips (set D) per pair in an envelope.
  • For C21: the anecdote prompt cards (set E, 12 cards) spread face-up on a side table, one story-arc planner per learner, one set of listener-reaction cards per circle of 4, and a talking-turn token per circle (a pen or a coin).
  • For C22: one pitch topic card per team of 3 (set F), one pitch planner per team, 2–3 objection cards per panelist, and one Persuasion Bingo grid per learner (set G; half version A, half version B). A phone timer you can show on screen.
  • Print the new identity cards (set B) and keep them on a side table where anyone can take one without asking. At B2 they are workplace personas with two story seeds each.
  • Optional: a real phone for the two voicemails in Script 7.1-A (read them holding the phone to your ear), and a small "podium" (a music stand or a box on a desk) for C22. It raises the stakes in a good way.

Lesson Plans

7.1 · C20

Register — Formal vs Informal English

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain register as a dial, not a switch, and place a sentence as informal, neutral or formal
  • shift the four features: word choice (ask for → request, put off → postpone), contractions (I'm → I am), sentence length (short and linked with and / so → longer with as, although, therefore), and set phrases (Just wondering… → I am writing to inquire about…)
  • soften a spoken request for a stranger or a manager with could, would, I was wondering whether, would it be possible to
  • build a five-part formal email: greeting → reason for writing → detail → request/action → sign-off, and match the sign-off to the greeting (US Sincerely,; UK Yours sincerely / faithfully)
  • take one workplace message and write it three ways (text to a friend, email to a manager, formal letter) without mixing registers
Materials
  • Workbook 7.1 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, "Two voices" dialogue, Register Switch Workshop sheet)
  • Slides 3–8 · Informal → formal word-pair flashcards · Register dial card · Register-sort strips (1 envelope per pair) · Register Switch situation cards (8) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 7.1-A (two voicemails: to a friend, to a manager) and 7.1-B (formal-phrase dictation)

Left: the dial, drawn as a long arrow: INFORMAL (text a friend) — NEUTRAL (talk to a colleague) — FORMAL (write to an official). Under it, one message three ways: Can you get back to me soon? · Could you let me know when you have a moment? · I would be grateful if you could reply at your earliest convenience.

Center: the four features with an arrow each way: 1 words: ask for ⇄ request · get ⇄ obtain / receive · find out ⇄ discover · put off ⇄ postpone · need ⇄ require · 2 I'm ⇄ I am · 3 short + and/so ⇄ longer + as / although / therefore · 4 Hi Sam, ⇄ Dear Mr. Clarke: · Thanks! ⇄ Thank you for your assistance.

Right: the email skeleton: ① Dear… ② I am writing to… ③ On May 3, I… ④ I would be grateful if you could… ⑤ I look forward to hearing from you. / Sincerely, (US) · UK: Dear Sir or Madam → Yours faithfully · Dear Mr Clarke → Yours sincerely. Bottom corner: ONE REGISTER PER MESSAGE.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: polite or blunt?8S–S → T–SReview C19 (6.3, indirect and negative questions). Board: three blunt questions: "Where's the bathroom?" · "When does the store close?" · "Is the manager here?" Pairs make each one polite with an indirect frame ("Could you tell me where… / Do you know whether…"). Then ask: "Who would you say the blunt one to? The polite one?" This is today's topic in one question: the words depend on the listener.
Lead-in: three messages, one meaning6T–SSlide 3. Show the three versions of Can you get back to me soon? Ask: "Which one is wrong?" (None!) "Who's the reader of each?" Elicit: a friend, a coworker, a company or an official. Draw the dial. Ask: "What happens if you send the formal one to your best friend?" (It sounds cold, maybe sarcastic.) "And the text to a bank?" (Rude, not serious.)
The four features14T–S → S–SSlides 4–5. Teach the four features with the board center. Word pairs: flip the flashcards (informal side → learners call out the formal side). Contractions and sentence length: read the two "heater" versions from the online lesson. Set phrases: the function table. CCQs (below). Then pairs do the register-sort strips (18 phrases → informal / neutral / formal columns, 4 min). Check with slide 5.
Spoken register + drill10T–S → SSlide 6. Read Script 7.1-A (two voicemails: Ana to her coworker friend, then Ana to her manager) holding a phone. Learners note 3 differences. Then the "Two voices" dialogue in the workbook in pairs, swapping roles. Drill the polite frames (see Drill script): choral, then individual, with falling-rising intonation.
The formal email8T–SSlide 7. The five parts, built on the board from Elena Marsh's complaint in the online lesson (Americanize it: Dear Customer Service Team, … Sincerely,). Then the US / UK sign-off note and the "one register per message" rule. Script 7.1-B (dictation of 4 formal phrases).
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1–4 alone (formal words, I / N / F, the inquiry email, soften the request), then check in pairs. Board-check only items pairs disagree on. Early finishers start Ex 5 (rewrite Ana's text to her manager).
Freer: Register Switch Workshop22S–S (groups of 3) → T–SSlide 8. Groups of 3 take a situation card (one workplace or everyday message). Each learner writes one version: A a text to a friend, B an email to a manager, C a formal letter or email to an organization (7 min). They pass the sheet: each learner checks the next version with the "four features" list and upgrades one line (4 min). Then each group reads its three versions aloud without saying which is which, in random order; the class names the reader and the feature that gave it away (8 min). Last 3 min: each group rotates one version down the dial (formal → neutral) on a new card. ICQs first.
Feedback7T–STwo columns on the board. Fix it (no names): "Dear Mr. Patel, … Cheers, Ana" · "I am writing for ask about…" · "I would be grateful if you can…" · "I'm writing to inform you I can't come." (in the formal letter). Upgrade it: "I want a refund" → "I would like to request a refund" · "The heater is broke" → "The heating is no longer working." Praise one group whose three versions were clearly different.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 7.1 on screen; point at a 🔊 button and the Audio control. Set the homework (below). Learners tick the "I can…" box in the workbook.
Total908 + 6 + 14 + 10 + 8 + 10 + 22 + 7 + 5
If you run long

Move Workbook Ex 3–4 to homework and do only Ex 1–2 in class. Never cut the Workshop's "read aloud and guess the reader" stage: it's where learners hear register, which is the whole point. With a strong class, shorten the register-sort to 2 minutes.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: register = fit, not quality. All registers are good English. The question is who the reader or listener is, how well you know them, and what the message is for.

"I would be grateful if you could reply at your earliest convenience." Is this better English than "Can you get back to me soon?" (No, just more formal.) Would you text it to your sister? (No, it would sound cold or sarcastic.) Would you write it to a bank? (Yes.) So what decides? (The reader and the situation.)

Meaning: the four features.

"find out" and "discover": same meaning? (Yes.) Which is more formal? (discover.) Is "find out" wrong in an email? (No, but it leans informal; in a formal letter choose the single verb.) · "I haven't received it" in a formal letter: OK? (Better: "I have not received it.") In a text to a friend? (Contractions are normal.)
"Sorry to bother you" and "I hope you don't mind my writing": which one for a company you've never contacted? (The second.) · A letter starts "Dear Sir or Madam" and ends "Cheers!" What's the problem? (Mixed registers.) What's the rule? (One register from greeting to sign-off.)

Meaning: polite spoken requests. Formal speech isn't about long words; it's about distance: past forms and modals give the other person an easy way to say no.

"I was wondering whether it might be possible to move our reservation." Is the guest talking about the past? (No! Now.) Why "was wondering"? (It's softer, more polite.) Is the guest sure the answer is yes? (No, "might" and "possible" leave room for no.) Would you say this to a close friend? (No, too stiff.)

Meaning: sign-offs, US and UK.

You're writing to a company in Chicago. How do you end? (Sincerely, or Best regards,.) A company in London, and you started "Dear Sir or Madam"? (Yours faithfully,.) You started "Dear Ms. Ford"? (Yours sincerely,.) Will an American reader mind "Yours sincerely"? (No. It's fine, just a little old-fashioned.)

Form.

Wordsphrasal verb / everyday word → single, often Latin-based verbask for → request · get → obtain / receive · find out → discover · put off → postpone · need → require · tell → inform · help → assist · but → however · so → therefore
Contractionsformal writing spells them outI'm → I am · haven't → have not · won't → will not
Sentencesshort + and / but / so → longer, linked with as, although, which, thereforeThe heater's broken. It's cold. → As the heating is no longer working, the apartment has become extremely cold.
Spoken requestsCould you…? · Would you mind + -ing? · I was wondering whether / if + past · Would it be possible to…? · …if that's not too much troubleI was wondering whether I could leave an hour early on Friday.
Email skeleton① Dear… ② I am writing to inquire about / complain about / request… ③ detail ④ I would be grateful if you could… / I would therefore request… ⑤ I look forward to hearing from you. + sign-offUS: Dear Mr. Clarke: (or ,) … Sincerely, · UK: Dear Mr Clarke, … Yours sincerely, · Dear Sir or Madam, … Yours faithfully,

Pronunciation. Register lives in the voice too. Casual speech is fast with heavy reductions (gonna, wanna, can ya, lemme know); polite speech is slower, with full forms and a gentle fall-rise on requests: "I was wondering whether it might be possible… ↘↗". A flat, fast "Could you move my appointment" sounds like an order; the same words with a softer, rising end sound like a request. In I would be grateful, would is weak /wəd/ and be is short; stress grateful. Inquire /ɪnˈkwaɪər/: stress on the second syllable. Contractions are fine in formal speech ("It's under Whitfield"); the no-contractions rule is for formal writing.

Drill script
  1. Flip the card (word-pair flashcards, informal side up): T: "ask for" → S: "request." T: "put off" → S: "postpone." T: "find out" → S: "discover." T: "need" → S: "require." T: "tell" → S: "inform." Then reverse: T: "assist" → S: "help." Choral, then fast individuals.
  2. Turn the dial up: T (casual voice): "Can you send me the form?" → S (polite): "Could you possibly send me the form?" → S2 (formal writing): "I would be grateful if you could send me the form."
  3. Soften it: T: "Move my shift." → S: "I was wondering whether I could move my shift." T: "Give me a day off." → S: "Would it be possible to take a day off on Friday?" Model the fall-rise; learners copy the tune, not just the words.
  4. Set-phrase ping-pong: T: "Hi…" → S: "Dear…" · T: "Let me know." → S: "I look forward to hearing from you." · T: "Thanks!" → S: "Thank you for your assistance." · T: "Talk soon." → S: "Please do not hesitate to contact me."
  5. Open pairs → closed pairs: S1 says a casual request to S2 ("Can I borrow your charger?"); S2 turns to S3 and "asks the manager" the same thing politely. Then all pairs, 1 minute.
Register Switch Workshop — how to run it
  • Groups of 3. Each group takes one situation card (set C): e.g. You can't work your Saturday shift and a coworker has agreed to cover it · The printer at work has been broken for a week · You'd like to take a first-aid course your employer mentioned · Your new washing machine was delivered with a dent. Every card has the core facts, so nobody has to invent personal details.
  • Roles: A writes a text to a friend or close coworker (1–3 lines), B an email to a manager (neutral-to-formal, 50–70 words), C a formal letter or email to an organization or company (five parts, 70–90 words). Rotate roles if the group does a second card.
  • Pass the sheet to the right. The checker uses the four features as a checklist (words · contractions · sentences · set phrases) and upgrades one line in a different color. They may not rewrite the whole thing.
  • Read-aloud round: the group reads its three versions in random order. The class says who the reader is and which feature gave it away ("It's the manager: no contractions but 'Hi Dan,' not 'Dear Mr.'"). This is the key learning moment: learners discover that the manager email is in the middle of the dial, and that's the one they'll write most in real life.
  • Dominant speakers: give the strongest writer role C (the hardest) and the job of reader for the read-aloud round only if a quieter group member doesn't want it. The quietest learner may choose role A: short, and a real skill. Everyone reads at least one line aloud.
  • Calling-in-sick card: remind the group that the message says "I'm unwell and unable to come in today", with no details. That is complete and professional in English.
ICQs before the Workshop
How many versions does your group write? (Three.) · Who writes the formal letter? (C.) · Does the text to a friend need "Dear"? (No!) · When you check a partner's version, do you rewrite everything? (No, upgrade one line.) · When you read aloud, do you say "This is the email to the manager"? (No, the class guesses.) · Do you need to use your real job or your real boss's name? (No, the card has everything.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with two ends of the dial only (text to a friend ⇄ formal email) and one feature at a time: first the set phrases, which carry most of the register. Give a phrase strip with the five email parts already started ("Dear…, / I am writing to… / I would be grateful if you could… / I look forward to hearing from you. / Sincerely,") so the learner fills in the facts. In the Workshop, take role A or B and copy the frame from the strip. Target: one text and one framed formal email, read aloud.

Stretch

Aim for the middle of the dial: the warm-but-professional email to a manager ("Hi Dan, I hope your week's going well. I wanted to check whether…"). Use would appreciate it if, further to our conversation, at your earliest convenience, and a formal passive ("The item was delivered damaged"). C1 step: rewrite one sentence three ways and say why each word changed. Early finishers write the company's polite reply to the complaint.

L1 watch-outs
  • Over-formal everywhere: learners from languages with strong formal address (Spanish/Portuguese usted/você/o senhor, Korean and Japanese honorifics, Arabic, Russian вы, Vietnamese kinship pronouns) often assume "formal = always safer" and write stiff texts to friends. Praise the formal version, then show the casual one is equally correct in its place.
  • Too direct in requests: Russian, Polish, German, Dutch, Arabic and Hebrew speakers may use a plain imperative or "I want" where English expects softening ("I want to change my shift"). It isn't rude in their L1; in English it can sound demanding. Teach I was wondering whether… as a chunk.
  • Over-long greetings and closings: Arabic, Persian, Urdu, Spanish and Tagalog letters often open with good wishes and close with blessings or long thanks. In English formal email, the purpose comes in the first line ("I am writing to…"). A short "I hope you are well" is fine in a manager email, not in a complaint.
  • "I am writing for ask" / "I would be grateful if you can": Spanish para preguntar, Portuguese para perguntar → "for ask"; teach writing to + verb. Keep would … could together: "I would be grateful if you could…".
  • Honorific without a surname: "Dear Mr. Daniel" (Tagalog, Arabic, Vietnamese, Thai and many African Englishes use a title + first name). In US formal email: Mr. / Ms. + last name, or the first name alone if the workplace uses first names.
  • Ms. vs Mrs. vs Miss: teach Ms. /mɪz/ as the safe default for any woman in business; learners from many backgrounds use Mrs. for all women.
Homework

Online lesson 7.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_19.html. Listen to the two exchanges and the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back in the matching voice; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. (Note for learners: the online lesson follows the British sign-off rule; in the US, Sincerely, works after any greeting.) Workbook 7.1: finish Ex 5 (Ana's message to her manager) if not done, write the project email (a complaint or an inquiry, 80–120 words) and read it aloud once at home, "My new words" and the journal (one real casual message rewritten formally, with the changed words listed).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Yours sincerely, (UK rule; in US English Sincerely,) · Q2 B I am writing to inquire whether any places are still available. · Q3 (new) C "Dear Ms. Ortiz, I am writing to request a copy of my contract. Thanks a bunch, see ya!" mixes registers. Script 7.1-A differences: Hey Sam, it's me / Hello, Mr. Patel, this is Ana Silva from the evening team · can't make it / I'm afraid I won't be able to · can you cover? / I was wondering whether it would be possible to… · Lemme know! / Please let me know if that works for you.

7.2 · C21

Telling Stories & Anecdotes

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • shape a spoken anecdote with an arc: open (a hook) → build (scene, then events) → climax → land (the point or punchline)
  • open with a hook (You'll never guess what happened… · So the other day… · This reminds me of the time…) and set the scene with the past continuous
  • control the pace with varied connectors (at first, then, a few minutes later, just as, all of a sudden, before I knew it) instead of "and then… and then"
  • bring the climax alive with the historic present, direct speech (he goes / she was like, informal speech only) and emphasis (absolutely furious, the worst… ever)
  • react as a listener (No way! · You must have been mortified. · What happened next?) and land the ending (In the end… · To cut a long story short… · And that's why…)
Materials
  • Workbook 7.2 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–5, Priya & Tom dialogue, story-arc planner)
  • Slides 9–13 · Connector and opener flashcards · Extreme-adjective picture cards (angry → furious, excited → thrilled, cold → freezing, hot → boiling, nervous → terrified) · Anecdote prompt cards (12) · Listener-reaction cards (1 set per circle) · Story-arc planners · Talking-turn tokens · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 7.2-A (the same story told flat, then shaped) and 7.2-B (a story with pauses for listener reactions)

Left: the arc drawn as a hill: OPEN (hook) ↗ BUILD (scene → events) ↗ CLIMAX (the big moment) ↘ LAND (point / punchline). Under OPEN: You'll never guess… · So the other day… · Did I ever tell you about…? Under LAND: In the end… · To cut a long story short… · And that's why… · Lesson learned!

Center: tenses on a timeline: scene = past continuous (I was walking home…) · events = past simple (a bus drove through a puddle) · backstory = past perfect (I'd just bought the suit) · climax = historic present (So I'm standing there, and he goes…). Connectors along the hill: at first → then → a few minutes later → just as → all of a sudden → before I knew it.

Right: the listener's column: 😮 No way! · 😂 That's hilarious! · 😬 Oh no! · You must have been mortified. · ➡️ Then what? · What did you do? Bottom: very furious → absolutely furious · and then… and then…

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: turn the dial8S–S → T–SReview C20. Pairs swap their homework emails and find the five parts, then point to one feature that makes it formal. Fast dial round: T reads a casual line ("Can't make it Friday, sorry!"); pairs say it to "the manager," then write it for "the company." Two volunteers read their best formal line.
Lead-in: flat vs shaped7T–SSlide 9. Read Script 7.2-A: first the flat version (monotone, "and then"), then the shaped version (with energy). "Same events? (Yes.) Which one would you listen to at a party? Why?" Elicit the differences onto the board: a hook, a scene, surprise words, what people said, an ending. Draw the arc.
Arc, openers, scene, connectors13T–SSlides 10–11. Openers and when to use them. Scene-setting with the past continuous (revisit B2 1.1: the narrative tenses; this is extension, so ask learners to tell you the tense). The connectors and their effect on pace: which ones slow down, which ones speed up. CCQs (below). Stick the connector cards along the arc.
Keeping it alive8T–SSlide 12. The historic present ("So I'm standing there…"), direct speech with goes / was like, and emphasis. Extreme adjectives with the picture cards: angry → furious, excited → thrilled, cold → freezing; the rule absolutely + extreme, very + normal. CCQs.
Drill6T–S → SSee Drill script: hooks with a suspense tune, the "just as" chain, the historic-present switch, and absolutely + extreme adjectives.
Controlled practice10S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1–4 alone (order the anecdote, connectors, tenses, extreme adjectives), then check in pairs. Pairs read the Priya & Tom dialogue with feeling: Priya rushes the exciting part; Tom reacts on time.
Listener reactions6T–S → SSlide 13. Read Script 7.2-B. At each pause (⏸), hold up a hand: learners call out a reaction; hold up a reaction card to prompt quieter learners. Drill the intonation of No way! ↗↘ and Oh no… ↘. Workbook Ex 5 orally.
Freer: Anecdote Circle22S (plan) → S–S (circles of 4) → T–S(1) Each learner chooses a story: true, a prompt card, a new identity card seed, or invented. They fill in the story-arc planner in key words only (5 min). (2) Circles of 4 with a talking-turn token and a set of reaction cards. Each teller has 2 minutes; listeners must use at least two reactions and ask one follow-up question at the end (12 min). (3) Each circle chooses one story for a "second telling": the teller moves to a new circle and tells it again, better and a bit shorter (3 min). (4) Two volunteers tell the class the best ending they heard, not the whole story (2 min). ICQs first.
Feedback5T–STwo columns. Fix it: "When I walked home, it started to rain." (scene → was walking) · "I was very furious." · "Suddenly, all of a sudden…" · "He said me, 'Is your car?'" Upgrade it: "and then… and then" → "just as… before I knew it" · "…so, yeah, anyway" → "And that's why I always…". Praise the best hook and the best listener.
Homework set-up5T–SOnline lesson 7.2; the story script mark-up; tell the story once at home or record it.
Total908 + 7 + 13 + 8 + 6 + 10 + 6 + 22 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Skip the "second telling" in the Anecdote Circle and move Workbook Ex 3–4 to homework. Don't cut the listener-reactions stage: without it, the circle turns into four monologues. If a story is running long, the timekeeper says "To cut a long story short…?" as a friendly signal to land it.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: a hook signals "a story is starting." Without it, the listener doesn't switch into listening mode.

"You'll never guess what happened to me this morning." Is this a real question? (No.) Does the speaker want you to guess? (No, to listen.) What's a good answer? ("What?" / "Go on!") · "This reminds me of the time…" When do you use it? (When the story links to what someone just said.)

Meaning: scene vs events.

"I was waiting for the bus when a dog ran off with my sandwich." Which action was in progress, the background? (Waiting.) Which one was short and sudden? (The dog ran.) Which tense for the background? (Past continuous.) · "Just as I opened the door, the phone rang." One thing after the other, or at the same moment? (The same moment.)

Meaning: the historic present.

"So I'm standing there, and this guy walks up and goes, 'Is this your car?'" Is this happening now? (No, it's a past story.) Why present tense? (It pulls the listener into the moment.) Is "goes" about walking? (No, here it means "says," informal.) Would you write this in a formal report to your manager? (No, spoken stories only.)

Meaning: extreme adjectives.

"I was absolutely furious." Stronger than "very angry"? (Yes.) Can I say "very furious"? (No, absolutely or really.) Can I say "absolutely angry"? (No, very angry.) · Freezing = very… (cold). Thrilled = very… (excited, happy).

Form.

Openhook phraseYou'll never guess what happened… · So the other day… · The funniest thing happened to me…
Scenepast continuous (+ when + past simple)We were sitting in a café when the lights went out.
Backstorypast perfectI'd just bought the suit the day before.
Buildvaried connectorsAt first… · Then… · A few minutes later… · Just as I… · All of a sudden… · Before I knew it…
Climaxhistoric present · direct speech · emphasisSo I'm standing there, soaked, and he goes, "Lovely weather!" · It was the worst day ever.
Extreme adj.absolutely / really + extreme · very + gradableabsolutely furious / thrilled / freezing / boiling / terrified / exhausted · very angry / excited / cold
Landending phrase + stopIn the end… · To cut a long story short… · And that's why… · I still can't believe it. · Lesson learned!

Pronunciation. A story is a performance. Teach three tools: (1) Pace: slower for the scene, faster at "all of a sudden," then a pause just before the punchline. (2) Pitch: a higher, wider pitch range for direct speech ("and he goes, 'Is this your car?'"). (3) Stress on the intensifier: "I was absolutely soaked." Hooks have a rising, inviting tune: "You'll never guess what happened ↗." No way! is a high rise then fall; Oh no… is low and falling. In fast speech, was is weak /wəz/: "I wəz walking home…"

Drill script
  1. Hook it (suspense tune): T: "I lost my keys." → S: "You'll never guess what happened: I lost my keys!" T: "A strange thing at work." → S: "The strangest thing happened at work…" Choral, then individuals with a dramatic face.
  2. Set the scene: T: "walk home · rain start" → S: "I was walking home when it started to rain." T: "cook dinner · power go out" → S: "I was cooking dinner when the power went out."
  3. Just as chain: S1: "Just as I sat down, …" S2 finishes: "…the phone rang." S2 starts a new one: "Just as I opened the fridge, …" Around the room.
  4. Switch to now: T: "He walked up to me and said, 'Is this your dog?'" → S: "So he walks up to me and goes, 'Is this your dog?'" Two or three more.
  5. Absolutely! (picture cards): T holds up angry: "Were you very angry?" → S: "Angry? I was absolutely furious!" · cold → "absolutely freezing" · excited → "absolutely thrilled" · nervous → "absolutely terrified."
Anecdote Circle — how to run it
  • Choosing a story. Spread the 12 prompt cards face-up (e.g. The wrong bus, A cooking disaster, The small world coincidence, Lost and found, The misunderstood message, A kind stranger, The pet that…). Each card has three story-starter facts, so an invented story is easy. Say: "True, card, or invented: all equal. Nobody will ask which."
  • Planning. The story-arc planner has five boxes (hook · scene · 2–3 events · climax with one line of direct speech · ending). Key words only; collect any full-sentence scripts before the telling. Learners talk from notes, not read.
  • Circles of 4. The teller holds the token. Listeners hold the reaction cards and must "spend" at least two during the story and ask one follow-up question at the end ("What did your boss say?"). The fourth person in each round is the range spotter: they write down one great phrase the teller used and read it back ("You said 'before I knew it'!").
  • Second telling. Research on fluency (the 4/3/2 technique) shows that telling the same story again, shorter, makes it faster and more accurate. Ask each circle's chosen teller to retell in 90 seconds in a new circle. Watch how much better the second version is and tell them so.
  • Dominant speakers: the 2-minute limit is firm (a phone timer in the middle of the circle). A strong storyteller becomes the range spotter in the first round so they listen first. A learner who dominates as a listener (interrupting) gets the job of asking the one follow-up question only.
  • Quiet learners may tell a 4-sentence version (hook, scene, one event, ending). That's a complete anecdote. Anyone can say "Pass" once and tell their story to you at the end instead.
ICQs before the Anecdote Circle
Does your story have to be true? (No: true, card, or invented.) · Do you write full sentences on the planner? (No, key words.) · How long is each story? (Two minutes.) · Listeners: how many reactions? (At least two.) And at the end? (One follow-up question.) · Can we ask "Is that true?" (No!) · Who holds the token? (The teller.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Use a picture prompt card and a 4-box planner with sentence starters printed: "You'll never guess… / I was ___ing when… / All of a sudden… / In the end…". Let the learner draw in the boxes instead of writing. In the circle, the learner tells the 4-sentence version and uses the reaction cards (read aloud from the card) as a listener. Target: one complete short anecdote and three reactions.

Stretch

Use the past perfect for a backstory twist ("What I didn't know was that she'd already…"), What I didn't realize was… clefts (from 4.2), and a planned pause before the punchline. C1 step: understatement for humor ("It wasn't my finest moment.") and an ending that ties back to the hook. Early finishers retell a classmate's story in reported speech for the class, keeping it short.

L1 watch-outs
  • Tense avoidance in stories: Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai, Indonesian and Malay speakers mark time with words, not verb forms, and may tell a whole story in the present simple ("Yesterday I go…"). That's different from the chosen historic present. Teach the contrast: past for the story, present only at the climax, on purpose.
  • Past continuous for everything: some Spanish, Portuguese and Italian speakers transfer the imperfect and write "I was going to the store and I was seeing my friend." Past continuous is for background; the events are past simple.
  • "Suddenly, all of a sudden" and "very furious / very exhausted": common everywhere. Upgrade in feedback, not during the story.
  • He / she in direct speech: Tagalog (siya), Chinese (spoken tā), Turkish, Persian, Finnish and Hungarian speakers mix he and she, which confuses who said what in a story. Point to the character on the planner.
  • Listening culture: in some cultures listeners stay quiet to show respect, and loud reactions feel rude; in others, overlapping talk is warm; and Japanese listeners give frequent small responses (aizuchi) that English speakers can mistake for agreement. Present English reactions as a tool, not a personality change, and model a quiet "Oh no…" as well as a big "No way!".
  • Final consonants in past-tense verbs (walked, stopped, laughed): Vietnamese, Cantonese, Thai and Korean speakers may drop /t/ or /d/, which makes the story sound present. Accept it in the circle; drill it briefly in feedback.
Homework

Online lesson 7.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_20.html. Listen to the Priya & Tom anecdote with 🔊 and perform both roles; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 7.2: write your anecdote as a short script and mark it up (underline the hook, circle each connector, box the ending, star the direct speech); tell it aloud once at home or record it. "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B You'll never guess what happened to me. · Q2 A Oh no! What did you do? · Q3 (new) C "I was waiting for the elevator when the fire alarm went off." sets the scene. Script 7.2-A differences (shaped version): a hook, scene with was walking, just as, direct speech, absolutely, an ending with a point. Script 7.2-B reactions: any natural reaction at each ⏸ (see the answer key).

7.3 · C22

Making a Case — Presentations & Persuasion

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • signpost a short talk so the audience always knows where they are: Today I'll cover… · First, let's look at… · Moving on to… · To sum up…
  • build an argument: a claim + reasons + evidence (a figure or a vivid example): The main advantage is… · Research suggests… · Take, for example…
  • use persuasive techniques: rhetorical questions, tripling, contrast (It's not a cost; it's an investment.), and appealing to the listener (you, we, together)
  • concede and counter an objection: Admittedly… However… · That said… · While it's true that…, …
  • handle questions calmly: That's a good question. · If I understand you correctly, you're asking… · I don't have the exact figure, but I'll follow up., and close with a call to action
Materials
  • Workbook 7.3 (New words, Grammar box, Exercises 1–4, Q&A dialogue, Pitch Planner)
  • Slides 14–18 · Signpost and technique flashcards · Pitch topic cards (8) · Objection cards (16) · Pitch planners (1 per team) · Persuasion Bingo grids (versions A and B) · A timer on screen · Optional "podium"
  • Listening scripts 7.3-A (a model 90-second pitch: bike parking at work) and 7.3-B (a Q&A exchange)

Left: the pitch shape: SIGNPOST (tell them the plan) → ARGUE (problem → solution → claim + reasons + evidence) → PERSUADE (techniques + concede/counter) → CLOSE (sum up + call to action). Under it: Today I'll cover… · First… · Moving on to… · For instance… · To sum up…

Center: four techniques with an example each: ❓ Wouldn't we all like a safer walk home? · 3️⃣ cheaper, faster and greener · ⚖️ It's not a cost; it's an investment. · 🫵 Think about your own commute. Together, we can…

Right: CONCEDE → COUNTER: Admittedly, it costs more at first… → however, it pays for itself within a year. · That said,… · While it's true that…, … · Q&A buttons: That's a good question. · If I understand you correctly,… · Let me come back to that. · I'll follow up. · Does that answer your question?

Stage plan

Warmer: 60-second story6S–S → T–SReview C21. New pairs: each tells their homework anecdote in 60 seconds (shorter again!). The listener reacts and names the hook afterwards. One volunteer tells the class the ending only. Point out: "A short true story is great evidence in a pitch. Keep yours."
Lead-in: weak vs strong4T–SSlide 14. Read both versions of the remote-work talk from the online lesson (the "um, like, and stuff" one, then the strong one). "What did the strong speaker do?" Elicit: said the plan, gave reasons, gave evidence, admitted a weakness. Draw the pitch shape.
Signposting & argument10T–S → S–SSlide 15. Signposts by stage, then claim + reasons + evidence (The main advantage is… · There are two reasons for this… · Research suggests… · Take, for example…). The pause at each signpost. CCQs. Pairs do Workbook Ex 1 (complete the phrases) and check quickly.
Persuasive techniques8T–SSlide 16. Read Script 7.3-A (the bike-parking pitch). Learners use a Persuasion Bingo grid as a listening demo: tick what they hear. Check: which techniques, which signposts? Then name the four techniques on the board with the pitch's examples. Workbook Ex 2 together (name the technique).
Concede–counter & questions8T–S → S–SSlide 17. Concede-and-counter pairs; why it makes you more convincing. Then the Q&A phrases with Script 7.3-B (two voices: a panelist's tough question and the presenter's calm answer). CCQs. Workbook Ex 3 (match the halves) in pairs.

Pitch planning10S–S (teams of 3)Teams take a pitch topic card (a community or workplace improvement with a problem, a solution, a cost and two facts). They fill in the pitch planner: who says which part (Speaker 1: signpost + problem · Speaker 2: solution + evidence + technique · Speaker 3: concede–counter + close + call to action), and they predict two panel questions. Key words only. ICQs first.
Freer: The Pitch Panel22S–S (team → panel) → T–SSlide 18. The rest of the class is the panel (a community board or a company's management team), each holding 2–3 objection cards and a Persuasion Bingo grid. Each team pitches for 2 minutes (timer on screen), then takes 2 minutes of questions: panelists read or adapt an objection card. After all pitches, each panelist gives one "vote" (a sticky note) to the pitch that persuaded them most. With a large class, run two panels at the same time in two corners.
Feedback + module wrap5T–SFix it: "Research suggest that…" · "I will cover about two points." · "Admittedly it is expensive, but however…" · "It's a good question." (said flatly, then silence). Upgrade it: "I think it is good" → "The main advantage is…" · "Any questions? No? OK bye." → "To sum up… So let's… I'm happy to take questions." Announce the winning pitch; learners tick the three Module 7 "I can…" lists.
