If you run long

This class is full. Cut Workbook Ex 4 to homework and shorten the drill; do the bingo demo with Script 7.3-A only if time allows (the bingo is used again in the panel anyway). With more than 5 teams, run two panels in parallel rather than cutting the Q&A: the questions are where learners grow most.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: signposts are for the listener. A reader can look back; a listener can't. Signposts tell them where they are.

"Moving on to my second point…" Is the speaker starting, changing topic, or finishing? (Changing to the next point.) What should you do right after a signpost? (Pause for a beat.) · "To sum up…" Will the talk go on for a long time? (No, it's ending.)

Meaning: an argument = claim + support.

"Bike parking is better." Opinion or argument? (Opinion only.) "Bike parking is better because twelve people already cycle and there's nowhere safe to lock a bike." (An argument: a claim + a reason + evidence.) · "Take my coworker Ana…" Is a story allowed as evidence? (Yes, an example makes it real.)

Meaning: the techniques.

"Wouldn't we all like a safer walk home?" Does the speaker want an answer? (No, it makes you think and agree.) · "It's not a cost; it's an investment." How many ideas are set against each other? (Two: that's contrast.) · "It saves time, saves money and saves stress." How many? (Three: tripling. Why three? It's memorable, rhythmic.)

Meaning: concede and counter.

"Admittedly, it costs more at first. That said, it pays for itself within a year." Does the speaker agree that it costs more? (Yes, they admit it.) Do they change their mind? (No.) Which part is stronger, the first or the second? (The second.) Why admit the weakness? (It builds trust; you look fair, not naive.)

Form.

SignpostIntroduce · first point · move on · add · example · concludeToday I'll cover… · To begin with… · Moving on to… / That brings me to… · What's more… · For instance… / Take, for example… · To sum up… / In conclusion…
Arguebenefit · reasons · evidence · resultThe main advantage is… · There are two reasons for this. First,… Second,… · Research suggests / The figures show… · As a result,… / This means that…
Techniquesrhetorical question · tripling · contrast · appealIsn't that worth it? · cheaper, faster and greener · not a problem, an opportunity · Think about how this affects you. Together, we can…
Concede → counterAdmittedly / Of course / While it's true that… → however / that said / nevertheless / butWhile it's true that there are risks, the benefits clearly outweigh them.
Q&Abuy time · check · answer · check backThat's a good question. · If I understand you correctly, you're asking… · I'm not sure, but my sense is… · Let me come back to that. · Does that answer your question?
Closesum up + call to actionTo sum up,… So let's… / I'm asking you to… · Thank you. I'm happy to take questions.

Pronunciation. Pause after every signpost (count "one" silently). Tripling has a list tune: rise, rise, fall: "cheaper ↗, faster ↗, and greener ↘." Contrast stress falls on the two contrasting words: "It's not a cost; it's an investment." Rhetorical questions usually fall (they're really statements): "Isn't that worth it? ↘". Admittedly /ədˈmɪtɪdli/: four syllables, stress on mit. Chunk the talk into thought groups and slow down: nerves make everyone fast; a pitch at 80% speed sounds confident.

Drill script
  1. Signpost and pause: T shows a stage card (START · NEXT · EXAMPLE · END) → S: "Today I'll cover two points. (pause)" · "Moving on to my second point… (pause)" · "Take, for example,… " · "To sum up… (pause)." Choral; exaggerate the pause.
  2. Rule of three: T: "cheaper, faster…" → S: "…and greener!" T: "save time…" → S: "save time, save money and save stress." Keep the rise-rise-fall tune.
  3. Not X; Y: T: "cost / investment" → S: "It's not a cost; it's an investment." T: "problem / opportunity" → S: "It's not a problem; it's an opportunity." T: "a luxury / a necessity."
  4. Concede in one breath: T: "expensive" → S: "Admittedly, it's expensive. That said, it pays for itself." T: "takes time" → S: "While it's true that it takes time, the benefits are huge."
  5. Buy time: T asks a hard question fast ("How much will it cost exactly?") → S: "That's a good question. I don't have the exact figure, but I'll follow up." Then S asks S: open pairs → closed pairs.
The Pitch Panel — how to run it
  • Topics (set F, 8 cards): bike parking at work · a staff language-buddy program · a community tool library · better lighting in the parking lot · a shared-shift swap board · an after-school homework club at the library · a community garden on an empty lot · water refill stations at the community center. Each card has the problem, the solution, the cost, and two facts for evidence. Teams may add an invented anecdote as an example.
  • Teams of 3, one part each (see the planner). A pair is fine; a solo learner can pitch with you as the "team." Everyone speaks. The strongest speaker in a team takes Part 3 (concede–counter + close), which is the hardest.
  • Pitch: 2 minutes, strict. Show the timer. At 1:45 hold up a "wrap up" card. Speakers talk from the planner, not a script.
  • Q&A: 2 minutes. The panel chair (rotate the job; give it to a dominant speaker, with the rule "call on someone who hasn't asked yet first") picks panelists. Panelists read or adapt an objection card ("Who's going to maintain it?" · "What if nobody uses it?" · "Has this worked anywhere else?"). Because the questions come from cards, nobody is attacking or revealing their own view, and nobody has to be against a classmate's idea.
  • Persuasion Bingo keeps the panel listening: tick each technique or signpost you hear. A line of three = a quiet "Bingo!" at the end of the pitch (not during). The team gets the credit.
  • Voting: one sticky note per panelist on the board under the most persuasive pitch, not their own. Award the "best answer to a question" too, so a team that pitched quietly but answered well is recognized.
ICQs before the Pitch Panel
How long is the pitch? (Two minutes.) · Does everyone in the team speak? (Yes, one part each.) · Can you read a script? (No, key words.) · Panel: where do your questions come from? (The objection cards.) · What do you say first when you get a hard question? ("That's a good question.") · When do you say "Bingo"? (At the end, not during the pitch.) · Can you vote for your own team? (No!)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give Part 1 (signpost + problem) with a frame strip: "Today I'll talk about ___. The problem is ___. For example, ___." Use a topic card with a picture (bike, garden, lamp) and read the facts aloud with the learner. In Q&A, the learner's job is the first line only: "That's a good question," and a teammate continues. Target: three signposted sentences, spoken without reading.

Stretch

Build a pitch that anticipates the objection before the panel asks ("Now, you might be thinking: who's going to maintain it?"). Use not only… but also, a conditional call to action ("If we act this month, we could open by spring."), and one figure + one example together. C1 step: answer a question you can't fully answer honestly and still sound in control. Early finishers draft the email to the community board (C20 register!) that would follow the pitch.

L1 watch-outs
  • "Research suggest" / "Studies shows": third-person -s slips appear under pressure for almost everyone, especially Chinese, Vietnamese, Thai and Haitian Creole speakers (no verb agreement in L1). Put research suggests · studies show on the board.
  • Double connector: "Although it is expensive, but it saves money." / "Admittedly…, but however…". Chinese (suīrán… dànshì), Vietnamese (tuy… nhưng), Korean and Arabic use a pair of linkers. English uses one: Although…, … or …, but….
  • Hedging vs over-claiming: some learners (e.g. from Japanese, Korean, Thai and some East African contexts) soften so much that there is no claim ("Maybe perhaps it could be a little good"); others state opinion as fact ("Everybody knows this is the best"). Aim for a clear claim + a fair concession.
  • Rhetorical questions answered: learners from classrooms where every question must be answered may reply to the presenter's rhetorical question. Explain the falling tune and the "don't answer" rule; enjoy the laugh.
  • "I will cover about two points" / "I will talk about the bike": Spanish/Portuguese hablar sobre → "talk about" is fine, but "cover about" isn't. cover / discuss / explain + object directly.
  • Stress on long words: adMITtedly, inVESTment, opporTUnity, eVIdence. Speakers of syllable-timed languages (Spanish, French, Tagalog, Yoruba) may give every syllable equal weight, which flattens the rhetoric. Tap the stressed syllable on the desk.
Homework

Online lesson 7.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_21.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and say them back with the pauses; do Exercises 1–3 and the Quick Quiz. Workbook 7.3: write a one-paragraph "case" for something you want to persuade someone of (an everyday or workplace topic; your team's pitch is fine) and mark it up (underline the signposts, box the reasons and evidence, star each technique and the concede–counter move). "My new words" and the journal.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A Admittedly, it's expensive; however, it saves money long-term. · Q2 B "That's a good question — I don't have the exact figure, but I'll follow up." · Q3 (new) B "It's not a cost; it's an investment." is contrast. Script 7.3-A: signposts Today I'd like to…, First,… Moving on to…, To sum up…; techniques: rhetorical question, tripling (healthier, cheaper and greener), contrast (not a luxury; it's basic), concede–counter (Admittedly… That said…), appeal (Think about…), call to action.

Closing the Module

End of C22: Module 7 wrap-up

Recap on the last slide: fit the reader (one message, three registers, one register per message) → hold the listener (hook, scene, connectors, the historic present, a landed ending, and good reactions) → move the audience (signpost, claim + evidence, a technique, concede and counter, handle the question, call to action). Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you; review it in the C23 warmer.

Watch for in Module 8: Lesson 8.1 (connected speech) explains the reductions learners heard in casual register (gonna, wanna, lemme): link back to the informal end of the dial. Lesson 8.2 (vague language: sort of, around, or so) is the informal cousin of hedging; remind learners it belongs at the casual end, not in a formal email. Lesson 8.3 / C25 is a structured debate: the signposts, concede–counter and Q&A phrases from C22 carry straight over, so keep the Persuasion Bingo grids and the objection cards for C25.

Final assessment (C26): the speaking interview is a natural place for a short anecdote ("Tell me about a time something funny or surprising happened") and a mini-case ("Persuade me that…"). The writing section can include a register task (a casual message → a formal email). Use the "Fix it / Upgrade it" notes you kept in this module to give each learner one accuracy target and one range target before C26.

Instructor Kit · Teacher's Guide

Module 8 — Fluency & the Final Project

The last three classes of the course. First, a listening lab on natural, connected speech: weak forms and the schwa, can vs can't, linking, elision and assimilation, and casual reductions (gonna, wanna, gotta, kinda, d'you, whatcha), practiced through micro-dictation and a dictogloss. Next comes vague language: approximating (about, around, -ish, or so, give or take), general nouns and placeholders (stuff, and that kind of thing, or something) and hedges (sort of, a bit, in a way), and knowing when vagueness is natural and when it is sloppy. Last, the B2 review project: a structured debate with a motion, teams on assigned sides, a chair, a timekeeper, rebuttals and questions from the floor.

3 lessons Lessons 8.1–8.3 Classes C23–C25 Weeks 12–13 90 min per class CEFR B2

How to Use This Guide

  • One lesson block = one 90-minute class. C23 and C24 follow the usual shape: warmer → lead-in → Meaning / Form / Pronunciation with concept-checking questions (CCQs) → drill → controlled practice (workbook) → freer practice → feedback with delayed error correction → homework. C23 is a listening lab, so its "controlled practice" is mostly listening and its freer stage is a group reconstruction task. C25 is a task-based class: pre-task → planning → rehearsal → task (two debates) → feedback.
  • The stage plan is your clock. Each one adds up to 90 minutes. The numbers in the Time column are minutes.
  • Present from slides/module-8-debate.html. Print materials/module-8/classroom-materials.html before C23. It holds the flashcards (reductions, weak forms, vague expressions, debate moves), the listening-lab scripts (each with a natural-speed and a slow version), the Decode It A/B cards, the "Appropriate or sloppy?" sort cards, the Register Switch role cards, the Roughly Speaking board game, the new identity cards, the debate kit (motion cards, side cards, role cards, the chair's script, timekeeper cards, floor-question and voting slips), the debate rubric and the teacher observation checklist.
  • The online lessons are the learners' homework and review. Each has 🔊 Listen buttons and an audio control that can slow speech down, which is exactly what learners need after C23.
  • Extension, not a first meeting. Learners met weak forms informally in every earlier pronunciation note, hedging in 7.1 (register) and 7.3 (persuasion), and conceding and countering in 7.3. Many already say about and stuff. The new B2 work is (1) naming the connected-speech processes so learners can decode fast speech on purpose, (2) using vagueness deliberately and knowing when it becomes sloppy, and (3) sustaining a formal, rule-bound exchange where you must answer what the other side actually said.
  • American English is the classroom standard. The online lessons use British forms and British sounds: recognise, favour, centre, neighbourhood, colour, practise, memorise, film, shop, quid, veg, cycle lanes, and the phonemic table gives British vowels (of /ɒv/, was /wɒz/, are /ɑː/). Teach recognize, favor, center, neighborhood, color, practice, memorize, movie, store, bucks / dollars, vegetables, bike lanes, and the American strong forms of /ʌv/, was /wʌz/, are /ɑr/. The weak forms are the same in both. Accept British forms when learners use them.
  • Dashed boxes = lines to say out loud, CCQs and ICQs (instruction-checking questions). Green boxes = scripts you read aloud. Amber = watch-outs.
The through-line

Hear it → loosen it → argue it. In C23 learners stop waiting for clear, separate words and learn how fast English really arrives: "I wz gonna callya" = I was going to call you. In C24 they learn that fluent speakers are often deliberately imprecise (about thirty or so, give or take) and that the skill is judging when to be vague and when to be exact. In C25 they put the whole level into one formal event: a debate in which they must listen to the other team at natural speed, concede what is fair, counter it, and switch between a formal backbone (the passive, conditionals, comparison, inversion) and natural spoken touches (hedges, vague language). Every class ends the same way: understanding and responding to real, fast, spoken English.

Fluency before accuracy — feedback on accuracy and range

During freer stages, do not interrupt. Walk around with a notepad divided into three columns: Fix (errors that matter), Upgrade (correct but plain sentences a B2 speaker could lift), and Wow (great language to praise). Correct after the task, on the board, without names. At B2, feedback is not only about mistakes: every feedback stage in this module boards two errors to fix and one or two sentences to upgrade, e.g. "Cars are bad for health." → "It's widely accepted that car fumes damage our health." Correct on the spot only when the error blocks meaning or is the target of a drill.

Dominant speakers are the biggest risk in C24's board game and in C25's debate. Use structure, not criticism: (1) the timekeeper is real: when the card says STOP, the speaker stops mid-sentence (make it a joke, but keep it); (2) give a dominant learner the chair or the rebuttal role (they must respond to others, not deliver a speech); (3) floor questions use one slip per person: when your slip is used, you're done; (4) a quiet word before class: "You're a strong speaker. Today, can you help your team by making sure everyone gets their turn?" Quieter learners get the opening speech (fully prepared from notes) or the summary (short, planned), and 30 seconds of thinking time before any spontaneous turn.

Trauma-informed: assigned sides and safe motions

A debate asks people to argue in public, and for some learners disagreement with a group, a raised voice, or "taking a side" carries real risk from past experience. Three rules protect everyone. (1) Sides are assigned by drawing a card, never chosen. Say it every time: "You argue the side you're given. It says nothing about what you believe. Lawyers and debaters do this every day." Nobody explains their own view, ever. (2) Motions stay in everyday, community and workplace territory: phones at dinner, a four-day week, homework for children, a car-free main street on weekends. The online lesson's examples include "University should be free": in class, don't use it or any motion about politics, religion, immigration, policing, or national identity. (3) The vote is secret and it is about the debate: "Which team made the stronger case?", never "What do you think?" In C24, personal description tasks (your week, your neighborhood) always allow a new identity card or an invented answer, and anyone can say "Pass."

Before the Module

  • Print the flashcards (reductions, weak forms, vague expressions, debate moves) once for the board. Cut them before C23.
  • For C23: rehearse the listening-lab scripts aloud at home. The natural version must sound natural: read the "sounds like" line as a guide, don't exaggerate, and keep your normal speed. Practice the slow version too (clear, not robotic). One set of Decode It A/B cards per pair.
  • For C24: one set of "Appropriate or sloppy?" sort cards per group of 3–4, one Roughly Speaking board + a die (or a coin: heads = 1, tails = 2) + counters per group, one set of Register Switch A/B cards per pair, and the new identity cards on a side table.
  • For C25 (set up at the end of C24): decide the teams and debates, cut the motion cards, the side cards (Proposition / Opposition) and the role cards. Print one chair's script per debate, one set of timekeeper cards per debate, floor-question slips and voting slips for everyone, one debate rubric per learner and the teacher observation checklist on a clipboard. A big timer on the screen helps.
  • Know your C26 plan. Read "Looking Ahead" at the end of this chapter and check the kit's B2 final assessment (assessment/) before C25, so what you tell learners matches it. Order or print certificates (the shared ESL toolkit has a template).
  • Room for C25: two tables facing each other (Proposition on the chair's right, Opposition on the left), the chair and timekeeper at the front, the floor facing the teams. Set it up before class; it changes the atmosphere at once.

Module at a Glance

Lesson Plans

8.1 · C23

Understanding Natural Speech — A Listening Lab

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • explain why fast English is hard to catch: content words are stressed and clear, grammar words are squeezed (English is stress-timed)
  • recognize the weak forms of to, of, and, for, can, was, are, them, at, some, than, that with the schwa /ə/, and hear the difference between can /kən/ and can't /kænt/
  • follow linking (turn it off → "tur-ni-toff"), elision (nex(t) day, san(d)wich) and assimilation (don't you → "doncha")
  • decode casual reductions (gonna, wanna, gotta, kinda, sorta, d'you, whatcha, lemme, gimme, dunno, 'em) and always write the full forms
  • rebuild a short message heard at natural speed (dictogloss), putting every "invisible" grammar word back
Materials
  • Workbook 8.1 (terms, weak-form box, Exercises 1–5, Sam & Jo dialogue, Decode It)
  • Slides 3–9 · Reduction and weak-form flashcards · Decode It A/B cards (1 set per pair)
  • Listening-lab scripts 8.1-A (careful vs natural), 8.1-B (can or can't?), 8.1-C (micro-dictation), 8.1-D (dictogloss: Dana's voicemail) — each with a natural-speed and a slow version
  • Optional: a phone to "play" Dana's voicemail (read the script holding the phone to your ear)

Left — STRESS-TIMED: I was GOing to CALL you → "I wz gonna callya" · big words = clear · small words = squeezed · schwa /ə/ = "uh"

Center — FOUR DISGUISES: 1 weak forms: to /tə/ · of /əv/ · and /ən/ · can /kən/ ≠ can't /kænt/ · 2 linking: turn‿it‿off · an‿apple · go (w) on · 3 elision: nex(t) day · san(d)wich · 4 reductions: gonna · wanna · gotta · kinda · d'you · whatcha · lemme

Right — THE RULE: 👂 hear "gonna" → ✏️ write going to. Never write "gonna" in an email. Listen for the stressed beats, not the gaps.

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: 30-second pitch8S–S → T–SReview C22 (7.3, persuasion). Each learner draws an everyday object or idea (a pen, Monday mornings, the bus, cold coffee) and "sells" it to a partner in 30 seconds with one persuasive technique (a rhetorical question, a rule of three, "Imagine…"). The partner asks one tough question; the speaker handles it ("That's a great question…"). Swap twice. Two volunteers perform for the class.
Lead-in: careful vs natural7T–SRead Script 8.1-A: the Sam & Jo conversation, first in "dictionary English" (every word careful), then at natural speed. "Which one sounds like real people? What happened to the words?" Elicit: words disappear, run together, get shorter. Slide 3. "Your listening isn't weak. Nobody showed you the disguises. Today you learn four of them."
Weak forms & can / can't13T–S → SSlides 4–5. Clap the stressed beats of "I was going to call you" and show how the small words squeeze between the claps. Present the weak-form table and the schwa. CCQs (below). Weak-form drill. Then Script 8.1-B: learners write can or can't (Workbook Ex 1), first at natural speed, then check with the slow version.
Linking, elision, assimilation10T–S → S–SSlides 6–7. Consonant → vowel linking (write turn it off and draw the ‿ arcs), the little /w/ and /j/ glides, then elision and assimilation. Linking drill (backchaining, below). Pairs do Workbook Ex 3 (the hidden word; mark the links and cross out the silent letters) and read the phrases aloud to each other.
Reductions8T–S → SSlide 8. Flash the reduction cards: T says "gonna," learners say "going to"; T says "going to," learners say "gonna." Stress the rule: these are for listening; write the full forms. Workbook Ex 2 alone, check in pairs.
Micro-dictation8S → S–SScript 8.1-C, Workbook Ex 4. Each sentence: natural speed twice (learners count the words on their fingers, then write), then the slow version once to check. Pairs compare. "Which words did you miss?" It is almost always the small ones: that's the point.
Dictogloss: Dana's voicemail20S → S–S → groupsSlide 9. Script 8.1-D, Workbook Ex 5. (1) Predict: "A coworker leaves a voicemail about work. What might she say?" (1 min). (2) Read once at natural speed: just listen (1 min). (3) Read again at natural speed: learners write stressed content words only (2 min). (4) Pairs pool their notes (3 min). (5) Groups of 3–4 rebuild the message in full, correct written English, with every grammar word back (8 min). (6) Read the slow version, or show the text; groups compare and find what they missed and why (weak form? linking? elision?) (5 min). ICQs first.
Freer: Decode It6S–SPairs, Decode It A/B cards. A says six sentences naturally (the card shows the full form and a "say it like this" hint); B writes the full form, then answers the sentence as a real reply ("D'you wanna grab lunch?" → writes Do you want to grab lunch? → says "Sure, where?"). Swap. Early finishers read the Sam & Jo dialogue at natural speed.
Feedback5T–SBoard 2 errors from the dictogloss reconstructions (no names), usually missing grammar words: "I was going call you" · "They want us bring a copy." Learners fix them (going to call, want us to bring). Then 1 upgrade: praise the group that kept the passive "The meeting has been moved." Which disguise tricked the class most? Tick it on the board.
Homework set-up5T–SShow online lesson 8.1; point at the 🎧 speed control and a 🔊 button. Set the homework (below): the online transcribe-and-decode project. Learners tick the "I can…" box.
Total908 + 7 + 13 + 10 + 8 + 8 + 20 + 6 + 5 + 5
If you run long

Do Workbook Ex 3 as homework and shorten the reductions stage to the flashcard drill. Never cut the dictogloss below 15 minutes. It is the heart of the lab: learners discover for themselves where their ears fail, and the group talk while rebuilding the text is real B2 negotiation ("No, I think she said has been moved, because…").

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: stress-timing. In English the stressed syllables come at roughly regular beats, and everything between them is squeezed to fit. Content words (nouns, main verbs, adjectives, adverbs, negatives) carry the beat; grammar words (articles, prepositions, auxiliaries, pronouns, conjunctions) are squeezed.

"I was GOing to CALL you." How many beats? (Two, on go- and call.) Which words are squeezed? (I, was, -ing to, you.) Are they gone? (No, still there, just short and quiet.) Is this lazy English? (No, everyone speaks like this, newsreaders too.)

Meaning: weak forms and the schwa. Most grammar words have a strong form (alone or stressed) and a weak form (most of the time). In the weak form the vowel becomes the schwa /ə/, the most common sound in English.

"It's for you." Do you hear "four"? (No, /fər/.) · When do we say the strong "FOR"? (When it's stressed: "Who's it FOR?", or at the end of a sentence.) · "a cup of tea" → "a cuppa tea": which word disappeared? (of.) Is it still in the meaning? (Yes.)
can / can't: "I can go." Is can long or short? (Short, /kən/.) · "I CAN'T go." Long or short? (Long, stressed, /kænt/, and the t is often almost silent.) · So how do you know which one you heard? (The vowel and the stress: short "kn" = can; long "kænt" = can't. Don't wait for the t.)

Meaning: linking, elision, assimilation.

"turn it off" → "tur-ni-toff." Where did the n go? (To the start of the next word.) · "go on" → "go-won." Is there a new word "won"? (No, a small /w/ glide.) · "next day": do you hear the t? (No.) Is it spelled? (Yes.) Do we write "nex day"? (Never.) · "Don't you know?" → "doncha": is that a new word? (No, /t/ + /j/ blend into "ch.")

Meaning: reductions. Some chunks shrink so often that they have informal spellings. They show the sound, not a new word.

"I'm gonna call her." Full form? (I'm going to call her.) · Can I write "gonna" in a work email? (No.) In a text to a friend? (Some people do; in class, practice the full form.) · "I'm going to the store." Can this be "gonna"? (No! Only going to + verb becomes "gonna.") · "I gotta go." Full form? (I've got to go / I have to go.)

Form.

WordStrong (US)WeakYou hear…
to/tu//tə/I want tə go.
of/ʌv//əv/, /ə/a cup ə tea · kind ə cold
and/ænd//ən/, /n/fish 'n' chips · you 'n' me
for/fɔr//fər/It's fər you.
can/kæn//kən/I kən go. (≠ I CAN'T go.)
was/wʌz//wəz/She wəz late.
are/ɑr//ər/They ər ready.
them/ðɛm//ðəm/, 'emTell 'em.
at · some · than · that/ət/ · /səm/ · /ðən/ · /ðət/at ət six · səm milk · bigger ðən · I think ðət…
Reductionsgonna = going to · wanna = want to · gotta = (have) got to · kinda / sorta = kind of / sort of · d'you = do you · whatcha = what are you / what do you · lemme = let me · gimme = give me · dunno = don't know · 'em = them · didja = did you · doncha = don't you

Pronunciation (American English). The flap t: between vowels, American t sounds like a quick soft d: water "wadder," got a "godda," what about "whadabout," let it "ledit." Many learners have never been told this, and it explains a lot of "missing" words. Final t before a consonant is often a glottal stop: can't go [kæʔ goʊ], not now [nɑʔ naʊ]. Production goal: learners don't need to produce reductions. Clear full forms are always fine. What helps their own speech is the rhythm: stress the content words, and let the grammar words be light.

Drill script
  1. Clap the beat: T claps two beats and says "CALL you LAter." Class repeats. Add small words without adding beats: "I'll CALL you LAter." → "I was GOing to CALL you LAter." The claps don't slow down, so the small words must squeeze. Choral, then rows, then individuals.
  2. Strong or weak? T: "Who's it for?" (strong, end of sentence) → "It's for you." (weak) · T: "Can you swim? Yes, I can." (strong) → "I can swim." (weak). Learners repeat both and say which is strong.
  3. can / can't hands: T says a sentence naturally; learners show a thumbs-up for can, thumbs-down for can't. Then pairs: A says one, B shows the thumb.
  4. Backchaining for linking: T: "off" → S: "off" · T: "toff" → S: "toff" · T: "ni-toff" → S: "ni-toff" · T: "turn it off" (linked) → S: "turn it off." Repeat with pick it up, this evening, far away.
  5. Reduce / expand ping-pong: T: "going to" → S: "gonna" · T: "wanna" → S: "want to" · T: "let me" → S: "lemme" · T: "dunno" → S: "don't know." Fast, choral, then individuals.
The dictogloss — how to run it
  • Natural means natural. Read Script 8.1-D at your normal speaking speed, both times. The temptation is to slow down "to help." Don't: the slow version comes at the end, as the check.
  • Content words only on the second listening. Learners who try to write everything fall behind and panic. Model it: write "Dana … call … yesterday … ran out … time" on the board.
  • Rebuild, don't transcribe. Groups write a correct, complete text with the same meaning. It doesn't have to be word for word, but every grammar word must be there. Circulate and ask: "What's missing between going and call?"
  • One writer per group, and the writer is not the strongest learner. The strong learner is the "checker": they may only ask questions ("Are you sure it's want us bring?"). This keeps the group talking and stops one person doing it all.
  • The check: read the slow version while groups compare, then show the text (project the Script 8.1-D page from the classroom materials, or write it on the board). Groups underline their misses and label each: WF (weak form), L (linking), E (elision), R (reduction). "Which label did you write most?"
ICQs before the dictogloss
How many times do I read it before you write? (Once: just listen.) · Second time, what do you write? (Only the big, stressed words.) · Then? (Share notes with a partner, then rebuild it in a group.) · Do you write "gonna"? (No, the full form.) · Must it be word for word? (No, same meaning, correct English, every small word.) · Who writes in your group? (The writer; the checker only asks questions.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Focus on three things only: can / can't (thumbs), gonna / wanna / gotta (flashcards with the full form on the back), and "listen for the big words." In the micro-dictation, give a gapped version with the content words already printed ("I ___ ___ ___ call ___, but I ran ___ ___ time.") so learners listen only for the small words. In the dictogloss, be the note-taker's partner: learners say the words they heard and you (or a partner) write them. Target: Ex 1 with 6/8 correct and 4 reductions expanded aloud.

Stretch

Explore the American flap t and the glottal stop (above) and find three examples in the dictogloss. Notice when a reduction is impossible: "I want her to come" can never be "I wanna her come"; "I'm going to the store" never becomes "gonna the store." Toward C1: listen to 60 seconds of an unscripted podcast and transcribe it, then compare with the auto-captions and find where the captions are wrong. Early finishers write the Sam & Jo dialogue as a formal message (no reductions, full sentences).

L1 watch-outs
  • Syllable-timed languages (Spanish, Portuguese, French, Italian, Turkish, Tagalog, and many West African languages) give every syllable similar length, so learners expect to hear to, of, was as full words and "lose" the sentence when they don't. Clapping the beats is the fix. Japanese (mora-timed) speakers have the same issue.
  • Adding vowels to clusters: Spanish and Portuguese speakers ("es-tudent"), Arabic speakers ("nex-i-t day"), Japanese and Korean speakers ("nexu-to"). When the English speaker drops a sound (nex(t) day), these learners hear something very different from what they expect. Show both on the board: what they'd say vs what they hear.
  • Dropped final consonants: Vietnamese, Cantonese, Mandarin and Thai speakers often release final consonants weakly, and so miss the consonant that links to the next vowel (pick‿it‿up → they hear "pi… i… up"). Train them to listen for the consonant at the start of the next word.
  • French speakers have an advantage: English linking is like French liaison. Tell them so; it builds confidence.
  • Missing articles in the dictogloss (Russian, Polish, Chinese, Japanese, Korean): "meeting has been moved to next Tuesday," "bring copy of report." Their L1 has no articles, and the English weak form /ə/ is almost silent, so they neither hear nor expect them. Board the missing the / a in feedback.
  • can / can't: learners from every background wait for the final t, which in American English is often silent. Teach the vowel: /ə/ vs /æ/.
Homework

Online lesson 8.1: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_22.html. Use the 🎧 control to hear the model sentences slowly, then at normal speed, and repeat them; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz. Do the lesson's project: transcribe 15–30 seconds of a clip with subtitles, compare, and label every miss (weak form, linking, elision, reduction). Workbook 8.1: "My new words" and the journal (three "disappearing words" from real listening this week, and the full forms).

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A I was going to call you. · Q2 A "I can help" (a weak, unstressed can). · Q3 (new) A I don't know. Let me check. Ex 1 (Script 8.1-B): 1 can't · 2 can · 3 can · 4 can't · 5 can · 6 can't … can · 7 can · 8 can't … can. Dictogloss: see the answer key and Script 8.1-D.

8.2 · C24

Vague Language & Being Approximate

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • approximate numbers, amounts and times naturally: about / around / roughly twenty · ten minutes or so · seven-ish · fifty, give or take · thirty-odd · a couple of · more or less
  • use general nouns and placeholders: stuff, things, and that kind of thing, and so on, or something, and everything, the thingy
  • soften statements and opinions with hedges: sort of, kind of, a bit, a little, in a way, I guess, I suppose
  • judge register: tell natural, friendly vagueness from sloppy vagueness (too many placeholders, or vagueness where precision is needed: dosages, deadlines, safety, contracts)
  • describe the same situation twice: relaxed and vague for a friend, clear and precise for a manager, doctor or landlord
Materials
  • Workbook 8.2 (phrase bank, grammar box, Exercises 1–5, Alex & Priya dialogue, Register Switch, Describe It Naturally)
  • Slides 10–14 · Vague-expression flashcards (approximators, placeholders, hedges) · "Appropriate or sloppy?" sort cards (1 set per group) · Roughly Speaking board game (1 per group + die or coin + counters) · Register Switch A/B cards (1 set per pair) · New identity cards
  • Listening scripts 8.2-A (Alex & Priya) and 8.2-B (Leo's two voicemails: to a friend, to his manager)
  • For the C25 set-up: motion cards, side cards, role cards, the debate prep sheet (Workbook 8.3)

Left — 1 APPROXIMATE: about / around / roughly 20 · 10 minutes or so · seven-ish · 50, give or take · thirty-odd guests · a couple of minutes · more or less finished

Center — 2 GENERAL NOUNS: stuff (uncountable! no "stuffs") · things · milk, bread, that kind of thing · pizza or something · pen, paper, and so on · the thingy · 3 HEDGES: sort of / kind of · a bit expensive · in a way · I guess

Right — READ THE ROOM: 😊 friend: "done-ish, sevenish" · 💼 manager: "nearly complete; by 7 p.m." · 💊 🔥 📄 dosage · safety · contract = PRECISE

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: decode it again6T–S → S–SReview C23. Say 5 sentences at natural speed ("Whatcha gonna do this weekend?" · "I dunno, lemme think." · "D'you wanna cuppa coffee?" · "I gotta go." · "It's kinda cold."). Teams write the full forms; 1 point each. Then pairs share one "disappearing word" from their homework.
Lead-in: robot or human?5T–SSlide 10. Read the two versions: "The trip takes 34 minutes. I bought 6 items. It cost $12.50." vs "It takes about half an hour. I picked up a few things. It was twelve bucks or so." "Which one is a robot? Why does the human sound friendlier?" Elicit the four reasons: precision can sound cold; we often don't know exactly; vagueness shows shared understanding; it softens.
Clarify: three jobs15T–SSlides 11–12. Job 1: approximate (positions matter: about before, or so after, -ish attached). Job 2: general nouns and placeholders (picture slide: "I picked up a few things: milk, bread, that kind of thing"). Job 3: hedges. Stick the flashcards on the board in three columns as you go. CCQs and drill (below).
Controlled practice7S → S–SWorkbook Ex 1 (insert the vague expression) and Ex 2 (soften the over-precise sentence) alone, then check in pairs. Board-check only the items pairs disagree on.
Listening: two voicemails7S → T–SScript 8.2-B, Workbook Ex 5. Read Leo's voicemail to his friend, then his voicemail to his manager. Learners complete the table: what's approximate for the friend and exact for the manager? What's missing from the manager version, and why? (The boxes and "stuff" aren't her business; the hours he'll make up are.)
Appropriate or sloppy?11S–S (groups) → T–SSlide 13. Groups of 3–4 sort the 12 situation cards into ✅ NATURAL and ❌ SLOPPY, then fix every ❌ card aloud (a precise version). Check against the key. Then Workbook Ex 3 (the same idea, new items) alone as a quick check. CCQs on register.
Freer 1: Roughly Speaking12S–S (groups)Groups of 3–4, one board. Roll (or flip a coin) and move. Answer the square's question for about 30 seconds using the vague language it asks for; the group checks with the flashcard columns. On a PRECISE square, the vague rule flips: answer with exact facts. STOP squares: the next player asks a follow-up ("Roughly how far is it?"). ICQs first.
Freer 2: Register Switch12S–S (pairs)Slide 14. Pairs, A/B cards. Each card has one situation and two listeners: first tell it to a friend (relaxed, vague, warm), then to a manager / doctor / landlord / child's teacher (clear, precise). The partner plays each listener and asks one question. Swap. Personal details can come from a new identity card. ICQs first.
Feedback5T–SBoard 2 errors ("I have many stuffs to do." · "More or less, thanks." as an answer to "How are you?") and 1–2 upgrades (plain → natural: "There were 31 people." → "There were thirty-odd people."; sloppy → precise: "The thingy is kind of broken." → "The kitchen faucet is leaking.").
Homework & C25 set-up10T–S → groupsOnline lesson 8.2 + workbook Ex 4 and journal. Then C25 set-up (slide 15): form the teams (see "Setting up the debates" in 8.3), each team draws a motion card and a side card, and learners draw role cards. Teams meet for 4 minutes: read the motion, share first ideas, and take the debate prep sheet (Workbook 8.3) home to fill with key words. "You argue the side on your card. It says nothing about what you believe."
Total906 + 5 + 15 + 7 + 7 + 11 + 12 + 12 + 5 + 10
If you run long

Cut Roughly Speaking to 8 minutes (or skip it: it's also a good C26 celebration game) and move Workbook Ex 3 to homework. Keep the Register Switch: it is where learners choose the level of vagueness, which is the real B2 skill. Never cut the C25 set-up. Teams that know their motion and side before C25 plan better arguments at home, and the debates are far stronger.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation

Meaning: approximating.

"There were thirty-odd people." Were the people strange? (No! -odd = a bit more than thirty.) · Exactly thirty? (No, 31, 33, 35…) · "Let's meet at seven-ish." Can I arrive at 7:10? (Yes.) At 8:30? (No, too late.) · "Fifty people, give or take." Could it be 47? (Yes.) Could it be 90? (No.) · "It's more or less finished." Is it 100% finished? (No, almost.)

Meaning: general nouns and placeholders.

"We need milk, bread, and that kind of thing." Only milk and bread? (No, similar things too: eggs, butter…) · Does the speaker know every item? (Maybe not, and it doesn't matter.) · "Pass me the thingy, the remote." Why "thingy"? (The word didn't come to mind right away.) · "I've got lots of stuffs." Correct? (No: stuff is uncountable, like water.)

Meaning: hedges.

"It's a bit expensive." Is it cheap? (No.) Is the speaker angry? (No, polite, soft.) · "I kind of see it differently." Does she agree? (No.) Why not just say "I disagree"? (Softer; keeps the relationship.) · Can you hedge too much? (Yes: "It's sort of kind of a bit maybe difficult" = nobody knows what you mean.)

Meaning: register, the key B2 judgment.

Medicine label: "Take two-ish tablets every four hours or so." OK? (No! Dangerous.) · Email to your manager: "The report's more or less done, I'll send it sevenish." OK? (No: "The report is nearly complete. I'll send it by 7 p.m.") · To a friend: "I'll be there sevenish." OK? (Perfect.) · At the doctor's: "It hurts sort of here, for about a week." OK? (Yes, honest; then give the facts you do know.)

Form.

Before the numberabout · around · roughly · approximately (more formal) · a couple of · a fewabout twenty · roughly $50
After the numberor so · give or take · or thereabouts · -odd (after a round number)ten minutes or so · thirty-odd
Attached-ish on times, numbers, ages, colors, some adjectivesseven-ish · fortyish · bluish
End of a listand that kind of thing · and stuff like that · and so on · and everything · or somethingpizza, snacks, that kind of thing
Before an adjective / verbsort of · kind of · a bit · a little · more or lessa bit tired · kind of boring · more or less agreed
Whole sentencein a way · I guess · I suppose · to be honestIn a way, you're right.

Pronunciation. Vague expressions are light and quick, like the weak forms from C23: kinda, sorta, a bit, "abou' twenny." The number or noun keeps the stress: "about TWENty," "SEVen-ish," "PIZza or something." A rising, trailing voice on or something… ↗ invites the listener in; a falling voice on the precise version ↘ sounds firm and professional. -ish can even stand alone as an answer: "Are you ready?" "Ish." (very informal, a nice stretch item).

Drill script
  1. Make it vague: T: "There were 31 people." → S: "There were about thirty people." / "thirty-odd" / "thirty or so." T: "We'll meet at 7:00." → S: "We'll meet at seven-ish." T: "It took 58 minutes." → S: "It took about an hour."
  2. Finish the list: T: "We need milk, bread…" → S: "…and that kind of thing." T: "Let's get pizza…" → S: "…or something." T: "Bring a pen, paper…" → S: "…and so on."
  3. Soften it: T (blunt): "This is expensive." → S: "This is a bit expensive." T: "I disagree." → S: "I kind of see it differently." T: "You're wrong." → S: "In a way that's true, but…"
  4. Now be precise (flip): T: "The report's done-ish. Sevenish." (to your manager) → S: "The report is nearly complete. I'll send it by 7 p.m." T: "Take a couple of pills or so." → S: "Take two tablets every six hours."
Roughly Speaking and Register Switch — how to run them
  • Roughly Speaking (board game, classroom materials set F): 22 squares between START and FINISH. Each square names a job (≈ APPROXIMATE, 🧺 GENERAL NOUN, 🪶 HEDGE, 🎯 PRECISE, ⏸ STOP) and a prompt ("How long does it take you to get here?"). Answer for about 30 seconds. The group gives a thumbs-up if you used the right kind of language. First to the finish wins, but the real point is the talk. Everyone may "pass" a personal prompt and take the next square's prompt instead.
  • The PRECISE squares are the twist: "Tell a new coworker how to get out of the building in a fire." A learner who says "go out that way or something" goes back 2 squares. It makes the register rule memorable.
  • Register Switch (A/B cards, set E): the speaker tells the same facts twice. Listener 1 (friend) reacts warmly; listener 2 (manager, doctor, landlord, teacher) asks for exact details ("What time exactly?" "How many?"). Afterward pairs say which facts they made vaguer or more precise, and why.
  • Dominant speakers: in Roughly Speaking, the 30-second rule is timed by the player on the right. In Register Switch the roles are fixed and short, so turns stay even.
ICQs before the games
Roughly Speaking: how long do you talk? (About 30 seconds.) · On a PRECISE square, do you use "about" and "stuff"? (No, exact facts.) · Don't want to answer a personal question? (Say "Pass" and take the next square's prompt.) · Register Switch: how many times do you tell the story? (Twice.) · Who is the first listener? (A friend.) The second? (The person on the card: a manager, a doctor…) · Are the facts different? (No, the same facts, different language.)
Differentiation
Literacy support

Work with 8 expressions only: about, around, -ish, or so · stuff, or something · a bit, kind of, using the flashcards with emoji (≈, 🧺, 🪶). In the sort, give the learner the ✅ / ❌ decision only (a partner writes the fix). In Register Switch, use a picture-supported card (clock, calendar, 💊) and a two-line frame: "Friend: It was about… / …or something. Manager: It was exactly… / at … o'clock." Target: 3 vague expressions used in speech and 1 correct "be precise" decision.

Stretch

Vague language in writing registers: in a formal report, approximately, roughly, in the region of, a number of, various, tends to, appears to are "vague" but professional. Rewrite one casual sentence formally ("thirty-odd people turned up" → "approximately thirty people attended"). Toward C1: hedging in argument (It could be argued that…, This may suggest…) is vague language for grown-ups, and it's exactly what C25 needs. Early finishers write the lesson's journal paragraph and a "robotic" version of it.

L1 watch-outs
  • "More or less" as "so-so": Spanish más o menos, Portuguese mais ou menos, Italian più o meno answer "How are you?" In English, "How are you?" "More or less." sounds strange. English more or less = almost / basically ("It's more or less finished"). For "so-so," teach not bad, OK, could be better.
  • "stuffs," "informations," "equipments": many languages count these nouns. Stuff is uncountable: a lot of stuff, never many stuffs.
  • "-odd" and "odd": learners who know odd = strange hear "thirty-odd people" as "thirty strange people." Teach it as a chunk after round numbers.
  • Hedging feels dishonest to some learners from more direct communication styles (German, Dutch, Russian, Hebrew, Polish): "Why say 'a bit' if it's very expensive?" Explain that in English the hedge is politeness, and everyone decodes it. Learners from indirect styles (Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Thai, Filipino) may hedge everything and bury the message. Aim for the middle: one hedge per opinion.
  • "like" as a filler: learners who picked up English from TV and social media may use like in every sentence ("It was, like, like thirty people"). Fine among friends, sloppy in an interview. It goes in Ex 3.
Homework

Online lesson 8.2: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_23.html. Listen to the model sentences with 🔊 and repeat them with the relaxed rhythm; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz, and record the lesson's "Describe It Naturally" project (60–90 seconds). Workbook 8.2: Ex 4 (robotic → natural, sloppy → clear), "My new words" and the journal. For C25: fill the debate prep sheet in Workbook 8.3 with key words only, for your assigned side and role.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 B About thirty or so, give or take. · Q2 A Writing dosage instructions on medicine. · Q3 (new) B "I put the stuff with the thingy near the whatsit." (too many placeholders: nobody can follow it). Ex 5 (Script 8.2-B): see the answer key.

8.3 · C25

Review Project — A Structured Debate

90 min
Objectives — learners can…
  • follow a structured debate at natural speed and identify how each speaker presents, concedes and counters
  • build a case for an assigned side of a motion: 2–3 arguments, each with a reason and an example or consequence
  • deliver a timed opening, rebuttal or summary speech from notes, with signposting
  • concede and counter ("While I accept that…, the fact remains…") and answer what the other team actually said, not what they expected
  • ask and answer a floor question, and chair or time a debate with the right formal language
  • use the B2 toolkit in extended speech: a conditional, an impersonal passive, an advanced comparison, a cleft or inversion, and a natural hedge
Materials
  • Workbook 8.3 (the format, language bank, Exercises 1–5, debate prep sheet, rubric self-check, final B2 journal)
  • Slides 15–18 · The debate kit (classroom materials set G): motion cards, side cards, role cards, the chair's script (1 per debate), timekeeper cards (1 set per debate), floor-question slips and voting slips (1 each per learner) · Debate rubric (set H, 1 per learner) · Teacher observation checklist (set I, C25 → C26) on a clipboard · a big timer on the screen
  • Listening scripts 8.3-A (the model debate: car-free city centers) and 8.3-B (your model opening speech and a model rebuttal)
  • Learners' own prep sheets (started at home after C24)

Left — THE ORDER: 🪑 Chair opens · 🗳 secret vote 1 · 🟦 Proposer (2 min) · 🟥 Opposer (2 min) · ⏸ 1-min huddle · 🟦 Rebuttal (1½) · 🟥 Rebuttal (1½) · 🙋 Floor questions (4) · 🟥 Summary (1) · 🟦 Summary (1) · 🗳 vote 2 · 🪑 Chair closes

Center — CONCEDE → COUNTER: Admittedly… / That may be true, but… · While I accept that…, I still believe… · Even so, we might ask whether… · My opponent claimed that… However… · 🙋 My question is for the proposition: if…, how would…?

Right — B2 toolkit: If we introduced…, …would… · It is widely believed that… · by far the most… · What really matters is… · Not only does it…, but… · sort of a trade-off · on balance

Corner: Your side = your card, not your opinion. · Attack ideas, never people. · The vote = which team argued better. · C26 = final class 🎉

Stage plan
StageTimeInteractionProcedure
Warmer: vague to precise5S–SReview C24 and C23. Pairs: A describes last weekend in a relaxed, vague way for 45 seconds; B asks two "precise" follow-ups ("What time exactly?"). Swap. Then teams sit together (the seating from C24) and check that everyone has their prep sheet and role.
Pre-task 1: the model debate10S → S–SRead Script 8.3-A (the online lesson's debate, three voices; ask two confident learners to read Maya and Tom with you as the chair, or read all three). Listen 1, gist: "What's the motion? Who is for, who against? Who wins, in your view?" Listen 2: Workbook Ex 1 (five questions + tick the moves you hear). Pairs check. Slide 16: point out how Tom concedes before he counters.
Pre-task 2: debate language10T–S → S–SSlides 15–16. The format and the roles (chair, timekeeper, proposer, opposer, rebuttal, summary, floor). Concede-and-counter drill and the chair drill (below). Workbook Ex 4 (match the concession and the counter) and Ex 5 (blunt → balanced) in pairs. Ex 2 and Ex 3 (mixed B2 review) are homework unless your class is fast.
Team planning14Groups (teams)Read Script 8.3-B once: your model opening speech (about 2 minutes) and a model rebuttal (1 minute). "How did the rebuttal use what the opening actually said?" Then teams finish the prep sheet: agree 2–3 arguments, share them out between speakers, predict the other side's 3 strongest arguments and plan a concede-and-counter for each. Tick the B2 toolkit items each speaker will plant. Chairs and timekeepers read their cards with you (see "Setting up the debates"). You circulate and help each team sharpen one argument.
Rehearsal5S–S (in teams)Each speaker gives the first 30 seconds of their speech to their own team. The team gives one star and one wish. Floor members (in the other debate) write one question for each team on their slips. Chair and timekeeper set up the front of the room.
TASK: Debate 117Whole class (teams + chair + floor)Slide 17. Debate 1 runs from the chair's script: welcome and motion → secret vote 1 → proposer → opposer → huddle → two rebuttals → floor questions → opposition summary → proposition summary → secret vote 2 → the chair announces which team moved more votes and thanks both teams. You sit at the side with the observation checklist and a notepad (Fix / Upgrade / Wow). Don't step in unless the chair needs rescuing.
TASK: Debate 217Whole classRoles swap: the Debate 1 teams become the chair, timekeeper and floor; the Debate 2 teams debate their motion. Same order, same timing.
Feedback & language focus7T–SFirst the message: name one strong argument and one excellent concede-and-counter from each debate. Then board 2 errors to fix and 2 sentences to upgrade (no names), e.g. "I am agree with my opponent, but…" · "If we will ban phones, families will talk more." · upgrade "Phones are bad at dinner." → "What really damages family meals is the constant checking." Learners fill the rubric self-check.
Looking ahead & homework5T–SSlide 18: preview C26 (see "Looking Ahead"): the final assessment and the celebration. Homework: the online capstone lesson, the final B2 journal, and Ex 2–3 if not done. Learners tick the 8.3 "I can…" list.
Total905 + 10 + 10 + 14 + 5 + 17 + 17 + 7 + 5
If you run long

Protect planning and both debates. Cut in this order: (1) do Workbook Ex 4 and Ex 5 as homework (keep the drills); (2) shorten the model debate to one listening with the gist questions only; (3) shorten the floor questions to 3 minutes per debate. Never cut a debate (a team that planned and doesn't speak will be hurt), and never cut Looking Ahead: learners must leave knowing exactly what C26 looks like.

Why a team debate in class, but a solo balanced talk online?

The online lesson (8.3) lets self-study learners give a 2–3 minute balanced talk alone, presenting both sides and concluding. In class you have an audience and opponents, so the real B2 challenge becomes possible: listening to an argument at natural speed and answering it on the spot. That's why the rebuttal and the floor questions matter more than the prepared opening. If a learner's opening is short but their rebuttal answers a real point, that is a strong B2 performance. Note it on the checklist.

Meaning · Form · Pronunciation (review)

No new grammar. The job today is structure (the debate format), interaction (responding to the other team), and range: planting the B2 structures on purpose. Keep CCQs fast.

"The motion is that phones should be switched off during family meals." Who argues FOR it? (The proposition.) Against? (The opposition.) · Does the opposition have to prove the opposite, that phones are good at dinner? (No, only that the motion is a bad idea or goes too far.) · You're on the proposition, but at home you love your phone at dinner. Is that a problem? (No! You argue your card.)
"Admittedly, cleaner air is a real benefit. That said, a total ban would hurt shift workers." Does Tom agree with anything? (Yes, cleaner air is a benefit.) Does he agree with the motion? (No.) Why concede first? (He sounds fair and reasonable, so his counter is more convincing.) · "No, you're completely wrong." Good rebuttal? (No: no concession, no reason, and it attacks the person.)
Rebuttal: do you read a speech you wrote at home? (Mostly no: you answer what the other team just said. You can prepare predicted answers.) · Floor question: "What do you think about phones?" Good? (Too general. Better: "My question is for the proposition: if a parent is on call for work, would the rule still apply?")
🪑 ChairGood evening and welcome to tonight's debate. · The motion is that… · I call on the first speaker for the proposition. · Thank you. I now call on… · We'll now take questions from the floor. · Please keep your question short. · Time is up, thank you. · Let's move to the vote. · The result is… Please thank both teams.neutral, formal, never gives an opinion
⏱ Timekeeper(cards) 1 MINUTE LEFT · 15 SECONDS · TIME · (spoken, if needed) "Time, please."fair to both sides
🟦🟥 OpeningMadam / Mr. Chair, ladies and gentlemen… · We are here to argue that… · Our case rests on three points. · First of all… · What's more… · Finally… · For these reasons, we urge you to support / oppose the motion.2–3 arguments, each + reason + example
🔁 RebuttalMy opponent claimed that… · Admittedly… / That may be so, but… · While I accept that…, I still believe… · Even if that's the case,… · That argument ignores… · The other side would have you believe… However,…concede → counter → new point
🙋 FloorMy question is for the proposition / opposition. · You said that… Could you explain…? · What would happen if…? · How would that work for…?one short question, to a named team
🏁 SummaryLet's look at what we've heard tonight. · The other side argued…, but… · Weighing it all up… · On balance… · The evidence points to… · So we urge you to vote for / against the motion.no new arguments: weigh the debate

Pronunciation for debating. Contrastive stress makes a rebuttal clear: "They say it's CHEAPer. It's NOT cheaper, it's SLOWer." Pause after the signpost ("Our second point… [pause] is safety."). Slow down: speed hides good English, and the timekeeper is on your side, not against you. The concession has a fall-rise: "AdMITtedly ↘↗, it's cheaper…" (= but wait). And now, use C23 in reverse: the other team speaks naturally and fast. Anchor on their stressed words and write those in your notes.

Drill script (quick, choral → individual)
  1. Concede and counter chain: T gives a claim: "Remote work saves time." S1: "Admittedly, it saves time." S2: "That said, it can be isolating." S3: "So on balance, a hybrid model might work better." New claim, new chain.
  2. Blunt → balanced: T (blunt): "That's wrong." → S: "That may be true in some cases, but…" · T: "Bad idea." → S: "While I accept that it sounds attractive, I still believe it would cost too much."
  3. The chair's voice: T: "(start)" → S: "Good evening and welcome. The motion is that…" · T: "(first speaker)" → S: "I call on the first speaker for the proposition." · T: "(too long)" → S: "Thank you, time is up." · T: "(end)" → S: "Please thank both teams."
  4. Plant the toolkit: T gives a structure and a motion; S makes a debating sentence. T: "impersonal passive · four-day week" → S: "It is often argued that a shorter week makes people more productive." T: "inversion · phones at dinner" → S: "Not only do phones interrupt conversation, but they also…"
Setting up the debates (end of C24) — teams, sides, roles

Motions (set G, 12 cards): all everyday, community or workplace, e.g. Phones should be switched off during family meals · Our workplace should switch to a four-day week · Children under 12 should get no homework · Remote work should be the default for office jobs · Our main street should be car-free on weekends. You choose two motions (or let each debate draw one). Sides are drawn, never chosen: each team draws a Proposition or Opposition card. If a learner is uncomfortable with a motion, swap the motion, not the learner.

Class sizeHow to run it
12–16Four teams of 3–4. Debate 1: Team A (proposition) vs Team B (opposition); Teams C and D give the chair, the timekeeper and the floor. Debate 2: the reverse. Team roles: opening speaker · rebuttal speaker · summary speaker (· a 4th member shares the rebuttal or is the team's "note-taker," who hands the rebuttal speaker the other side's key words).
8–11Four teams of 2 (opening + rebuttal/summary) or two debates of 3 v 3 with fewer floor members. Everyone still gets one speaking role and one support role.
6–7One debate, 3 v 3 (or 3 v 2 + a timekeeper); you are the chair; run it twice with sides swapped (the same teams, a second motion) so everyone speaks twice.
17+Run the two debates at the same time in two corners with learner chairs, and float between them with the checklist. Or run three debates of 3 v 3 at 14 minutes each (cut the huddle and shorten the floor to 3 minutes).

Who does what: give the opening to learners who prepare well but find spontaneity hard (it is fully planned), the rebuttal to quick thinkers and to dominant speakers (they must listen first), the summary to careful learners who like a short, clear turn. The chair needs a calm, confident learner, or a dominant one (the chair must be neutral and bring others in). The timekeeper is a good role for an anxious learner who still wants to be at the front.

ICQs: Who chose your side? (Nobody, we drew it.) · Does your side show your real opinion? (No.) · Opening speech: how long? (Two minutes.) Rebuttal? (A minute and a half.) Summary? (One minute, no new arguments.) · Can you read a full script? (No, key words only.) · Floor: how many questions each? (One, to a named team.) · The vote asks what? (Which team argued better, not what you believe.) · When the timekeeper shows TIME? (Finish your sentence and stop.)
Running a debate — the chair's order and timing (about 16–17 minutes)
  1. Chair opens (1 min): welcome, the motion, the teams, the rules (time limits, one question each, respect). Secret vote 1: floor members write P or O on a slip: "Which side do you expect to argue better?" (No personal views.)
  2. Proposer (first proposition speaker, 2 min) → Opposer (first opposition speaker, 2 min).
  3. Huddle (1 min): teams whisper and pass notes; rebuttal speakers choose which points to answer.
  4. Proposition rebuttal (1½ min) → Opposition rebuttal (1½ min): concede → counter → one new point if time.
  5. Floor questions (4 min): the chair takes questions one by one, alternating teams. Any team member may answer, briefly. Each floor member asks one question at most.
  6. Summaries (1 min each): the opposition first, then the proposition (the proposition always opens and closes).
  7. Secret vote 2 + close (1 min): "Which team made the stronger case?" The timekeeper counts. The chair announces the result and which team gained more votes since vote 1, then: "Please thank both teams."
The rubric and the observation checklist → C26

The debate rubric (classroom materials set H, and the self-check in Workbook 8.3) has six criteria: 1 structure & signposting · 2 arguments & support · 3 rebuttal: concede & counter · 4 range of B2 language · 5 accuracy & register · 6 fluency, delivery & interaction. Three levels: ★★★ strong B2 (reaching toward C1) · ★★ B2 · ★ not yet. Floor members fill a peer star and wish for one speaker (assign who listens for whom). Learners self-assess at home. You can use the full rubric for your notes, but don't grade C25: it's practice. Tell learners so.

The observation checklist (set I) has one row per learner and 10 can-do items. Three marks: ✓ did it clearly · ~ did it with help, or errors that affect meaning · ○ not yet / not observed. You won't see every learner do everything: listen to each learner for their whole speech and at least one floor question or answer. After class, use it to:

  • Plan each learner's C26 assessment. The B2 final assessment is an individual speaking interview (about 8–10 minutes) plus short listening, reading and writing tasks — see the kit's B2 final assessment. Before C26, read it and match your checklist notes to its speaking criteria: which topics suit each learner, and where to start so that each learner begins with something they did well.
  • Plan the C26 warm-up: the two items with the most ~ / ○ across the class become a short review before the assessment starts.
  • Give each learner one sentence of feedback at the end of C25 or the start of C26: one real strength from the debate, named specifically ("Your concession to Omar's point was excellent").
  • Spot who needs reassurance: anyone who froze or stopped early gets a quiet word at the end of C25: "Next class is one-to-one and friendly. No team, no timer on the wall. You'll be fine."
Differentiation
Literacy support

Give the timekeeper or opening role. The prep sheet can be three boxes with symbols and 3–4 key words each; allow a 1-minute opening from a phrase card: We are here to argue that… · First… · What's more… · For these reasons…. For the floor, give a question already started on a slip: "My question is for the ____. What would happen if ____?" Rehearse the opening with you or a patient partner during team planning. Target: a 1-minute opening with two signposted points, and one floor question.

Stretch

Take the rebuttal or chair. Plant six toolkit items (a mixed or third conditional, an impersonal passive, by far, a cleft, a not only inversion, a hedge). Toward C1: use a point of information ("On a point of information: …") if you agree the rule with the chair, refute with evidence rather than opinion ("The figures suggest otherwise…"), and use C1 concession frames (Granted,… nevertheless… · It's hard to argue with…, yet… · Be that as it may,…). Write the team's case up as a 250-word balanced essay at home.

L1 watch-outs
  • "I am agree" / "I am not agree" (Spanish estoy de acuerdo, French je suis d'accord, Portuguese estou de acordo): still common at B2 under pressure. I agree / I don't agree. Board it after the debate, not during.
  • False friends in argument: actually ≠ "currently" (Spanish actualmente, French actuellement, German aktuell); eventually ≠ "possibly" (Spanish eventualmente, French éventuellement); sensible ≠ "sensitive." A speaker who says "Actually, 40% of people work from home" means "currently."
  • "On the other side" for on the other hand (Spanish por otro lado, Portuguese por outro lado), and "In my opinion, I think…" (double opinion markers, many L1s). Accept in fluency, fix in feedback.
  • Very direct disagreement ("This is not true," German, Dutch, Russian, Hebrew, Polish) or none at all (Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Thai, Vietnamese and Filipino learners may find public disagreement very uncomfortable). The assigned side helps both groups: it's a role, not a relationship. Teach both the English middle: concede, then counter.
  • Written-style speeches: learners from exam systems that reward memorized speeches may write and read the whole opening. Allow a full script at home for confidence; in class, the prep sheet has key words only. Collect scripts at the start of the debate if needed, kindly.
  • "Discuss about" (Spanish discutir sobre, Arabic, Hindi and others) and "explain me" (Romance languages): discuss something · explain it to me.
Homework

Online lesson 8.3: https://rays-esl.netlify.app/curriculum-b2/lesson_24.html. Listen to the model debate with 🔊 and repeat one speaker's part; do Exercises 1–2 and the Quick Quiz. Do the capstone as a solo balanced talk on the other motion from class (both sides + a conclusion, 2–3 minutes) and record it on your phone. Workbook 8.3: Ex 2 and Ex 3 (if not done in class), the rubric self-check and the final B2 journal. Bring the workbook to C26.

Check — self-check answers

Q1 A "While I accept that it's expensive, I still believe it's worth it." · Q2 A a reasoned conclusion that weighs both sides and takes a position. · Q3 (new) B answers the other team's arguments, conceding what is fair and countering it. Ex 1 (Script 8.3-A): 1 public health: car emissions damage our lungs, so car-free centers would have cleaner air (+ space for parks and bike lanes) · 2 older people, people with disabilities, shift workers, and small shops that need deliveries (the online lesson says "the elderly, the disabled"; teach the person-first forms) · 3 she concedes access matters, then says exemptions can be arranged for people who really need them · 4 whether public transportation is good enough yet · 5 the transition must be gradual and fair.

Closing the Module and the Course

End of C25: Module 8 and B2 wrap-up

Recap on slide 18: "I wz gonna callya" → I was going to call you · about thirty, give or take (to a friend) → exactly 31 (to the fire marshal) · Admittedly…, but… → on balance. Learners tick the three "I can…" lists in the workbook and look back at the "I can…" lists from Module 1. Anything still unticked goes on a sticky note for you, for the C26 warm-up.

The C26 final assessment covers the whole B2 course. From this module the most relevant skills are: understanding natural, connected speech; using vague language appropriately and switching to precision; and presenting, conceding and countering in an extended spoken turn. Check the kit's B2 final assessment for exactly how it tests them.

Looking Ahead — C26: Final Assessment & Celebration

What to tell learners at the end of C25 (slide 18)

C26 has two parts. First, the B2 final assessment: an individual speaking interview (about 8–10 minutes) plus short listening, reading and writing tasks — see the kit's B2 final assessment. Read it before C26 and tell learners how the class will be organized (the order of the tasks and when each learner will be interviewed), so there are no surprises. Then a celebration: certificates, a look back at what everyone can do now, and the next step, the C1 course, Advanced English.

Say it simply: "Next class is our last class. You'll talk with me one-to-one for about ten minutes, and do some short listening, reading and writing. It's everything we've practiced. Then we celebrate!"

Reduce anxiety: show B2 lesson 1.1 on screen and ask: "Week 1: could you have argued a side you didn't choose, for two minutes, in front of everyone, and answered questions?" Let them hear how far they've come. Remind them that the online lessons stay open for review, and that the recording of their balanced talk is the best practice for an extended turn.

Celebration planning: if learners want to bring food to share in C26, great. Ask everyone to label their dish with its main ingredients (allergies, halal, vegetarian). Nobody should feel they have to bring anything. Invite 2–3 volunteers to give a 1-minute "closing speech" using the summary language from the debate ("Weighing it all up, this course has been…").

Teacher reflection (after C25)

Look at your checklist and answer three questions: Which two can-dos need a C26 warm-up? Which learners need a quiet word of encouragement, and which strength will you name for each? Which learners are clearly ready for C1, and which would benefit from more B2 practice first? Write your answers on the back of the checklist.
